Stability in the category of smooth mod- p p representations of SL 2 ⁡ ( ℚ p ) \SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
Konstantin Ardakov, Peter Schneider

Abstract
. Let p ≥ 5 p\geq 5 be a prime number and let G = SL 2 ⁡ ( ℚ p ) G=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}) . Let Ξ = Spec ⁡ ( Z ) \Xi=\Spec(Z) denote the spectrum of the centre Z Z of the pro- p p Iwahori Hecke algebra of G G with coefficients in a field k k of characteristic p p . Let ℛ ⊂ Ξ × Ξ \mathcal{R}\subset\Xi\times\Xi denote the support of the pro- p p Iwahori Ext \Ext -algebra of G G , viewed as a ( Z , Z ) (Z,Z) -bimodule. We show that the locally ringed space Ξ / ℛ \Xi/\mathcal{R} is a projective algebraic curve over Spec ⁡ ( k ) \Spec(k) with two connected components, and that each connected component is a chain of projective lines. For each Zariski open subset U U of Ξ / ℛ \Xi/\mathcal{R} , we construct a stable localising subcategory ℒ U \mathcal{L}_{U} of the category of smooth k k -linear representations of G G .



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

Let
k
be a field, let
G
be a
p
-adic reductive group and let
\Mod_{k}(G)
denote the category of smooth
k
-linear representations of
G
. The centre
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
of the category
\Mod_{k}(G)
is called the
Bernstein centre
of
G
. When
k
is the field of complex numbers,
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
was studied in detail by Bernstein
[
Ber
]
and it plays a fundamental role in the classical local Langlands correspondence. Recently there has been interest in the case where
k
is a field of characteristic
p
motivated by considerations from the
p
-adic and mod-
p
Langlands programmes. However in this case,
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
turned out to be quite small:
in our previous work
[
AS23
]
, we showed that
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
only depends on the centre
Z(G)
of
G
, and in particular, that
\mathfrak{Z}(G)
is isomorphic to the finite dimensional
k
-algebra
k[Z(G)]
whenever
G
is assumed to be connected and semisimple.

This situation is reminiscent of the fact that the ring of global regular functions
\mathcal{O}(X)
on any projective variety
X
is also rather small. This observation becomes relevant to the Bernstein centre of
G
when we recall the work of Gabriel
[
Gab
]
, who proved that the structure sheaf of a noetherian scheme
X
can be reconstructed from the category
\QCoh(X)
of quasi-coherent sheaves on
X
, by associating with any open subscheme
U
of
X
the localizing subcategory
\mathcal{L}_{U}
of
\QCoh(X)
consisting of sheaves supported on the complement of
U
in
X
and by showing that the ring
\mathcal{O}(U)
can be recovered from
\QCoh(X)
as the centre of the quotient category
\QCoh(X)/\mathcal{L}_{U}
. In our recent work
[
AS24
]
, we generalised Gabriel’s construction to the case of an arbitrary Grothendieck category
\mathcal{C}
, as follows.

Recall that the localizing subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{C}
is said to be
stable
if it is stable under essential extensions. The set
\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})
of stable localizing subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
forms a partially ordered set under reverse inclusion. For
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{L}_{n}
in
\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})
we call
\{\mathcal{L}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
a
covering
of
\mathcal{L}
if
\mathcal{L}=\bigcap_{i}\mathcal{L}_{i}
. This notion makes
\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})
into a Grothendieck site, and we proved in
[
AS24
]
Thm. 1.1 that the presheaf
\mathcal{L}\mapsto Z(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{L})
on
\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})
is in fact a sheaf. When the category
\mathcal{C}
is additionally assumed to be locally noetherian, we showed that there is an order-reversing bijection
U\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{U}
between the so-called
stable subsets
of the injective spectrum
\bf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})
. We showed in
[
AS24
]
Thm. 1.2 that the corresponding presheaf
U\mapsto Z(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{L}_{U})
on
\bf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
satisfies the sheaf condition with respect to arbitrary coverings. These results suggest that even though
Z(\Mod_{k}(G))
may be small and uninteresting, this only reflects the fact that this centre is the ring of global sections of the sheaf formed by the centres of the quotient categories of
\Mod_{k}(G)
. Of course, this sheaf is only interesting if one can write down sufficiently many stable localizing subcategories of
\Mod_{k}(G)
.

1.2.
Main results

The main goal in this paper is to construct a large family of stable localizing subcategories of
\Mod_{k}(G)
in the case where
p\geq 5
and
G
is the group
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. In order to state our results, it will be convenient to introduce an axiomatic framework as follows. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a full abelian subcategory of a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{C}
. We say that
\mathcal{C}
is a
thickening
of
\mathcal{A}
if, roughly speaking
1
1

1

see Definition
3.1.1
for the precise definition
, every noetherian object in
\mathcal{C}
has a finite filtration all of whose subquotients lie in
\mathcal{A}
, and the inclusion functor
i:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{C}
has a left exact right adjoint
r:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{A}
which restricts to the identity functor on
\mathcal{A}
and which respects non-zero objects in
\mathcal{C}
. We prove that
r
induces a bijection
r:\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
between the corresponding injective spectra and that the map
\mathcal{L}\mapsto\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{A}
defines a bijection between the set of localizing subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
and the set of localizing subcategories of
\mathcal{A}
. The bijection
r:\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
respects stable subsets and therefore induces an injective map
\mathbf{r}:\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{L}^{st}(\mathcal{A})
; however
\mathbf{r}
is in general not surjective.

Example 1.2.1
.

Let
G=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and let
I
be the pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup of
G
. Let
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
denote the full subcategory of
\Mod_{k}(G)
whose objects are generated by their
I
-fixed vectors and let
r(V)=k[G]\cdot V^{I}
for all
V
in
\Mod_{k}(G)
. We show in
\lx@sectionsign
2.1
below that
\Mod_{k}(G)
is a thickening of
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
, and moreover that the compactly induced representation
\ind_{I}^{G}(k)
is in fact a noetherian projective generator of
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
.

Returning to our axiomatic framework, we assume in addition that
\mathcal{A}
has a noetherian projective generator
P
. The functor
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,-):\mathcal{A}\to\Mod(H)
is then an equivalence of categories with quasi-inverse
P\otimes_{H}-:\Mod(H)\to\mathcal{A}
, where
H:=\End_{\mathcal{A}}(P)^{\op}
, and in Thm.
3.5.8
we show that the image of
\mathbf{r}
consists precisely of those localizing subcategories of
\mathcal{A}
that are preserved under the functor
P\otimes_{H}\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-):\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
. To understand this condition better we introduce the graded
(H,H)
-bimodule

(1)

\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(P,P):=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(P,P)

which is in particular a module for
Z\otimes Z:=Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Z
where
Z:=Z(H)
is the centre of
H
. We let
\mathcal{J}:=\Ann_{Z\otimes Z}(\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(P,P))
be its annihilator ideal and
\mathcal{R}:=V(\mathcal{J})\subseteq\Spec(Z\otimes Z)
be the corresponding Zariski closed subset. Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\Xi:=\Spec(Z)
denote the restrictions of the two projection maps
\Spec(Z\otimes Z)\rightrightarrows\Xi
. We then have a coequaliser diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{q}}
\textstyle{\Xi/\mathfrak{R}}

in the category of locally ringed spaces (cf.
[
DG
]
Prop. I.1.1.6). To state our main theorem, we introduce the map
\tau:\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\Xi/\mathfrak{R}
by the commutativity of the following diagram:

\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{r}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\Mod(H))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Spec(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\Spec(Z)=\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{q}}
\textstyle{\Xi/\mathfrak{R}.}

The first vertical arrow on the left comes from the equivalence
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,-):\mathcal{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Mod(H)
and the map
\varphi
is given by
\varphi(P)=P\cap Z
for all
P\in\Spec(H)
. The second vertical arrow comes from
[
Gab
]
V
\lx@sectionsign
4; it is a bijection if
H
is finitely generated as a module over its centre. Our first main result is then the following

Theorem 1.2.2

(Prop.
3.7.4
)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a thickening of
\mathcal{A}
. Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has a noetherian projective generator
P
and let
H=\End_{\mathcal{A}}(P)^{\op}
. Assume furthermore that:

(A1)

H
is finitely generated as a module over its centre
Z
,

(A2)

there is an integer
d
such that
\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,-)|_{\mathcal{A}}=0
for any
j>d
, and

(A3)

Z\otimes Z/\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
is noetherian.

Then
\tau:\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\to\Xi/\mathcal{R}
is continuous with respect to the stable topologies.

In this generality,
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
is just a locally ringed space. Section
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:QuotSpace}
of our paper is devoted to the computation of this space in the situation of Example
1.2.1
above, relying crucially on the calculations of
[
OS22
]
on the structure of the pro-
p
Iwahori
\Ext
-algebra (
1
). Our second main result is then the following

Theorem 1.2.3

(Thm.
4.5.23
, Cor.
4.5.24
)
.

Suppose that
p\geq 5
,
G=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
,
\mathcal{C}=\Mod_{k}(G)
and
\mathcal{A}=\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
.

a)

\Xi/\mathcal{R}
is a scheme.

b)

\Xi/\mathcal{R}
has two connected components.

c)

Each connected component of
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
is a chain of projective lines.

Example 1.2.4
.

When
p=13
, the scheme
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
looks as follows:

In Example
4.5.25
below, we sketch how to obtain this gluing from the scheme
\Xi
.

Corollary 1.2.5
.

Let
p\geq 5
and let
G=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. For every Zariski open subset
U
of
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
,
\tau^{-1}(U)
is a stable open subset of
\bf{Sp}
(\Mod_{k}(G))
.

Proof.

This follows from Thm.
1.2.2
, once we verify its conditions. Note that
\mathcal{C}=\Mod_{k}(G)
is a thickening of
\mathcal{A}=\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
, and that
P=\ind_{I}^{G}(k)
is a noetherian projective generator of
\mathcal{A}
by Example
1.2.1
.

(A1)
. The pro-
p
Iwahori-Hecke algebra
H=\End_{\mathcal{C}}(P)^{\op}
is finitely generated as a module over its centre by
[
Vig
]
; see also
[
OS18
]
Cor. 3.4 and Remark 3.5.

(A2)
. Since
p\geq 5
, the group
I
has no elements of order
p
, and therefore has finite
p
-cohomological dimension equal to
3
, the dimension of
I
as a
p
-adic Lie group. Using Frobenius reciprocity, we see that for all
V
in
\mathcal{C}
,

\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,V)=\Ext^{j}_{G}(\ind_{I}^{G},V)=\Ext^{j}_{I}(k,V)=H^{j}(I,V)=0\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j>3.

(A3)
. In view of Remark
3.7.5
below, we may replace
(A3)
by the weaker assumption that
Z\otimes_{k}Z
is noetherian, because
\mathcal{C}
happens to be a
k
-linear category. Prop.
4.1.5
and equation (
11
) imply that
Z
is finitely generated as a
k
-algebra. Hence
Z\otimes_{k}Z
is noetherian, by Hilbert’s Basis Theorem. ∎

The paper
[
DEG
]
considers, for the group
G=GL(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, the full subcategory
\Mod_{k,\zeta}(G)
of those representations in
\Mod_{k}(G)
which have a fixed central character
\zeta
. They associate with this subcategory a scheme
X
which is also a chain of projective lines. This scheme is definitively the analogue of a connected component of our quotient space
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
in this situation. But the idea of
[
DEG
]
behind
X
is completely different:
X
is viewed as a kind of moduli space of 2-dimensional semisimple Galois representations modulo
p
. From this point of view, the relation between
X
and the representation theory of
G
comes from Breuil’s semisimple Langlands correspondence modulo
p
. Based on this the paper
[
DEG
]
also develops a localisation theory for the category
\Mod_{k,\zeta}(G)
and shows that the closed points of
X
parameterize the blocks of the Krull-dimension
0
subcategory of
\Mod_{k,\zeta}(G)
. We emphasize that in the present paper we work entirely on the representation theoretic side of the full category
\Mod_{k}(G)
and our quotient space
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
arises from the non-vanishing (and the structure) of universal
\Ext
-groups. We therefore like to think that the two papers complement each other in an interesting way.

2.
Basic facts about the category
\Mod_{k}(G)

We fix a field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. For any locally compact and totally disconnected group
\mathcal{G}
we denote by
\Mod_{k}(\mathcal{G})
the abelian category of smooth
\mathcal{G}
-representations on
k
-vector spaces. Let
G=SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We fix the pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup
I\subseteq SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
of all matrices which are upper triangular unipotent mod
p
.

2.1.
Locally noetherian

Let
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
denote the full subcategory of all
V
in
\Mod_{k}(G)
which are generated by their pro-
p
Iwahori fixed vectors
V^{I}
. Write
H:=\End_{k[G]}(k[G/I])^{\op}
.

Theorem 2.1.1
.

The functor

\displaystyle\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\Mod(H)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\longmapsto V^{I}

is an equivalence of categories with quasi-inverse
M\longmapsto k[G/I]\otimes_{H}M
. Moreover,
k[G/I]
is projective and faithfully flat as an
H
-module.

Proof.

[
Koz
]
and
[
OS18
]
Prop. 3.25 and its proof.
∎

Lemma 2.1.2
.

\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
is an abelian subcategory of
\Mod_{k}(G)
closed under the formation of subobjects, quotient objects, and arbitrary colimits.

Proof.

Clearly
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
is closed under the formation of arbitrary direct sums. Let
0\rightarrow V_{0}\rightarrow V_{1}\rightarrow V_{2}\rightarrow 0
be an exact sequence in
\Mod_{k}(G)
such that
V_{1}
lies in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
. Obviously, then also
V_{2}
lies in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
. For
V_{0}
we consider the commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[G/I]\otimes_{H}V_{0}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[G/I]\otimes_{H}V_{1}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{k[G/I]\otimes_{H}V_{2}^{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

The upper horizontal row is exact by the left exactness of the functor
(-)^{I}
and the fact that
k[G/I]
is flat as a (right)
H
-module. By the category equivalence in Thm.
2.1.1
the middle and right perpendicular arrows are isomorphisms. Hence the left one is an isomorphism as well. This shows that
V_{0}
lies in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
.
∎

The
k
-algebra
H
is affine and finitely generated as a module over its centre (
[
Vig
]
or, in an explicit form,
[
OS18
]
Cor. 3.4 and Remark 3.5) and hence noetherian. Therefore the abelian category
\Mod(H)
is locally noetherian Grothendieck.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be any locally compact and totally disconnected group with an open pro-
p
subgroup
J
.
Any finitely generated
\mathcal{G}
-representation
V
in
\Mod_{k}(\mathcal{G})
has a finite filtration
\{0\}\subset V_{1}\subset\ldots\subset V_{\ell}=V
by subrepresentations such that each subquotient
V_{i}/V_{i-1}
, for
0<i\leq\ell
, is generated by finitely many
J
-fixed vectors.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case where
V
is generated by a single vector
v
. We can then find an open normal subgroup
N\subseteq J
such that
N
fixes
v
. Then
V
is a quotient of the
\mathcal{G}
-representation
k[\mathcal{G}/N]
. Hence it actually suffices to consider the case
V=k[\mathcal{G}/N]
. In
V
we then have the
J
-subrepresentation
k[J/N]
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
denote the augmentation ideal of the ring
k[J/N]
. Since
J/N
is a finite
p
-group we find an integer
\ell\geq 0
such that
\mathfrak{m}^{\ell}=0
. Observe that
J
acts trivially on each subquotient
\mathfrak{m}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}^{i+1}
. We now define
V_{i}\subseteq V
as the
\mathcal{G}
-subrepresentation generated by
\mathfrak{m}^{\ell-i}
. The subquotient
V_{i}/V_{i-1}
then is generated by the image of
\mathfrak{m}^{\ell-i}/\mathfrak{m}^{(\ell-i)+1}
, which is contained in
(V_{i}/V_{i-1})^{J}
.
∎

Proposition 2.1.4
.

The abelian category
\Mod_{k}(G)
is locally noetherian.

Proof.

By Lemma 1(iv)
[
Sch
]
,
\Mod_{k}(G)
is a Grothendieck category. We have to show that any finitely generated
G
-representation
V
in
\Mod_{k}(G)
is noetherian. Lemma
2.1.3
reduces us to the case that
V
is generated by finitely many
I
-fixed vectors. According to Lemma
2.1.2
any increasing chain of
G
-subrepresentations of
V
already lies in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
. By Thm.
2.1.1
it therefore corresponds to a chain in the finitely generated
H
-module
V^{I}
. Hence it must become stationary.
∎

The exact inclusion functor
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)\subseteq\Mod_{k}(G)
is left adjoint to the left exact functor

\displaystyle\Mod_{k}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\longmapsto V(I):=G\text{-subrepresentation of $V$ generated by $V^{I}$}(\cong k[G/I]\otimes_{H}V^{I})\ .

Lemma 2.1.5
.

The functor
V\mapsto V(I)

a)

restricts to the identity functor on
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
,

b)

respects non-zero objects,

c)

respects injective objects, and

d)

commutes with arbitrary filtered colimits.

Proof.

a) is obvious. b) holds since
I
is pro-
p
. c) is a consequence of the functor being right adjoint to a (left) exact functor. For d) let
V=\varinjlim_{j}V_{j}
be a filtered colimit in
\Mod_{k}(G)
. Since filtered colimits are exact in the Grothendieck category
\Mod_{k}(G)
we have the inclusion
\varinjlim_{j}V_{j}(I)\subseteq V(I)
. But
V^{I}=\varinjlim_{j}V_{j}^{I}
. Hence this inclusion is an equality.
∎

2.2.
Krull dimension

We briefly recall Gabriel’s notion of a Krull dimension for arbitrary Grothendieck categories
\mathcal{C}
. Gabriel’s dimension filtration of
\mathcal{C}
is a filtration by localising subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
of
\mathcal{C}
indexed by ordinals
\alpha
. His convention is that
\mathcal{C}_{-1}
is the subcategory of all zero objects of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is the smallest localising subcategory containing all objects of finite length. The
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
then are defined successively as follows. If
\alpha=\beta+1
then
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
is the preimage of
(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{\beta})_{0}
under the quotient functor
q_{\mathcal{C}_{\beta}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
; if
\alpha
is a limit ordinal then
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
is the smallest localising subcategory containing all
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
for
\beta<\alpha
. This process terminates as soon as
\mathcal{C}_{/}\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
has no simple objects. But by
[
Gab
]
Prop. 7 on p. 387 the latter implies that
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
must be equal to
\mathcal{C}
. The Krull dimension
\kappa(\mathcal{C})
of
\mathcal{C}
can now be defined as the smallest
\alpha
such that
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
. Similarly the Krull dimension
\kappa(Y)
of an object
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is defined to be the smallest
\alpha
such that
Y
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
. Correspondingly the set

\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})=\dot{\bigcup}_{\alpha<\kappa(\mathcal{C})}\mathbf{Sp}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{C})

is stratified by the subsets
\mathbf{Sp}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{C})
of all
[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
such that
q_{\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}}(E)
contains a simple object (
[
Gab
]
p. 383). In general it is not possible to read off the Krull dimension
\kappa(E)
of an
[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{C})
from the
\alpha
.

Remark 2.2.1
.

Let
\alpha<\kappa(\mathcal{C})
be an ordinal such that
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha+1}
is a stable localising subcategory; then
\kappa(E)=\alpha+1
for any
[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}_{\alpha}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By our assumption
E
is
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
-closed and contains a nonzero subobject
S
lying in
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha+1}
(cf.
[
Gab
]
p. 383). Since
E
is an essential extension of any of its nonzero subobjects (cf.
[
Gab
]
Prop. 11 on p. 361) it follows from the stability of
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha+1}
that
E
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha+1}
.
∎

Remark 2.2.2
.

(
[
Gab
]
Prop. 7 on p. 387) Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is locally noetherian. Then
\mathcal{C}
has a Krull dimension and, for any non-limit ordinal
\alpha
, the category
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}/\mathcal{C}_{\alpha-1}
is locally finite.

For the convenience of the reader we point out the following elementary consequences of the fact that the subcategories
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
are localising:

–

If
0\rightarrow U_{1}\rightarrow U_{2}\rightarrow U_{3}\rightarrow 0
is a short exact sequence in
\mathcal{C}
then

\kappa(U_{2})=\sup(\kappa(U_{1}),\kappa(U_{3}))\ .

–

If
U
in
\mathcal{C}
is the inductive limit of a family of subobjects
U_{i}
then
\kappa(U)=\sup_{i}\kappa(U_{i})
. In particular, for any
U
, we have
\kappa(U)=\sup_{i}\kappa(U_{i})
where the
U_{i}
run over all noetherian subobjects of
U
.

In
[
MCR
]
Chap. 6 the notion of Krull dimension for the category
\mathcal{C}=\Mod(R)
of modules over a noetherian ring
R
is introduced using posets. By loc. cit. Lemmas 6.2.4 and 6.2.17 it also has the above two properties. Therefore it follows, e.g., from
[
GR
]
Prop. 2.3 (beware that this reference shifts the Gabriel definition by 1) that these two notions of Krull dimension coincide for noetherian rings
R
.

Proposition 2.2.3
.

The categories
\Mod_{k}(G)
and
\Mod(H)
have Krull dimension one.

Proof.

The computation of
Z(H)
in
[
OS18
]
§3.2.4 (together with
[
MCR
]
Cor. 6.4.8) shows that
Z(H)
has Krull dimension one. By
[
OS18
]
Cor. 3.4 the centre
Z(H)
contains a polynomial ring
k[\zeta]
over which
H
is a finitely generated free module. Using
[
MCR
]
Cor. 6.5.3 it then follows that
H
has Krull dimension one as well. Hence
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)\simeq\Mod(H)
has Krull dimension one. By the proof of Lemma
2.1.2
the subcategory
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
is closed under the passage to subobjects in
\Mod_{k}(G)
. Therefore the Krull dimensions of an object in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
, when viewed in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
or in
\Mod_{k}(G)
, coincide.

Since the subcategory
\Mod_{k}(G)_{1}
is closed under extensions it follows from Lemma
2.1.3
that every finitely generated representation lies in
\Mod_{k}(G)_{1}
. The assertion about
\Mod_{k}(G)
then is immediate from the fact that
\Mod_{k}(G)_{1}
also is closed under inductive limits.
∎

3.
Thickenings

It will be useful to axiomatize the situation in the previous section.

3.1.
Definitions

For this we fix a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{C}
together with a full abelian subcategory
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

The category
\mathcal{C}
is called a
thickening
of
\mathcal{A}
if:

a)

\mathcal{A}
is closed under the formation of subobjects, quotient objects, and arbitrary colimits in
\mathcal{C}
;

b)

the inclusion functor
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
has a left exact right adjoint functor
r=r_{\mathcal{C},\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
such that:

-

the functor
r:\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{r}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\subseteq}\mathcal{C}
is a subfunctor of the identity functor
\id_{\mathcal{C}}
,

-

r|\mathcal{A}=\id_{\mathcal{A}}
,

-

r
preserves non-zero objects, and

-

r
commutes with arbitrary filtered colimits;

c)

every noetherian object
V
in
\mathcal{C}
has a finite filtration
0=V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq V_{m}=V
such that all subquotients
V_{j}/V_{j-1}
, for
1\leq j\leq m
, lie in
\mathcal{A}
.

In the following we fix an
\mathcal{A}
as in the above definition. Note that
\mathcal{A}
is a strictly full subcategory. For any
V
in
\mathcal{C}
the subobject
r(V)
is the largest subobject of
V
which lies in
\mathcal{A}
. The functor
r
in fact gives rise to a whole sequence of subfunctors
r_{j}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, for
j\geq 0
, of the identity functor
\id_{\mathcal{C}}
which are defined inductively as follows. We define

r_{0}:\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{r}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{\subseteq}\mathcal{C}\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{j}(V)/r_{j-1}(V)=r_{0}(V/r_{j-1}(V))\quad\text{for $j\geq 1$}.

Obviously we have, for any
V
in
\mathcal{C}
, the increasing filtration by subobjects

r(V)\subseteq r_{1}(V)\subseteq\ldots\subseteq r_{j}(V)\subseteq\ldots\subseteq V\ .

Lemma 3.1.2
.

For any
j\geq 0
the functor
r_{j}
has the following properties:

a)

r_{j}
is left exact;

b)

r_{j}\circ r_{i}=r_{i}\circ r_{j}=r_{j}
for any
i\geq j
;

c)

r_{j}
preserves filtered unions of subobjects.

Proof.

In
[
Ste
]
VI§1 the functor
r_{0}
is called a
left exact idempotent preradical
of
\mathcal{C}
and the asserted properties of the
r_{j}
can be found in the Exercises 1 and 2 of that Chap. VI.
∎

Correspondingly we introduce, for any
j\geq 0
, the strictly full subcategory

\mathcal{A}_{j}:=\ \text{all objects $V$ such that $r_{j}(V)=V$}

of
\mathcal{C}
. We have
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}
, and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
is a subcategory of
\mathcal{A}_{j+1}
. Each functor
r_{j}
may be viewed as a functor
r_{j}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{j}
.

Lemma 3.1.3
.

For any
j\geq 0
we have:

a)

\mathcal{A}_{j}
is an abelian subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
closed under the formation of subobjects, quotient objects, and arbitrary colimits in
\mathcal{C}
;

b)

r_{j}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{j}
is right adjoint to the inclusion functor
\mathcal{A}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
;

c)

r_{j}|\mathcal{A}_{j}=\id_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}
;

d)

r_{j}
preserves non-zero objects;

e)

r_{j}
commutes with arbitrary filtered colimits;

f)

any object
V
in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
has the finite filtration
0\subseteq r_{0}(V)\subseteq r_{1}(V)\subseteq\ldots\subseteq r_{j}(V)=V
whose subquotients
r_{i}(V)/r_{i-1}(V)
all lie in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

a) follows from
[
Ste
]
Propositions VI.1.2 and VI.1.7. For b) we observe that for any homomorphism
f:V_{0}\rightarrow V_{1}
in
\mathcal{C}
we have
r_{j}(f)=f|r_{j}(V_{0})
. c), d), and f) hold by construction. e) We proceed by induction with respect to
j
. For
j=0
the claim is part of the Def.
3.1.1
. Now assume that the claim holds for some
j
. Let
V=\varinjlim_{i}V_{i}
be a filtered colimit in
\mathcal{C}
. We then have

\displaystyle r_{j+1}(V)/r_{j}(V)

\displaystyle=r_{0}(V/r_{j}(V))=r_{0}(\varinjlim_{i}V_{i}/r_{j}(\varinjlim_{i}V_{i}))=r_{0}(\varinjlim_{i}V_{i}/\varinjlim_{i}r_{j}(V_{i}))

\displaystyle=r_{0}(\varinjlim_{i}(V_{i}/r_{j}(V_{i})))=\varinjlim_{i}r_{0}(V_{i}/r_{j}(V_{i}))=\varinjlim_{i}(r_{j+1}(V_{i})/r_{j}(V_{i}))

\displaystyle=\varinjlim_{i}r_{j+1}(V_{i})/\varinjlim_{i}r_{j}(V_{i})=(\varinjlim_{i}r_{j+1}(V_{i}))/r_{j}(V)\ ,

where we use for equations
4
and
7
that filtered colimits in
\mathcal{C}
are exact. It follows that
r_{j+1}(V)=\varinjlim_{i}r_{j+1}(V_{i})
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.4
.

Let
0\rightarrow X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z\rightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence in
\mathcal{C}
such that
X
lies in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
for some
j\geq 0
and
Z
lies in
\mathcal{A}
; then
Y
lies in
\mathcal{A}_{j+1}
.

Proof.

Since
r_{j}
is left exact by Lemma
3.1.2
.a, we have the commutative exact diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X=r_{j}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{r_{j}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y/r_{j}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

which exhibits
Y/r_{j}(Y)
as a quotient object of
Z
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is closed under quotient objects it follows that
r_{0}(Y/r_{j}(Y))=Y/r_{j}(Y)
. On the other hand, by construction we have
r_{j+1}(Y)/r_{j}(Y)=r_{0}(Y/r_{j}(Y))
. It follows that
r_{j+1}(Y)=Y
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.5
.

For any
V
in
\mathcal{C}
we have
V=\bigcup_{j\geq 0}r_{j}(V)
.

Proof.

Write
V
as a filtered union of noetherian subobjects. Using Lemma
3.1.2
.c we see that it suffices to prove the assertion for a noetherian
V
. But in this case it follows from Def.
3.1.1
.c and an iterated application of Lemma
3.1.4
that
V=r_{j}(V)
for sufficiently large
j
.
∎

Proposition 3.1.6
.

\mathcal{A}_{j}
, for any
j\geq 0
, is a locally noetherian Grothendieck category.

Proof.

By
[
BP
]
Lemma 3.4 the category
\mathcal{A}_{j}
is Grothendieck. That it is locally noetherian is clear.
∎

Remark 3.1.7
.

Let
V
be an object in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
for some
j\geq 0
; then the Krull dimensions of
V
viewed in
\mathcal{A}_{j}
and viewed in
\mathcal{C}
coincide.

Proof.

This is immediate from
\mathcal{A}_{j}
being closed under the passage to subobjects in
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

3.2.
A spectral sequence

The Ext-groups in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{A}
are related by the following spectral sequence.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

For objects
A
in
\mathcal{A}
and
V
in
\mathcal{C}
we have the Grothendieck spectral sequence

\Ext_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(A,R^{j}r(V))\Longrightarrow\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{i+j}(A,V)\ .

Proof.

We have the adjunction
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(A,r(V))=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A,V)
. Since
r
, being right adjoint to a (left) exact functor, preserves injective objects, this equation extends to the asserted composed functor spectral sequence.
∎

Corollary 3.2.2
.

Suppose that
P
is a projective object in
\mathcal{A}
. For any object
V
in
\mathcal{C}
and any
j\geq 0
we then have

\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,R^{j}r(V))\ .

3.3.
Injective spectra

We first recall some standard notations. For any object
Y
in a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{D}
a choice of injective hull of
Y
is denoted by
E_{\mathcal{D}}(Y)
or simply
E(Y)
. The injective spectrum
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
is the collection of isomorphism classes
[Y]
of indecomposable injective objects
Y
of
\mathcal{D}
; it is a set, for example, by
[
Her
]
p. 523.

In the following we keep the setting of a thickening
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

For any
V
in
\mathcal{C}
we have:

a)

The inclusion
r(V)\subseteq V
is an essential extension. In particular, if
V
is injective then
V
is an injective hull of
r(V)
.

b)

If
V
is injective in
\mathcal{A}
then
V\cong r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))
.

c)

If
V
is injective in
\mathcal{C}
then
V
is indecomposable if and only if
r(V)
is indecomposable.

Proof.

i. Let
U\subseteq V
be a non-zero subobject. Since
r
preserves non-zero objects we then have
0\neq r(U)\subseteq r(V)
and hence
U\cap r(V)\neq 0
.

ii. We obviously have
V=r(V)\subseteq r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))\subseteq E_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
. Since
r
, as a right adjoint of a (left) exact functor, preserves injective objects both terms in the essential extension
V=r(V)\subseteq r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))
are injective in
\mathcal{A}
. Hence we must have equality.

iii. Suppose that
r(V)=U_{1}\oplus U_{2}
with
U_{i}\neq 0
(and necessarily lying in
\mathcal{A}
). Using
[
Ste
]
Prop. V.2.6 we see that
V
, being an injective hull of
r(V)
by i., is isomorphic to the direct sum of injective hulls of
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
and hence is decomposable. On the other hand if
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
with
V_{i}\neq 0
then
r(V)=r(V_{1})\oplus r(V_{2})
with
r(V_{i})\neq 0
.
∎

The above lemma implies that the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle[E]

\displaystyle\longmapsto r([E]):=[r(E)]

is a bijection with inverse
[U]\longmapsto[E_{\mathcal{C}}(U)]
. In fact, because of Lemma
3.1.3
all of the above remains valid for each functor
r_{j}
. Hence we have the bijections

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle[E]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[r_{j}(E)]\longmapsto[r(E)]\ .

3.4.
Localizing subcategories

We recall that a full subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{D}
is called
localising
if it is closed under the
formation of subobjects, quotient objects, extensions, and arbitrary direct sums. In particular, it is strictly full and contains the zero object (as the empty direct
sum). It will be technically useful to also recall the following fact. Let
\mathcal{D}^{noeth}
denote the full subcategory of all noetherian objects in
\mathcal{D}
.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

The map

collection of all localising

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\text{collection of all Serre}

subcategories of
\mathcal{D}

subcategories of
\mathcal{D}^{noeth}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{D}^{noeth}

is a bijection; its inverse sends a Serre subcategory
\mathcal{S}
to the smallest localising subcategory
\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle
of
\mathcal{D}
which contains
\mathcal{S}
; equivalently
\langle\mathcal{S}\rangle
is the full subcategory of all filtered colimits of objects in
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

[
Her
]
Thm. 2.8.
∎

Lemma 3.4.2
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is a localising subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
then
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is localising in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof.

Each of the categories
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{L}
is closed under subobjects, quotient objects, and arbitrary direct sums. Therefore the same holds for
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
. It remains to consider extensions. Let
0\rightarrow X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z\rightarrow 0
be a short exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
such that
X
and
Z
lie in
\mathcal{L}
. Then
Z
must lie in
\mathcal{L}
and hence in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
.
∎

Proposition 3.4.3
.

The map

collection of all localising

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\text{collection of all localising}

subcategories of
\mathcal{C}

subcategories of
\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}

is a bijection; its inverse sends a localising subcategory
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathcal{A}
to the smallest localising subcategory
\langle\mathcal{K}\rangle
of
\mathcal{C}
which contains
\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

We obviously have
\mathcal{A}^{noeth}=\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{C}^{noeth}
. Hence Prop.
3.4.1
reduces the asserted bijectivity to the bijectivity of the map

collection of all Serre

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\text{collection of all Serre}

subcategories of
\mathcal{C}^{noeth}

subcategories of
\mathcal{A}^{noeth}

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}\ .

But it follows from Def.
3.1.1
.c that any such
\mathcal{S}
is the smallest Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{noeth}
which contains
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}
. On the other hand let
\mathcal{T}
be a Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{A}^{noeth}
. We define
\mathcal{S}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{noeth}
whose objects
V
have a finite filtration
0=V_{0}\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq V_{m}=V
with
V_{j}/V_{j-1}
in
\mathcal{T}
for any
1\leq j\leq m
. It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{S}
is a Serre subcategory of
\mathcal{C}^{noeth}
and that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{T}
.
∎

The above Prop.
3.4.3
, of course, holds true correspondingly with
\mathcal{A}
replaced with
\mathcal{A}_{j}
.

For any localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{D}
one defines the subset

A(\mathcal{L}):=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D}):\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(V,E)=0\ \text{for any $V\in\ob(\mathcal{L})$}\}

of
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
. These subsets
A(\mathcal{L})
form the closed subsets of a topology on
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
which is called the
Ziegler topology
(cf.
[
Her
]
Thm. 3.4). In fact, by Prop.
3.4.1
and
[
Her
]
Thm. 3.8, the map

(3)

collection of all localising

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\text{set of all Ziegler-closed}

subcategories of
\mathcal{D}

subsets of
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\longmapsto A(\mathcal{L})

is an inclusion reversing bijection. This means that the Ziegler closed subsets of
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
classify the Serre subcategories of
\mathcal{D}^{noeth}
as well as the localising subcategories of
\mathcal{D}
. It also implies that
\mathcal{L}
can be reconstructed from
A(\mathcal{L})
by

\ob(\mathcal{L})=\{V\in\ob(\mathcal{D}):\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(V,E)=0\ \text{for all $[E]\in A(\mathcal{L})$}\}.

Corollary 3.4.4
.

The maps
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
in (
2
) are homeomorphisms for the Ziegler topologies.

Proof.

Using
[
Her
]
Prop. 3.2 and Cor. 3.5 we see that Def.
3.1.1
.c implies that the sets

\displaystyle O_{\mathcal{C}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C}):\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(U,E)\neq 0\},

\displaystyle\text{resp.}\ O_{\mathcal{A}}(U)

\displaystyle:=\{[r(E)]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}):\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(U,r(E))\neq 0\},

for
U\in\ob(\mathcal{A})
, form a base for the Ziegler-open subsets of
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
, resp.
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
. But, for such
U
, we have
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(U,E)=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(U,r(E))
since
r
is right adjoint to the inclusion functor.
∎

Later on another topology on
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
will be more important for our purposes.

Proposition 3.4.5
.

For a localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{D}
, the following are equivalent:

a)

there is a noetherian object
C
in
\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathcal{L}
is the smallest localising subcategory containing
C
;

b)

there is a noetherian object
C
in
\mathcal{D}
such that

A(\mathcal{L})=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D}):\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(C,E)=0\};

c)

the Ziegler open subset
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})\setminus A(\mathcal{L})
is quasi-compact.

Proof.

For the equivalence of a. and b. we consider more generally an arbitrary object of
\mathcal{D}
, and we let
\langle C\rangle
denote the smallest localising subcategory containing
C
. Let
E
be any injective object in
\mathcal{D}
. If
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(-,E)
vanishes on
\langle C\rangle
then obviously
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(C,E)=0
. But the injectivity of
E
easily implies that the converse holds as well. It follows that

A(\langle C\rangle)=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D}):\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(C,E)=0\}.

For the equivalence of b. and c. see
[
Her
]
Cor. 3.9.
∎

The topology on
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
which has as a base of open subsets the complements of quasi-compact Ziegler-open subsets is called the
Gabriel-Zariski topology
.

Corollary 3.4.6
.

The maps
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
in (
2
) are homeomorphisms for the Gabriel-Zariski topologies.

3.5.
Stability

We recall that a localising subcategory of a locally noetherian Grothendieck category
\mathcal{D}
is called
stable
if it is closed under the passage to essential extensions.

Lemma 3.5.1
.

For any localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{D}
the following are equivalent:

a)

\mathcal{L}
is stable;

b)

any indecomposable injective object of
\mathcal{D}
either lies in
\mathcal{L}
or has no non-zero subobject lying in
\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

We argue similarly as in
[
Gol
]
Prop. 11.3.

a)\Longrightarrow b)
: Let
E
be an indecomposable injective object in
\mathcal{D}
and let
t_{\mathcal{L}}(E)
denote the largest subobject of
E
contained in
\mathcal{L}
. Suppose that
t_{\mathcal{L}}(E)\neq 0
. Then
E
is an injective hull of
t_{\mathcal{L}}(E)
and hence, by stability, is contained in
\mathcal{L}
.

b)\Longrightarrow a)
: We write an injective hull
E(V)
of an object
V
lying in
\mathcal{L}
as a direct sum
E(V)=\oplus_{i\in I}E_{i}
of indecomposable injective objects
E_{i}
. Since
E_{i}\cap V\neq 0
lies in
\mathcal{L}
for any
i\in I
we see that all
E_{i}
and hence
E(V)
lie in
\mathcal{L}
.
∎

Corollary 3.5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a stable localising subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
; then

A(\mathcal{L})=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D}):E\not\in\ob(\mathcal{L})\}\ .

The following is a straightforward generalization of
[
Lou
]
Prop. 4.

Lemma 3.5.3
.

For a subset
A\subseteq\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
the following are equivalent:

a)

A=A(\mathcal{L})
for a stable localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{D}
;

b)

if
[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
satisfies
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,E^{\prime})\neq 0
for some
[E^{\prime}]\in A
, then
[E]\in A
.

Proof.

a)\Longrightarrow b)
: Let
[E^{\prime}]\in A(\mathcal{L})
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,E^{\prime})\neq 0
. Then
E
does not lie in
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is stable Lemma
3.5.1
applies and tells us that
E
does not have any non-zero subobject lying in
\mathcal{L}
. Hence
[E]\in A(\mathcal{L})=A
.

b)\Longrightarrow a)
: Let
\mathcal{L}
be the localising subcategory of
\mathcal{D}
cogenerated by the
E^{\prime}
for
[E^{\prime}]\in A
. This means that
\mathcal{L}
is the full subcategory of those objects
V
in
\mathcal{D}
which satisfy
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(V,E^{\prime})=0
for any
[E^{\prime}]\in A
. It is immediate that
A\subseteq A(\mathcal{L})
. Consider any
[E]\in A(\mathcal{L})
. Then
E
cannot lie in
\mathcal{L}
. Hence there must exist an
[E^{\prime}]\in A
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{D}}(E,E^{\prime})\neq 0
. It follows from (b) that
[E]\in A
. This shows that
A=A(\mathcal{L})
. To establish that
\mathcal{L}
is stable we use Lemma
3.5.1
. We have just seen that the
E
which do not lie in
\mathcal{L}
must have
[E]\in A(\mathcal{L})
. By the very definition of
A(\mathcal{L})
such
E
do not have a non-zero subobject lying in
\mathcal{L}
.
∎

A subset
A\subseteq\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
will be called
stable
, resp.
stable-open
, if it is of the form
A=A(\mathcal{L})
for some stable localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{D}
, resp. if it is stable and open for the Gabriel-Zariski topology. It is clear, for example from Lemma
3.5.3
, that arbitrary intersections and unions of stable subsets are stable again. Therefore the stable, resp. stable-open, subsets are the open subsets for a topology which we call the stable, resp. stable Zariski, topology of
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{D})
.

Corollary 3.5.4
.

The bijections
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
in (
2
) respect stable subsets; in particular, the inverse maps are continuous for the stable topologies.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the map
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
sending
[E]
to
[r(E)]
. Let
S\subseteq\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
be any stable subset and
[r(E^{\prime})]
be any point in the image of
S
. Now suppose that we have
[U]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(U,r(E^{\prime}))\neq 0
. Then
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(U,E^{\prime})\neq 0
and hence
\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(E_{\mathcal{C}}(U),E^{\prime})\neq 0
. Using Lemma
3.5.3
we deduce that
[E_{\mathcal{C}}(U)]\in S
. But
U\cong r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(U))
. It follows that
[U]
lies in the image of
S
. Using again Lemma
3.5.3
we conclude that the image of
S
is stable.
∎

Lemma 3.5.5
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a stable localising subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
; then the right derived functors
R^{j}r
of
r
, for any
j\geq 0
, map
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
.

Proof.

Let
V
be any object in
\mathcal{L}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is stable an injective hull
E(V)
of
V
in
\mathcal{C}
lies already in
\mathcal{L}
. Hence we find an injective resolution
V\xrightarrow{\simeq}I^{\bullet}
of
V
in
\mathcal{C}
all of whose terms lie in
\mathcal{L}
. The values
R^{j}r(V)
of the right derived functors in question are the cohomology objects of the complex
r(I^{\bullet})
. On the other hand, Def.
3.1.1
.b easily implies that
r
maps
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
. It follows that the complex
r(I^{\bullet})
and then also its cohomology objects lie in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
.
∎

For the rest of this section we impose on our thickening
\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{A}
the additional condition that
\mathcal{A}
has a noetherian projective generator
P
. Note that
P
then also is noetherian as an object in
\mathcal{C}
. We let
H:=\End_{\mathcal{A}}(P)^{\op}=\End_{\mathcal{C}}(P)^{\op}
denote the opposite ring of the endomorphism ring of the generator
P
. In this situation one has the equivalence of categories

(4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\Mod(H)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)

(cf.
[
Pop
]
Cor. 5.9.5). In fact, the
\Ext
-functors
\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,-)
on
\mathcal{C}
, for
j\geq 0
, can naturally be viewed as functors

\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,-):\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\Mod(H)\ .

In particular, the natural isomorphism

(5)

\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,V)=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,R^{j}r(V))\qquad\text{for any $V$ in $\mathcal{C}$ and any $j\geq 0$}

from Cor.
3.2.2
is an isomorphism of
H
-modules.

Lemma 3.5.6
.

Let
A
and
V
be objects in
\mathcal{A}
.

a)

There is a natural exact sequence of abelian groups

0\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,V)\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,V)\xrightarrow{\varphi_{A,V}}\Hom_{H}(\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A),\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(P,V))\rightarrow\Ext^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,V)\ .

b)

If
V
is an injective object in
\mathcal{A}
, then
\varphi_{A,V}
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof.

a) By Prop.
3.2.1
we have the convergent Grothendieck spectral sequence

\Ext^{i}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,R^{j}r(V))\Rightarrow\Ext^{i+j}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,V)\ .

The exact sequence of low degree terms is

0\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,r(V))\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,V)\rightarrow\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(A,R^{1}r(V))\rightarrow\Ext^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,r(V))\ .

Since
V
lies in
\mathcal{A}
we have
r(V)=V
. Moreover, using (
5
) for the second equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(A,R^{1}r(V))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{H}(\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A),\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,R^{1}r(V)))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{H}(\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A),\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(P,V))\ .

b) Since
V
is injective, we have
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,V)=\Ext^{2}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,V)=0
.
∎

Now let
\mathcal{L}
be a localising subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathcal{L}\cap\mathcal{A}
is a localising subcategory of
\mathcal{A}
. We want to investigate how the stability requirements for
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
are related to each other. It is obvious that if
\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{C}
then
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
. The converse is more subtle. We denote the corresponding localising subcategory of
\Mod(H)
by

\mathcal{L}^{H}:=\ \text{essential image of $\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}$ in $\Mod(H)$ under the functor $\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,-)$}.

Proposition 3.5.7
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
and that
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
maps
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}^{H}
. Let
V
in
\mathcal{C}
be a nonzero object such that

a)

V=r_{1}(V)
, and

b)

r(V)
is the largest subobject of
V
contained in
\mathcal{L}
.

Then
V=r(V)
.

Proof.

We first note that we have
r(V)\neq 0
by Def.
3.1.1
.b.

Let
E_{\mathcal{C}}(V)
be an injective hull of
V
in
\mathcal{C}
and let
E=r_{1}(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))
. Then
r(E)=r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))
is an injective object in
\mathcal{A}
, and
r(V)\subseteq r(E)
. In fact, we have the inclusions

\textstyle{r(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{r(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{r(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq}
\textstyle{E_{\mathcal{C}}(V).}

By Lemma
3.3.1
.a the extension
r(V)\subseteq V
is essential. Hence the extension
r(V)\subseteq r(E)
is essential as well.
Since
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
and since
r(V)
lies
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
by assumption b), we see that
r(E)
lies in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
.

Now, because
r_{1}
is left exact,
V=r_{1}(V)\subseteq r_{1}(E_{\mathcal{C}}(V))=E
. Next,
(r(E)+V)/r(E)\cong V/(V\cap r(E))=V/r(V)
because
r
is left exact. Since
r(V)
is the largest subobject of
V
contained in
\mathcal{L}
, we see that
V/r(V)
is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free. Also,
V=r_{1}(V)
means that
V/r(V)
lies in
\mathcal{A}
. We deduce that
(r(E)+V)/r(E)\cong V/r(V)
lies in
\mathcal{A}
and is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free. Hence we need to show that
V\subseteq r(E)
.

Consider any intermediate object
r(E)\subseteq V^{\prime\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}:=r(E)+V\subseteq E
in
\mathcal{C}
. We obtain extension classes
e^{\prime}=[0\rightarrow r(E)\rightarrow V^{\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime}/r(E)\rightarrow 0]\in\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(V^{\prime}/r(E),r(E))
and analogously
e^{\prime\prime}\in\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{1}(V^{\prime\prime}/r(E),r(E))
. Note that
V^{\prime}/r(E)
,
V^{\prime\prime}/r(E)
, and
r(E)
are objects in
\mathcal{A}
with
V^{\prime}/r(E)
and
V^{\prime\prime}/r(E)
being
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
-torsion free and
r(E)
being injective in
\mathcal{A}
. Hence Lemma
3.5.6
applies. We define the
H
-modules
N^{\prime\prime}:=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,V^{\prime\prime}/r(E))\subseteq N^{\prime}:=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,V^{\prime}/r(E))
and obtain the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(V^{\prime}/r(E),r(E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\subseteq^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Hom_{H}(N^{\prime},\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,r(E)))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\res}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(V^{\prime\prime}/r(E),r(E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Hom_{H}(N^{\prime\prime},\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,r(E))).}

with horizontal isomorphisms. Obviously the left perpendicular arrow maps
e^{\prime}
to
e^{\prime\prime}
. Since
r(E)
lies in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
the
H
-module
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,r(E))
lies in
\mathcal{L}^{H}
by our assumptions. We have the
H
-linear map
f^{\prime}:=\varphi^{\prime}(e^{\prime}):N^{\prime}\rightarrow\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,r(E))
. Suppose now that
N^{\prime}\neq 0
and choose a nonzero element
v\in N^{\prime}
. Let
J:=\Ann_{H}(f^{\prime}(v))\subseteq H
be the annihilator left ideal. Then
H/J\cong Hf^{\prime}(v)
lie in
\mathcal{L}^{H}
. We now consider specifically the
H
-submodule
N^{\prime\prime}:=Jv\subseteq N^{\prime}
. By the category equivalence there is a unique
V^{\prime\prime}
as above such that
N^{\prime\prime}=\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,V^{\prime\prime}/r(E))
. We first check that
N^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
. Otherwise we would have
J\subseteq\Ann_{H}(v)
, so that
H/J
surjects onto
Hv\subseteq N^{\prime}
, which implies that
Hv
lies in
\mathcal{L}^{H}
. On the other hand the modules
Hv\subseteq N^{\prime}
are
\mathcal{L}^{H}
-torsion free, which is a contradiction. Hence
N^{\prime\prime}\neq 0
. Now

\res(\varphi^{\prime}(e^{\prime}))(N^{\prime\prime})=\res(f^{\prime})(N^{\prime\prime})=f^{\prime}(N^{\prime\prime})=f^{\prime}(Jv)=Jf^{\prime}(v)=0\ .

The commutativity of the above diagram then implies that the extension class
e^{\prime\prime}=0
, i.e., the short exact sequence
0\rightarrow r(E)\rightarrow V^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow V^{\prime\prime}/r(E)\rightarrow 0
in
\mathcal{C}
splits. But then because
V^{\prime\prime}/r(E)\neq 0
we see
r(E)
is not essential in
E
, which contradicts Lemma
3.3.1
.a. It follows that
N^{\prime}
must be zero, i.e., that
r(E)+V=r(E)
.
∎

Theorem 3.5.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
. Then the following are equivalent:

a)

\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{C}
,

b)

R^{j}r
maps
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
for all
j\geq 0
,

c)

\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
maps
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}^{H}
for all
j\geq 0
,

d)

\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
maps
\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}^{H}
,

e)

\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
maps
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
to
\mathcal{L}^{H}
.

Proof.

a)
\Rightarrow
b). This is Lemma
3.5.5
.

b)
\Rightarrow
c). This follows from (
5
) and the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{H}
.

c)
\Rightarrow
d)
\Rightarrow
e). These are trivial.

e)
\Rightarrow
a). Let
E
be an indecomposable injective object in
\mathcal{C}
and let
Y
be its largest subobject in
\mathcal{L}
. We suppose that
Y\neq 0
and will aim to show that
Y=E
. Let
E_{n}:=r_{n}(E)
for
n\geq 0
and note that
E=\bigcup\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}E_{n}
by Lemma
3.1.5
. Correspondingly
Y=\bigcup\limits_{n=0}^{\infty}Y_{n}
with
Y_{n}:=r_{n}(Y)
, and note that
Y_{n}=Y\cap V_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
because each
r_{n}
is left exact.

Since
Y
is the largest subobject of
E
contained in
\mathcal{L}
,
E/Y
is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free. Hence for any
n\geq 0
,
(E_{n}+Y)/Y
is also
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free. But
(E_{n}+Y)/Y\cong E_{n}/(E_{n}\cap Y)=E_{n}/Y_{n}
. Hence for all
n\geq 0
,
E_{n}/Y_{n}
is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free, and
Y_{n}
is the largest subobject of
E_{n}
contained in
\mathcal{L}
.

With
Y\neq 0
also
Y_{0}=r(Y)
is nonzero. Since
E
is an indecomposable injective in
\mathcal{C}
, it follows that
Y_{0}
is essential in
E
.

Suppose for a contradiction that
Y\neq E
and let
n
be minimal such that
Y_{n}\neq E_{n}
. Now,
Y_{0}\subseteq E_{0}
is an essential extension in
\mathcal{A}
with
Y_{0}
lying in
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
; since
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
we see that
Y_{0}=E_{0}
. Hence
n\geq 1
, and the minimality of
n
implies that
Y_{n-1}=E_{n-1}
so that
E_{n-1}\subseteq Y_{n}
.

Now, consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow Y_{n}/E_{n-1}\rightarrow E_{n}/E_{n-1}\rightarrow E_{n}/Y_{n}\rightarrow 0

in
\mathcal{A}
. Note that
Y_{n}/E_{n-1}
lies in
\mathcal{L}
, and that
E_{n}/Y_{n}
is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free and nonzero. If
Y_{n}/E_{n-1}
is also nonzero, then because
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
, this extension is not essential. This gives us some subobject
W
of
E_{n}
containing
E_{n-1}
, such that
W/E_{n-1}
is isomorphic to a nonzero subobject of
E_{n}/Y_{n}
; thus
W/E_{n-1}
is also nonzero and
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free. If
Y_{n}=E_{n-1}
, then we can take
W=E_{n}
to obtain an object with the same properties.

Write
E_{-1}:=0
and
X:=W/E_{n-2}\subseteq E_{n}/E_{n-2}
, and consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow E_{n-1}/E_{n-2}\rightarrow X\rightarrow W/E_{n-1}\rightarrow 0\ .

Since the outer terms in this short exact sequence lie in
\mathcal{A}
, we see that
r_{1}(X)=X
. Also,
r(X)\subseteq r(E_{n}/E_{n-2})=E_{n-1}/E_{n-2}=r(E_{n-1}/E_{n-2})\subseteq r(X)
, where the left equality uses Lemma
3.1.2
.b, shows that

r(X)=E_{n-1}/E_{n-2}\subsetneqq X\ .

Since
W/E_{n-1}
is
\mathcal{L}
-torsion free and
E_{n-1}/E_{n-2}=Y_{n-1}/E_{n-2}
is a subquotient of
Y
, we see that
r(X)
is the largest subobject of
X
contained in
\mathcal{L}
. Applying now Prop.
3.5.7
with
X
leads to a contradiction.
∎

3.6.
A ring-theoretic case

In this section, we discuss stability for the category
\mathcal{C}=\Mod(H)
of left
H
-modules over a ring
H
which is assumed to be left noetherian and finitely generated as a module over its centre
Z(H)
. This material will be needed in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec:Z-scheme}
.

The ring
Z(H)
is necessarily noetherian by
[
MCR
]
Cor. 10.1.11(ii). The ring
H
is also right noetherian, and the abelian categories
\Mod(H)
and
\Mod(Z(H))
are locally noetherian Grothendieck categories. Of course,
\Spec(H)
and
\Spec(Z(H))
denote the prime ideal spectra of the respective rings equipped with its Zariski topology.

Let
\varrho:\Mod(H)\rightarrow\Mod(Z(H))
denote the functor of passing to the underlying
Z(H)
-module. By
[
Gab
]
Prop. 10 on p. 428 any localising subcategory of
\Mod(Z(H))
is stable. We further have the following facts.

Lemma 3.6.1
.

a)

For any localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\Mod(Z(H))
the localising subcategory
\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L})
of
\Mod(H)
is stable.

b)

For any ordinal
\alpha
we have
\varrho^{-1}(\Mod(Z(H))_{\alpha})=\Mod(H)_{\alpha}
, which therefore is stable.

c)

For any ordinal
\alpha<\kappa(\Mod(H))
we have

\mathbf{Sp}_{\alpha}(\Mod(H))=\{[E]\in\mathbf{Sp}(\Mod(H)):\kappa(E)=\alpha+1\}.

Proof.

a)
[
Gab
]
Prop. 12 on p. 431. b) The equality is
[
MCR
]
Cor. 10.1.10, and the stability then follows from a). c) Because of b) this follows from Remark
2.2.1
.
∎

Next we have:

(A)

The continuous map
\varphi:\Spec(H)\rightarrow\Spec(Z(H))
sending
\mathfrak{p}
to
\mathfrak{p}\cap Z(H)
is surjective with finite fibers (cf.
[
Gab
]
Prop. 11 on p. 429).

(B)

The map

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sp}(\Mod(H))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}\Spec(H)

\displaystyle[J]

\displaystyle\longmapsto\text{the unique prime ideal associated to $J$}.

is a homeomorphism w.r.t. the Gabriel-Zariski and the Zariski topology on the left hand and right hand side, respectively (
[
Ste
]
Thm. VII.2.1 recalling the fact that
H
is fully left and right bounded by
[
Ste
]
VII §2 Example 4),
[
Gab
]
V §4). Let
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(H)
. Fix an injective hull
E(H/\mathfrak{p})
of
H/\mathfrak{p}
in
\Mod(H)
. From
[
Ste
]
Prop. VII.1.9 we know that
E(H/\mathfrak{p})\cong\oplus_{i\in I}E_{\mathfrak{p}}
for a single indecomposable injective
H
-module
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Using
[
Ste
]
Lemma VII.1.7 we see that the inverse of the bijection (
6
) is given by
\mathfrak{p}\longmapsto[E_{\mathfrak{p}}]
.

First we describe the stable Zariski topology, where we will always make silently the identification (
6
). Hence on
\Spec(Z(H))
the stable Zariski topology is the Zariski topology. From
[
Gab
]
V §4 we know that the Ziegler-open subsets of
\Spec(H)
are the possibly infinite unions of Zariski-closed subsets. Hence the quasi-compact Ziegler-open subsets are the Zariski-closed subsets. Therefore the open subsets for the stable Zariski topology are the stable Zariski-open subsets.

Lemma 3.6.2
.

A(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))=\varphi^{-1}(A(\mathcal{L}))
for any localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}
of
\Mod(Z(H))
.

Proof.

(Recall the notation
A(\mathcal{L})
from
\lx@sectionsign
3.4
.) Using Cor.
3.5.2
we compute

\displaystyle A(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{p}\not\in\Spec(H):E_{\mathfrak{p}}\in\ob(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(H):H/\mathfrak{p}\not\in\ob(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(H):\varrho(H/\mathfrak{p})\not\in\ob(\mathcal{L})\}

\displaystyle\supseteq\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(H):Z(H)/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})\not\in\ob(\mathcal{L})\}

\displaystyle=\varphi^{-1}(A(\mathcal{L}))\ .

For the reverse inclusion we suppose that
Z(H)/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})
lies in
\mathcal{L}
. Being a finitely generated
Z(H)/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})
-module
\varrho(H/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})H)
also must lie in
\ob(\mathcal{L})
and hence
H/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})H\in\ob(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))
. It follows that
H/\mathfrak{p}
, as a quotient of
H/\varphi(\mathfrak{p})H
, is contained in
\ob(\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}))
.
∎

Lemma 3.6.3
.

For
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\in\Spec(H)
we have:

a)

Let
z\in Z(H)
; if
z\in\mathfrak{p}
, then
z
acts locally nilpotently on
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
; if
z\not\in\mathfrak{p}
then
z
acts invertibly on
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
.

b)

If
\Hom_{H}(E_{\mathfrak{p}},E_{\mathfrak{q}})\neq 0
then
\mathfrak{p}\cap Z(H)\subseteq\mathfrak{q}\cap Z(H)
.

Proof.

a) Let
L
be a uniform left ideal in
H/\mathfrak{p}
so that
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the injective hull
E(L)
of
L
.

Suppose first that
z\notin\mathfrak{p}
. Multiplication by
z
is an injective
H
-linear map
\ell_{z}:H/\mathfrak{p}\to H/\mathfrak{p}
: if
x+\mathfrak{p}\in H/\mathfrak{p}
is such that
z(x+\mathfrak{p})=\mathfrak{p}
then
(HzH)(HxH)\subseteq\mathfrak{p}
since
z
is central in
H
, so
x\in\mathfrak{p}
since
\mathfrak{p}
is prime and
z\notin\mathfrak{p}
. Since
L
is an
H
-submodule of
H/\mathfrak{p}
,
\ell_{z}:L\to L
is also injective. Hence it extends to an injective
H
-linear map
\ell_{z}:E(L)\to E(L)
by the injectivity of
E(L)
. The image of
\ell_{z}
must admit a complement in
E(L)
since it is itself injective. Since
E(L)\cong E_{\mathfrak{p}}
is indecomposable,
\ell_{z}:E(L)\to E(L)
is an isomorphism, as claimed.

Now suppose that
z\in\mathfrak{p}
and let
x\in E_{\mathfrak{p}}
be non-zero. Since
L
is essential in
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
, we see that
Hx\cap L
is essential in
Hx
. Since
H
is noetherian and
z
is central, the ideal
zH
of
H
satisfies the left Artin-Rees property by
[
MCR
]
Prop. 4.2.6. Since
z\in\mathfrak{p}
kills
L
, it also kills
Hx\cap L
. So
z^{n}
kills
Hx
for some
n\geq 1
by the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii) of
[
MCR
]
Thm. 4.2.2.

b) Let
\phi:E_{\mathfrak{p}}\rightarrow E_{\mathfrak{q}}
be a nonzero map. Suppose that there is a
z\in(\mathfrak{p}\cap Z(H))\setminus\mathfrak{q}
. Then, by a),
z
acts invertibly on
E_{\mathfrak{q}}
, but locally nilpotently on
E_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Consider any
x\in E_{\mathfrak{p}}
such that
\phi(x)\neq 0
. We find a
t\geq 1
such that
z^{t}x=0
. Hence
z^{t}\phi(x)=\phi(z^{t}x)=\phi(0)=0
, which is a contradiction.
∎

For any subset
A\subseteq\Spec(H)
the full subcategory
\mathcal{L}_{A}
of all modules
U
in
\Mod(H)
such that
\Hom_{H}(U,E_{\mathfrak{p}})=0
for any
\mathfrak{p}\in A
is a localising subcategory of
\Mod(H)
. Obviously we have
A\subseteq A(\mathcal{L}_{A})
.

We now describe explicitly all localising subcategories of
\Mod(H)
which are of the form
\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{L})
as in Lemma
3.6.1
.a.

Recall that a subset
S
of a topological space
T
is called
generalization-closed
if any point
y\in T
whose closure
\overline{\{y\}}
contains a point in
S
also lies in
S
. Equivalently,
S
is generalization-closed if and only if it is a (possibly infinite) intersection of open subsets of
T
.

Before Lemma
3.6.2
we had noted that the Ziegler-open subsets of
\Spec(H)
are the unions of Zariski-closed subsets. Hence the Ziegler-closed subsets are the generalization-closed subsets. Therefore the inverse of the bijection (
3
) is the map

set of all generalization-closed

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\ \ \text{collection of all localising}

subsets of
\Spec(Z(H))

subcategories of
\Mod(Z(H))

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z}:=\ \text{all $M$ with $\Hom_{Z(H)}(M,E)=0$}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{ for any $[E]\in W$}.

We conclude from Lemma
3.6.2
that

(7)

\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z})=\mathcal{L}_{\varphi^{-1}(W)}\ .

For an ideal
I
of
Z(H)
, recall that
V(I)
denotes the subset of
\Spec(Z(H))
consisting of all prime ideals containing
I
.

Proposition 3.6.4
.

Let
W
be a generalization-closed subset of
\Spec(Z(H))
. The following are equivalent for an
H
-module
M
:

a)

M
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi^{-1}(W)}
;

b)

V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v))\cap W=\emptyset
for all nonzero
v\in M
;

c)

V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(N))\cap W=\emptyset
for all noetherian submodules
N\subseteq M
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi^{-1}(W)}
is localising we may assume that
M
is finitely generated. If
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}
are generators of the
H
-module
M
then

V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(M))=V\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{r}\Ann_{Z(H)}(v_{i})\right)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v_{i}))\ .

This shows the equivalence of b) and c).

For the equivalence of a) and b) we first note that, because
H
is noetherian,
M
contains an essential submodule
N
of the form
N=N_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus N_{r}
, where each
N_{i}
is a uniform
H
-module (
[
GW
]
Cor. 5.18 and Prop. 5.15). Hence
E(M)=E(N)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}E(N_{i})
. By
[
GW
]
Lemma 5.26, for each
i=1,\cdots,r
there is a unique prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
of
H
which is equal to the annihilator of some nonzero
H
-submodule
N_{i}^{\prime}
of
N_{i}
, and which contains the annihilators of all nonzero
H
-submodules of
N_{i}
. This
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
is the
assassinator
of
N_{i}
in the sense of
[
GW
]
Def. on p. 102.

Since
H
is assumed to be a finitely generated module over its centre, it is an FBN-ring by
[
GW
]
Prop. 9.1(a). Then we can apply
[
GW
]
Prop. 9.14 to see that the uniform injective
H
-module
E(N_{i})
is isomorphic to the
H
-injective hull
E(L_{i})
of any uniform left ideal
L_{i}
of
H/\mathfrak{p}_{i}
. In other words (
[
GW
]
Lemma 5.14),
E(N_{i})\cong E_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
, and

E(M)\cong E(N)\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}E(N_{i})\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}E_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}\ .

We identify
M
with its image inside
E(M)
. Note that
M
is not contained in the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i\neq j}E_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
for any
j
, because otherwise we would have
M\cap E_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}}=0
, which contradicts the fact that
M
is essential in
E(M)
. So,
\Hom_{H}(M,E_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}})
is nonzero for any
j
.

a)
\Rightarrow
b). Suppose that
M
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi^{-1}(W)}
. From
\Hom_{H}(M,E_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}})\neq 0
for any
j
we deduce that
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\notin\varphi^{-1}(W)
for all
j
. Now consider any nonzero
v\in M
and any
\mathfrak{p}\in\varphi^{-1}(V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v)))
. Because
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\cap Z(H)
acts locally nilpotently on
E_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}
by Lemma
3.6.3
.a, we see that

((\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap Z(H))(\mathfrak{p}_{2}\cap Z(H))\cdots(\mathfrak{p}_{r}\cap Z(H))^{k}\cdot v=0

for some
k>0
. This implies that
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\cap Z(H)\subseteq\mathfrak{p}\cap Z(H)
for some
j
. Since
W
is generalization-closed and
\mathfrak{p}_{j}\cap Z(H)\notin W
, we see that
\mathfrak{p}\cap Z(H)\notin W
. Hence
V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v))\cap W=\emptyset
.

b)
\Rightarrow
a). Suppose that
\Hom_{H}(M,E_{\mathfrak{q}})\neq 0
for some
\mathfrak{q}\in\varphi^{-1}(W)
. Then
\Hom_{H}(E_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}},E_{\mathfrak{q}})\neq 0
for some
1\leq i\leq r
and so
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\cap Z(H)\subseteq\mathfrak{q}\cap Z(H)
by Lemma
3.6.3
.b. Hence
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\cap Z(H)\in W
since
W
is generalization-closed.

Choose a non-zero element
v\in N_{i}^{\prime}
; since
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
kills
N_{i}^{\prime}\supseteq Hv
we have
\mathfrak{p}_{i}\subseteq\Ann_{H}(Hv)
; on the other hand,
\mathfrak{p}_{i}
contains
\Ann_{H}(Hv)
as we saw above and hence
\mathfrak{p}_{i}=\Ann_{H}(Hv)
. Since
Z(H)
is central in
H
, we have
\Ann_{Z(H)}(v)=\Ann_{Z(H)}(Hv)
. This ideal of
Z(H)
is equal to
\Ann_{H}(Hv)\cap Z(H)=\mathfrak{p}_{i}\cap Z(H)
. Thus
\Ann_{Z(H)}(v)=\mathfrak{p}_{i}\cap Z(H)
lies in
W
and therefore
V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v))\cap W\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Obviously we have a corresponding result for modules over
Z(H)
.

Remark 3.6.5
.

Let
W
be a generalization-closed subset of
\Spec(Z(H))
. The following are equivalent for a
Z(H)
-module
M
:

a)

M
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z}
;

b)

V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(v))\cap W=\emptyset
for all nonzero
v\in M
;

c)

V(\Ann_{Z(H)}(N))\cap W=\emptyset
for all noetherian submodules
N\subseteq M
.

3.7.
A stability criterion for thickenings

Throughout this section let
\mathcal{C}
be a locally noetherian Grothendieck category which is a thickening of its full abelian subcategory
\mathcal{A}
. Recall from
\lx@sectionsign
3
that we then have the increasing filtration
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\hookrightarrow\ldots\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{j}\hookrightarrow\ldots
of
\mathcal{C}
by full abelian subcategories, which all are locally noetherian Grothendieck categories. By (
2
) the corresponding injective spectra all are in bijection

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle[E]

\displaystyle\longmapsto r_{j}([E])\longmapsto r([E])\ .

By Cor.
3.4.6
and Cor.
3.5.4
the inverses of these bijections are continuous for the stable as well as the stable Zariski topologies. Actually it is straightforward to deduce that, for
i\leq j
, the map

\displaystyle\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{j})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\;\simeq\;}\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A}_{i})

\displaystyle x=[E_{x}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto r_{j,i}(x):=r_{i}(x)=[r_{i}(E_{x})]

is a bijection with inverse
[U]\mapsto[E_{\mathcal{A}_{j}}(U)]
, and this inverse is continuous for the stable as well as the stable Zariski topologies.

In this section we will present a technique to actually find stable subsets in
\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})
. For this we assume again from now on that
\mathcal{A}
has a noetherian projective generator
P
. Recall that
H=\End_{\mathcal{A}}(P)^{\op}=\End_{\mathcal{C}}(P)^{\op}
and let
Z=Z(H)
denote its centre. By the equivalence of categories (
4
) this centre
Z
acts naturally on any object in
\mathcal{A}
, i.e.,
Z=Z(\mathcal{A})
is the centre of the category
\mathcal{A}
.

But we need further assumptions. The first one is:

(A1)

H
is finitely generated as a module over its centre
Z
.

The central tool for our investigation will be the (graded)
(H,H)
-bimodule

\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(P,P):=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\infty}\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{i}(P,P)\ .

This, in particular, is a module for
Z\otimes Z:=Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Z
. We let
\mathcal{J}:=\Ann_{Z\otimes Z}(\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(P,P))
be its annihilator ideal and
\mathcal{R}:=V(\mathcal{J})\subseteq\Spec(Z\otimes Z)
be the corresponding Zariski closed subset. Further let
\pi_{i}:\mathcal{R}\rightarrow\Xi:=\Spec(Z)
, for
i=1,2
, denote the restrictions of the two projection maps
\Spec(Z\otimes Z)\rightrightarrows\Xi
. We then have a coequaliser diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{q}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Z}}

in the category of locally ringed spaces (cf.
[
DG
]
Prop. I.1.1.6). We briefly recall that
\mathbf{q}
is a topological quotient map which identifies the points
\pi_{1}(x)
and
\pi_{2}(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{R}
, and that the sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Z}}
is given, for any open subset
U\subseteq\Xi
, by the equalizer diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{Z}}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{q}^{\hash}=\subseteq}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\Xi}(\mathbf{q}^{-1}(U))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}^{\hash}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}^{\hash}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}}((\mathbf{q}\pi_{i})^{-1}(U))\ .}

In section
3.6
we considered, for any generalization-closed subset
W\subseteq\Xi
, the localising subcategories

\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z}
of
\Mod(Z)
and
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}:=\mathcal{L}_{\varphi^{-1}(W)}
of
\Mod(H)
. These satisfy
\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z})=\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
, by (
7
). Under the category equivalence (
4
) the subcategory
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
corresponds to the localising subcategory

\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{A}}:=\ \text{all $A$ in $\mathcal{A}$ such that $\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)$ lies in $\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}$}

of
\mathcal{A}
. Finally, by Prop.
3.4.3
, we have the localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{C}}:=\langle\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{A}}\rangle
of
\mathcal{C}
, which is the unique localising subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{C}}=\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

Theorem 3.7.1
.

In addition to
(A1)
we assume:

(A2)

There is an integer
d
such that
\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(P,-)|_{\mathcal{A}}=0
for any
j>d
.

(A3)

Z\otimes Z/\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
is noetherian.

Then, for any generalization-closed subset
W\subseteq\Xi
with the property that
\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}^{-1}(W))\subseteq W
, the localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{C}}
is stable.

We begin the proof by recalling the following useful fact.

Lemma 3.7.2
.

Let
M
be a noetherian object in
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,-)
commutes with filtered colimits for all
j\geq 0
.

Proof.

In the Grothendieck category
\mathcal{C}
filtered colimits are exact. Moreover, because
\mathcal{C}
is locally noetherian and
M
is a noetherian object in
\mathcal{C}
,
[
Gab
]
Cor. 1 on p. 358 tells us that:

–

the full subcategory of injective objects in
\mathcal{C}
is closed under filtered colimits;

–

the functor
F:=\Hom_{\mathcal{C}}(M,-)
from
\mathcal{C}
to the category of abelian groups commutes with filtered colimits.

In this situation,
[
KS
]
Prop. 15.3.3 implies that the derived functors
R^{j}F=\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,-)
of
F
commute with filtered colimits.
∎

Next, we first need an auxiliary result. Since
Z=Z(\mathcal{A})
the ring
Z\otimes Z
acts, for any two objects
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
in
\mathcal{A}
, naturally on the groups
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(A_{1},A_{2})
with the first and second factor in
Z\otimes Z
acting through endomorphisms in the category
\mathcal{C}
on
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, respectively.

Lemma 3.7.3
.

Suppose that
(A2)
holds true, and let
A
be an arbitrary object in
\mathcal{A}
; then, for any
j\geq 0
, the
Z\otimes Z
-module
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)
is annihilated by
\mathcal{J}^{d+1-j}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7.2
, the functors
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
, for any
j\geq 0
, commute with arbitrary direct sums. It follows that
\mathcal{J}
annihilates
\Ext_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(P,F)
for any
F
which is a possibly infinite direct sums of copies of
P
. Since
P
is a generator
2
2

2

The projectivity of
P
is not needed for this argument.
of
\mathcal{A}
, using
[
Ste
]
p. 93 Prop 6.2 repeatedly we find an exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
of the form

\ldots\longrightarrow F_{n}\longrightarrow\ldots\longrightarrow F_{0}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0

where each
F_{n}
is a direct sum of copies of
P
. We break it up into short exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow Y_{0}\rightarrow F_{0}\rightarrow A\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow Y_{1}\rightarrow F_{1}\rightarrow Y_{0}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow Y_{n}\rightarrow F_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n-1}\rightarrow 0

\displaystyle\cdots

Applying
\Ext^{*}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
we obtain exact sequences

\displaystyle\Ext^{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,F_{0})\rightarrow\Ext^{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Ext^{i+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{0})

\displaystyle\Ext^{i+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,F_{1})\rightarrow\Ext^{i+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{0})\xrightarrow{\delta}\Ext^{i+2}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{1})

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle\Ext^{i+n}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,F_{n})\rightarrow\Ext^{i+n}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{n-1})\xrightarrow{\delta}\Ext^{i+n+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{n})\ ,

\displaystyle\cdots

where
\delta
denotes the connecting homomorphisms. These are sequences of
Z\otimes Z
-bimodules. The first term in each sequence is annihilated by
\mathcal{J}
. Now choose
n:=d-i
. Then
\Ext^{i+n+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,Y_{n})=0
. The assertion now follows by downward induction along the above sequences.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.7.1
.

Since
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}=\varrho^{-1}(\mathcal{L}_{W}^{Z})
, it follows from Lemma
3.6.1
that
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
is stable in
\Mod(H)
. Hence
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathcal{A}
is stable in
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore, by Thm.
3.5.8
, it is enough to show that the functor
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
maps
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{A}}
to
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
. By Lemma
3.7.2
, the functor
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
commutes with filtered colimits. Hence it suffices to show that for any noetherian object
A
in
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{\mathcal{C}}\cap\mathcal{A}
, the
H
-module
M:=\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
. Since the functor
\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,-)
is also half-exact, we may assume further, using a prime series for
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
as in
[
GW
]
Prop. 3.13, that the annihilator of any nonzero
H
-submodule of
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
is a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of
H
. Then necessarily
\mathfrak{p}=\Ann_{H}(\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A))
, and we write
\mathfrak{p}_{Z}:=\mathfrak{p}\cap Z\in\Xi
.

By Lemma
3.7.3
the
Z\otimes Z
-module
M=\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)
is killed by
\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
. Letting
\mathcal{R}_{d}:=\Spec((Z\otimes Z)/\mathcal{J}^{d+1})
, we can then regard
M
as an
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})=(Z\otimes Z)/\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
-module in a natural way. Next, consider the commutative diagram

(8)

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}}
\textstyle{\Xi}

where
i
is the closed embedding, and the
f_{j}
are, similarly as before, the restrictions of the two projection maps.

Let
S:=\Xi\setminus W
; then our assumption
\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}^{-1}(W))\subseteq W
is equivalent to
\pi_{1}^{-1}(W)\subseteq\pi_{2}^{-1}(W)
, which is the same as
\pi_{2}^{-1}(S)\subseteq\pi_{1}^{-1}(S)
. Since
\pi_{j}=f_{j}\circ i
, this is equivalent to
i^{-1}f_{2}^{-1}(S)\subseteq i^{-1}f_{1}^{-1}(S)
. However,
i
is a bijection because
\mathcal{J}
is nilpotent modulo
\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
, so this is in turn equivalent to
f_{2}^{-1}(S)\subseteq f_{1}^{-1}(S)
.

Now,
\mathfrak{p}
is the annihilator of any nonzero
H
-submodule of
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
, so
\mathfrak{p}_{Z}=\mathfrak{p}\cap Z=\Ann_{Z}(v)
for all nonzero
v\in\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
. Since
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
lies in
\mathcal{L}_{W}^{H}
, this means that
V(\mathfrak{p}_{Z})\cap W=\emptyset
by Prop.
3.6.4
, and hence
V(\mathfrak{p}_{Z})\subseteq S=\Xi\setminus W
.

By assumption
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})=(Z\otimes Z)/\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
is noetherian. Let
\overline{i}_{2}:Z\to(Z\otimes Z)/\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
, sending
z
to
1\otimes z+\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
, be the ring homomorphism which induces
f_{2}
. Let
Q_{1},\cdots,Q_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})
be the minimal prime ideals lying above
\overline{i}_{2}(\mathfrak{p}_{Z})\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})
; then
Q_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots Q_{k}^{a_{k}}\subseteq\overline{i}_{2}(\mathfrak{p}_{Z})\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})
for some positive integers
a_{1},\cdots,a_{k}
. Since
Q_{j}\in f_{2}^{-1}(V(\mathfrak{p}_{Z}))\subseteq f_{2}^{-1}(S)\subseteq f_{1}^{-1}(S)
, we see that
\mathfrak{q}_{j}:=f_{1}(Q_{j})
lies in
S
for all
j=1,\cdots,k
. Hence
V(\mathfrak{q}_{j})\subseteq S
for all
j
, because
S
is specialization-closed.

With
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(P,A)
also
A
is killed by
\mathfrak{p}_{Z}
. Hence
M=\Ext^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,A)
is killed by
