Z\otimes\mathfrak{p}_{Z}
as well as
\mathcal{J}^{d+1}
. We see that
M
is killed by
\overline{i}_{2}(\mathfrak{p}_{Z})\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{d})\supseteq Q_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots Q_{k}^{a_{k}}
. Hence
\mathfrak{q}_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\mathfrak{q}_{k}^{a_{k}}\cdot v=0
for any
v\in M
, so
V(\Ann_{Z}(v))\subseteq V(\mathfrak{q}_{1})\cup\cdots\cup V(\mathfrak{q}_{k})\subseteq S
. Therefore the
H
-module
M
(where
H
acts on
M
through its action on
P
) lies in
\mathcal{L}^{H}_{W}
by Prop.
3.6.4
as required.
∎

Now we form the following diagram, which defines the map
\tau
:

\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{A})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}(\Mod(H))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Spec(H)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{\Spec(Z)=\Xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{q}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{Z}=\Xi/\mathfrak{R}}

Proposition 3.7.4
.

Assume
(A1), (A2), (A3)
. If
U\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}
is an intersection of open subsets, then
\tau^{-1}(U)
is stable.

Proof.

Put
W=\mathbf{q}^{-1}(U)
. Then

\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}^{-1}(W))=\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{q}^{-1}(U))=\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{q}^{-1}(U))\subseteq\mathbf{q}^{-1}(U)=W.

Now apply Thm.
3.7.1
.
∎

Remark 3.7.5
.

Suppose that our category
\mathcal{C}
is
k
-linear. Then the
Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Z
-action on the
\Ext
-groups
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(M,N)
for
M,N
in
\mathcal{A}
, factors through
Z\otimes_{k}Z
. Therefore the proof of Theorem
3.7.1
goes through if we replace
\mathbf{(A3)}
by the weaker assumption that
(Z\otimes_{k}Z)/\overline{\mathcal{J}}^{d+1}
is noetherian, where
\overline{\mathcal{J}}
is the image of
\mathcal{J}
under the surjection
Z\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}Z\twoheadrightarrow Z\otimes_{k}Z
.

We also apply Thm.
3.7.1
to give a sufficient criterion for the stability of the Krull-dimension filtration of the locally noetherian category
\mathcal{C}
. For every ordinal
\alpha
, the set

W_{\alpha}:=\{\mathfrak{p}\in\Xi:\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p})>\alpha\}

is a generalization-stable subset of
\Xi
. We identify the corresponding localising subcategory
\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{\alpha}}
of
\mathcal{C}
in Lemma
3.7.7
below, but first we recall a standard fact about Krull dimension.

Lemma 3.7.6
.

Let
M
be a finitely generated module over a commutative noetherian ring
A
. Then

\kappa(M)=\sup_{v\in M}\kappa(Av)=\sup_{v\in M}\quad\sup_{\mathfrak{p}\in V(\ann(v))}\kappa(A/\mathfrak{p}).

Proof.

An induction on the number of generators of
M
together with
[
MCR
]
Lemma 6.2.4 shows the first equality, namely
\kappa(M)=\sup_{v\in M}\kappa(Av)
.

Let
I
be an ideal of
A
. Since
A
is noetherian,
[
MCR
]
Lemma 6.2.4 implies that
\kappa(A/I)=\kappa(A/I^{m})
for all
m\geq 0
. Since
(\sqrt{I})^{m}\subseteq I
for sufficiently large
m
, we have
\kappa(A/I)\geq\kappa(A/\sqrt{I})=\kappa(A/(\sqrt{I})^{m})\geq\kappa(A/I)
, so in fact we have the equality
\kappa(A/I)=\kappa(A/\sqrt{I})
. Since
\sqrt{I}
is equal to the intersection of the finitely many minimal primes
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{p}_{n}
containing it, there is a natural
A
-linear embedding
A/\sqrt{I}\hookrightarrow\oplus_{i=1}^{n}A/\mathfrak{p}_{i}
. So,

\kappa(A/I)=\kappa(A/\sqrt{I})\leq\sup_{1\leq i\leq n}\kappa(A/\mathfrak{p}_{i})\leq\sup_{\mathfrak{p}\in V(I)}\kappa(A/\mathfrak{p})\leq\kappa(A/I).

Therefore for any
v\in M
,
\kappa(Av)=\kappa(A/\Ann_{A}(v))=\sup\limits_{\mathfrak{p}\in V(\ann(v))}\kappa(A/\mathfrak{p})
.
∎

Recall (
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: KdimSec}
) that
\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of objects
V
such that
\kappa(V)\leq\alpha
.

Lemma 3.7.7
.

For any ordinal
\alpha
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{\alpha}}=\mathcal{C}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

By Gabriel’s definition of Krull dimension found at
[
Gab
]
p. 382, we see that
\kappa(V)=\sup_{i}\kappa(V_{i})
, if we write
V=\varinjlim V_{i}
as a filtered colimit of its noetherian subobjects
V_{i}
. Therefore, it is enough to show that for noetherian
V\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
V\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathcal{C}}_{W_{\alpha}}
if and only if
\kappa(V)\leq\alpha
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is a thickening of
\mathcal{A}
, we may further assume that
V\in\mathcal{A}
. Hence we are reduced to showing that for finitely generated
H
-modules
M
, we have

M\in\mathcal{L}^{H}_{W_{\alpha}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\kappa(M)\leq\alpha.

However since
H
is a finitely generated
Z
-module,
\kappa(M)=\kappa(\rho(M))
by
[
MCR
]
Cor. 10.1.10, whereas
M\in\mathcal{L}^{H}_{W_{\alpha}}
if and only if
\rho(M)\in\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{W_{\alpha}}
by equation (
7
). Next,
\rho(M)\in\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{W_{\alpha}}
if and only if for all
v\in M
and all
\mathfrak{p}\in\Spec(Z)
containing
\Ann_{Z}(v)
, we have
\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p})\leq\alpha
. As
\rho(M)
is a finitely generated
Z
-module, this is equivalent to
\kappa(\rho(M))\leq\alpha
by Lemma
3.7.6
. ∎

We can now establish the stability of the Krull-dimension filtration on
\mathcal{C}
under certain fairly restrictive hypotheses on
\Xi
and
\mathcal{R}
.

Proposition 3.7.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is
k
-linear, that
Z
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra, and that there is an integer
d
such that
\Ext^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(P,.)|_{\mathcal{A}}=0
for any
j>d
.
Suppose further that
\pr_{2}:\mathcal{R}\to\Xi
is quasi-finite
. Then
\mathcal{L}_{W_{\alpha}}^{\mathcal{C}}
is a stable localising subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
for all
\alpha
.

Proof.

By assumption, the
k
-algebra
Z\otimes_{k}Z
is also finitely generated, so it is noetherian by Hilbert’s Basis Theorem. By Thm.
3.7.1
and Remark
3.7.5
, it is enough to show that

\pr_{2}(\pr_{1}^{-1}(W_{\alpha}))\subseteq W_{\alpha}.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}:=\Xi\backslash W_{\alpha}
be the complement of
W_{\alpha}
in
\Xi
; then it is enough to show that

\pr_{1}(\pr_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}))\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}.

Let
P\in\pr_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}_{\alpha})
and set
\mathfrak{p}_{i}:=\pr_{i}(P)
for
i=1,2
. Then
\mathfrak{p}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
, so
\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p}_{2})\leq\alpha
. The morphisms
\pr_{1}
and
\pr_{2}
induce the following diagram of residue fields:

k(\mathfrak{p}_{1})\hookrightarrow k(P)\hookleftarrow k(\mathfrak{p}_{2}).

Since
\pr_{2}:\mathcal{R}\to\Xi
is quasi-finite,
k(P)
is a finite dimensional
k(\mathfrak{p}_{2})
-vector space. Hence

\tr.\deg_{k}(k(\mathfrak{p}_{1}))\leq\tr.\deg_{k}(k(P))=\tr.\deg_{k}(k(\mathfrak{p}_{2})).

Since
Z
is a finitely generated
k
-algebra, we can apply a version of the Noether Normalization Theorem — see
[
Mat
]
Thm. 5.6 — to deduce that

\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p}_{i})=\tr.\deg_{k}(Z/\mathfrak{p}_{i})=\tr.\deg_{k}(k(\mathfrak{p}_{i}))\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,2.

Hence
\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p}_{1})\leq\kappa(Z/\mathfrak{p}_{2})\leq\alpha
, which means that
\mathfrak{p}_{1}=\pr_{1}(P)\in\mathcal{S}_{\alpha}
. ∎

4.
The quotient space for
\Mod_{k}(SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p}))

4.1.
The pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra and its centre

4.1.1.
The set-up

Let
\mathfrak{F}
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with ring of integers
\mathfrak{O}
, maximal ideal
\mathfrak{M}
and residue field
\mathfrak{f}
. We fix a choice of generator
\pi
of
\mathfrak{M}
. Necessarily
\mathfrak{f}\cong\mathbb{F}_{q}
for some power
q
of
p
.
We assume that our ground field
k
contains
\mathfrak{f}
.

4.1.2.
Groups

Let
G=\SL_{2}(\mathfrak{F})
and
K=\SL_{2}(\mathfrak{O})
, and define the
pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup

I:=\begin{pmatrix}1+\mathfrak{M}&\mathfrak{O}\\
\mathfrak{M}&1+\mathfrak{M}\end{pmatrix}\cap G.

Let
T=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{F}^{\times}&0\\
0&\mathfrak{F}^{\times}\end{pmatrix}\cap G
be the subgroup of diagonal matrices in
G
and let
T^{0}:=T\cap K
. The projection onto the
(1,1)
-entry gives group isomorphisms
T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{F}^{\times}
and
T^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathfrak{O}^{\times}
. Hence
T/T^{0}
is an infinite cyclic group. The normaliser
N_{G}(T)
of
T
in
G
is generated by
T
and
s_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}
, and
N_{G}(T)
is isomorphic to the semi-direct product
T\rtimes\langle s_{0}\rangle
, where
s_{0}
acts on
T
by inversion. This action preserves the subgroup
T_{0}
, so that
T_{0}
is normal in
N_{G}(T)
. Then we can form the
affine Weyl group

W:=N_{G}(T)/T_{0}
which is isomorphic to the infinite dihedral group, generated by the
T_{0}
-cosets of
s_{0}
and
s_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\pi^{-1}\\
\pi&0\end{pmatrix}
. The subgroup
T^{1}:=T\cap I
is also normal in
N_{G}(T)
and this gives the
extended Weyl group

\widetilde{W}:=N_{G}(T)/T^{1}
. Let
\Omega:=T^{0}/T^{1}
; then
\Omega\cong\mathfrak{f}^{\times}
and we have the short exact sequence

1\to\Omega\to\widetilde{W}\to W\to 1.

Note that
\Omega
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
and is therefore a finite cyclic group of order
q-1
.

4.1.3.
The pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra

Let
\widehat{\Omega}:=\Hom(\Omega,k^{\times})
be the group of
k
-linear characters of
\Omega
. Since
k
contains the residue field
\mathfrak{f}
of
\mathfrak{F}
by assumption,
\widehat{\Omega}
is a cyclic group of order
q-1
, generated by the
identity character

\id:\Omega\to k^{\times},\quad\begin{pmatrix}a&0\\
0&a^{-1}\end{pmatrix}T^{1}\mapsto a+\mathfrak{M}\in\mathfrak{f}^{\times}\hookrightarrow k^{\times}.

In other words, we have
\widehat{\Omega}=\{\id^{j}:0\leq j\leq q-2\}
where
\id^{0}=\mathbb{1}
is the
trivial character
. The group algebra
k[\Omega]
contains
q-1
primitive idempotents

(9)

e_{\lambda}:=-\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\lambda(\omega)^{-1}\omega,\quad\quad\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}

that form a basis for
k[\Omega]
as a
k
-vector space. These idempotents are pairwise orthogonal:

(10)

e_{\lambda}e_{\mu}=\delta_{\lambda\mu}e_{\lambda}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\lambda,\mu\in\widehat{\Omega}.

We have the compactly induced
G
-representation

\ind_{I}^{G}(k):=k[G/I]=k[G]\underset{k[I]}{\otimes}{}k

from the trivial representation
k
of
I
. It is a smooth
k
-linear representation of
G
, so
k[G/I]
is an object in
\Mod_{k}(G)
. The
pro-
p
Iwahori Hecke algebra
is the opposite ring of the endomorphism ring of this representation:

H:=\End_{\Mod_{k}(G)}(k[G/I])^{\op}.

The following facts are known about
H
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

a)

The double cosets
\{\tau_{w}:=IwI:w\in\widetilde{W}\}
form a
k
-basis for
H
.

b)

The map
k[\Omega]\to H
given by
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\tau_{\omega}
is an injection of
k
-algebras.

c)

The elements
\tau_{0}:=\tau_{s_{0}}
and
\tau_{1}:=\tau_{s_{1}}
generate
H
as a
k
-algebra together with
k[\Omega]
.

d)

We have
\tau_{i}e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{i}
for
i=0,1
and any
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

e)

We have the
quadratic relations

\tau_{i}^{2}=-\tau_{i}e_{\mathbb{1}}
for
i=0,1
.

We will never use the notation
\tau_{w}
when
w
is the identity element of
\widetilde{W}
, because this is equal to the identity element in
H
. Therefore writing
\tau_{1}=\tau_{s_{1}}
should not lead to any confusion.

4.1.4.
The centre of
H

The centre
Z=Z(H)
of this
k
-algebra can be described explicitly. The following statement is well-known, but see also
[
OS18
]

\lx@sectionsign
3.2.2.

Lemma 4.1.2
.

The element

\zeta:=(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}=(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})+\tau_{0}\tau_{1}

generates a polynomial ring
k[\zeta]
inside
Z(H)
.

Definition 4.1.3
.

For each
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
, we define the following elements of
H
:

X_{\lambda}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cll}e_{\lambda}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}&:&\lambda\neq\mathbb{1}\\
e_{\mathbb{1}}\zeta&:&\lambda=\mathbb{1}.\end{array}\right.

Again, we omit the proof of the following well-known result.

Lemma 4.1.4
.

Let
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

a)

X_{\lambda}
is central in
H
.

b)

If
\lambda\neq\lambda^{-1}
, then
(e_{\lambda}+e_{\lambda^{-1}})\zeta=X_{\lambda}+X_{\lambda^{-1}}
.

c)

If
\lambda=\lambda^{-1}
, then
e_{\lambda}\zeta=X_{\lambda}
.

d)

We have
\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}X_{\alpha}=\zeta
.

We will now describe
Z
completely as a
k
-algebra with relations and generators. Suppose that
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
satisfies
\lambda\neq\lambda^{-1}
; then using Lemma
4.1.1
, we see that the idempotent
e_{\lambda}+e_{\lambda^{-1}}
is central in
H
. It is known, furthermore, that in this case

X_{\lambda}\cdot X_{\lambda^{-1}}=0.

Note that precisely two characters
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
satisfy
\lambda=\lambda^{-1}
, namely the trivial character
1=\id^{0}
and “the quadratic character”
\id^{\frac{q-1}{2}}
. All other elements of
\widehat{\Omega}
are not equal to their inverse. This gives us the decomposition of
Z
as a direct product of non-unital subrings:

(11)

Z=e_{\mathbb{1}}Z\quad\oplus\quad e_{\id^{\frac{q-1}{2}}}Z\quad\oplus\quad\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\frac{q-3}{2}}(e_{\id^{j}}+e_{\id^{-j}})Z

From
\lx@sectionsign 3.2
of
[
OS18
]
we deduce the following

Proposition 4.1.5
.

Let
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

a)

Suppose that
\lambda=\lambda^{-1}
. Then the
k
-algebra homomorphism

k[x]\to e_{\lambda}Z,\quad 1\mapsto e_{\lambda},\quad x\mapsto X_{\lambda}

is an isomorphism.

b)

Suppose that
\lambda\neq\lambda^{-1}
. Then the
k
-algebra homomorphism

b_{\lambda}:\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}\to(e_{\lambda}+e_{\lambda^{-1}})Z,\quad 1\mapsto e_{\lambda}+e_{\lambda^{-1}},\quad x\mapsto X_{\lambda},\quad y\mapsto X_{\lambda^{-1}}

is an isomorphism.

4.2.
The normalisation of
\Spec(Z)

4.2.1.
Categorical quotients of schemes

Let
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}
be two morphisms of schemes. Recall that a morphism of schemes
\psi:X\to Y
is said to be a
categorical quotient of
X
by
\mathcal{R}
if it is a coequaliser of this diagram in the category of schemes.

Given a morphism of locally ringed spaces
f:X\to Y
, we write
|f|:|X|\to|Y|
for the underlying continuous map of topological spaces.

Let
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{Y}
be a diagram of schemes such that
\psi f=\psi g
, and let
\{Y_{i}:i\in I\}
be an open covering of
Y
. For each
i\in I
, form the fibre products
X_{i}:=X\times_{Y}Y_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i}:=\mathcal{R}\times_{Y}Y_{i}
, and consider the diagram
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{g_{i}}
\textstyle{X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{i}}
\textstyle{Y_{i}}
where
f_{i},g_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
are the pullbacks of
f,g
and
\psi
, respectively.

We denote the category of schemes by
\Sch
, and the category of locally commutatively ringed topological spaces by
\LRS
.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

Let
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{Y}
be a diagram of schemes such that
\psi f=\psi g
, and let
\{Y_{i}:i\in I\}
be an open covering of
Y
. Suppose that

a)

\textstyle{|\mathcal{R}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|f|}
\scriptstyle{|g|}
\textstyle{|X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\psi|}
\textstyle{|Y|}
is a coequaliser diagram of topological spaces,

b)

Y_{i}
,
X_{i}
and
\mathcal{R}_{i}
is affine for all
i\in I
, and

c)

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(Y_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{i}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{g_{i}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}_{i})}
is an equaliser diagram of commutative rings for all
i\in I
.

Then
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{Y}
is a coequaliser in
\LRS

3
3

3

Note that the functor from schemes to LRS does not respect coequalisers (see
[
LMB
]
for an example).
, and
\psi
is a categorical quotient of
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X}
.

Proof.

By the construction of colimits in
\LRS
(
[
DG
]
Prop. I.1.1.6) the first statement is equivalent to the following conditions:

–

\textstyle{|\mathcal{R}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|f|}
\scriptstyle{|g|}
\textstyle{|X|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\psi|}
\textstyle{|Y|}
is a coequaliser diagram of topological spaces;

–

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\psi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{(\psi f)_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}}=(\psi g)_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{R}}}
is an equaliser diagram of sheaves of commutative rings on
Y
.

The second condition can be checked after restriction to the open subschemes
Y_{i}
. By the second assumption these and there preimages in
X
and
\mathcal{R}
all are affine. Hence these restricted sheaves are determined by there global sections in these affine schemes. Therefore this reduces to the third assumption.

Since
\Sch
is a full subcategory of
\LRS
, the second statement follows from the first.
∎

We will now give an example of Lemma
4.2.1
in action, which will come in useful later. Consider the union of two crossing affine lines:

\mathcal{X}:=\Spec\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}\hskip 2.84544pt.

We will ‘separate’ the two crossing affine lines to form a disjoint union of two affine lines

\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:=\Spec\left(k[x]\times k[y]\right)

and then re-glue them together to form
\mathcal{X}
as a quotient of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
by a certain relation.

To be more precise, note that
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of
\Spec k[x]
and
\Spec k[y]
. Let
\mathcal{S}:=\Spec k=\{s\}
be a point, let
a:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be the inclusion of the origin into the first affine line, and let
b:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
be the inclusion of the origin into the second affine line.

Consider the
k
-algebra homomorphism

\varphi:\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}\to k[x]\times k[y],\quad\quad\varphi\left(\overline{f(x,y)}\right)=(f(x,0),f(0,y))\quad\mbox{for any}\quad f(x,y)\in k[x,y],

and let
\theta:=\Spec(\varphi):\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
be the corresponding morphism of affine schemes.

Proposition 4.2.2
.

\theta:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is a categorical quotient of
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We will apply Lemma
4.2.1
. The co-morphism
a^{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S})
(respectively,
b^{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S})
) is equal to the composition of the first projection from
k[x]\times k[y]
onto
k[x]
(respectively, the second projection
k[x]\times k[y]
onto
k[y]
) with the evaluation-at-zero map
k[x]\to k
,
f(x)\mapsto f(0)
(respectively,
k[y]\to k
,
f(y)\mapsto f(0)
). The
k
-algebra homomorphisms
a^{\sharp}\varphi
and
b^{\sharp}\varphi
both kill
\overline{x}
and
\overline{y}
; since these two elements generate
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
as a
k
-algebra, we see that
a^{\sharp}\varphi=b^{\sharp}\varphi
. This gives us a complex of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
-modules

(12)

0\to\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad k[x]\times k[y]\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a^{\sharp}-b^{\sharp}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad k.

Since
\langle x\rangle\cap\langle y\rangle=\langle xy\rangle
in
k[x,y]
, the
k
-algebra homomorphism
\varphi
is injective. If
u=(f(x),g(y))\in k[x]\times k[y]
is such that
a^{\sharp}(u)=b^{\sharp}(u)
, then
f(0)=g(0)
, and then

\varphi\left(\overline{f(x)+g(y)}\right)=(f(x),f(0))+(g(0),g(y))=(g(0),f(0))+(f(x),f(y))=\varphi\left(\overline{f(0)}\right)+u

shows that
\ker(a^{\sharp}-b^{\sharp})=\mathrm{Im}(\varphi)
. So, the complex
(\ref{eq: phiab})
is in fact exact, and this verifies condition c) of Lemma
4.2.1
.

We have already observed that
\overline{x},\overline{y}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
both kill
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S})=k
when we consider
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{S})
as an
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
-module via
a^{\sharp}\varphi=b^{\sharp}\varphi
. So it is also killed by
\overline{x}+\overline{y}
. Therefore if we localise the exact sequence (
12
) at the element
\overline{x}+\overline{y}\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{X})
, then we obtain the isomorphism

\left(\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}\right)\left[\frac{1}{\overline{x}+\overline{y}}\right]\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad k[x,x^{-1}]\times k[y,y^{-1}].

Let
O:=\langle\overline{x},\overline{y}\rangle\in\mathcal{X}
be the intersection point of the two crossing affine lines. Then the restriction of
\theta=\Spec(\varphi)
to
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}-\{a(s),b(s)\}=\Spec\left(k[x,x^{-1}]\times k[y,y^{-1}]\right)
is an isomorphism onto
\mathcal{X}\backslash\{O\}
. Since
\theta
maps both origins in
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}
to
O\in\mathcal{X}
, we see that
|\theta|
is surjective, and that if
p,q\in|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|
satisfy
|\theta|(p)=|\theta|(q)
but
p\neq q
, then necessarily
p,q\in\{a(s),b(s)\}
. Thus
|\mathcal{X}|
is obtained as a set by identifying
a(s)
with
b(s)
in
|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|
.

Finally, both
|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|
and
|\mathcal{X}|
carry the cofinite Zariski topologies, and the quotient topology on
|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|/(a(s)\sim b(s))
is still the cofinite topology. Hence
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.55557pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.55557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\mathcal{S}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.19925pt\raise 9.08334pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|a|}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.55557pt\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.60974pt\raise-9.08334pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{|b|}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.55557pt\raise-2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 32.55557pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{|\mathcal{X}^{\prime}|}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\to|\mathcal{X}|
is a coequaliser diagram of topological spaces, as required for condition a) of Lemma
4.2.1
.
∎

4.2.2.
The normalisation of
\Spec(Z)

We return to the notation of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: CentOfH}
.

Definition 4.2.3
.

a)

Let
\Xi=\Spec(Z)
.

b)

Let
Z^{\prime}:=k[\Omega][t]
where
t
is a formal variable, and let
\Xi^{\prime}:=\Spec Z^{\prime}
.

c)

Let
\varphi:Z\to Z^{\prime}
be the
k
-algebra homomorphism defined by

\varphi(X_{\lambda})=e_{\lambda}t\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}.

d)

Let
\theta:\Xi^{\prime}\to\Xi
be defined by
\theta=\Spec(\varphi)
.

Note that
\Xi^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of
|\widehat{\Omega}|=q-1
affine lines
\Xi^{\prime}_{\lambda}:=\Spec k[t_{\lambda}]
, where
t_{\lambda}:=e_{\lambda}t\in Z^{\prime}
. We call
\Xi^{\prime}_{\lambda}
the
\lambda
-component
of
\Xi^{\prime}
. We denote the image of the origin
\langle t_{\lambda}\rangle\in\Xi^{\prime}_{\lambda}
inside
\Xi^{\prime}
by
O_{\lambda}
. Equivalently,
O_{\lambda}
is the maximal ideal
\langle 1-e_{\lambda},t\rangle
of
Z^{\prime}
.

Definition 4.2.4
.

a)

Let
\Xi_{\sing}:=\{s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{\frac{q-3}{2}}\}
be the disjoint union of
\frac{q-3}{2}
copies of
\Spec k
.

b)

Define
a:\Xi_{\sing}\to\Xi^{\prime}
and
b:\Xi_{\sing}\to\Xi^{\prime}
to be the closed embeddings, given by

a(s_{j})=O_{\id^{j}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad b(s_{j})=O_{\id^{-j}}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad j=1,\cdots,\frac{q-3}{2}.

The notation is intended to indicate that
\Xi_{\sing}
is a copy of the singular locus of the scheme
\Xi
: indeed, in view of the decomposition (
11
) and Prop.
4.1.5
.b,
\Xi
contains
\frac{q-3}{2}
copies of the pair of crossing lines
\Spec\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle xy\rangle}
, so the singular locus of
\Xi
consists of
\frac{q-3}{2}
closed points.

Proposition 4.2.5
.

\theta:\Xi^{\prime}\to\Xi
is a categorical quotient of
\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The decomposition of
Z=\mathcal{O}(\Xi)
as a direct product of non-unital subrings given in (
11
) means that
\Xi
decomposes into the disjoint union of connected components

(13)

\Xi=\Xi_{0}\quad\cup\quad\Xi_{\frac{q-1}{2}}\quad\cup\quad\coprod\limits_{j=1}^{\frac{q-3}{2}}\Xi_{j,-j}

where these connected components are defined by

\Xi_{0}:=\Spec e_{\mathbb{1}}Z,\quad\Xi_{\frac{q-1}{2}}:=\Spec e_{\id^{\frac{q-1}{2}}}Z,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Xi_{j,-j}:=\Spec(e_{\id^{j}}+e_{\id^{-j}})Z

for all
j=1,\cdots,\frac{q-3}{2}
. On the other hand, we can write

(14)

\Xi^{\prime}=\Xi_{\mathbb{1}}\quad\cup\quad\Xi_{\id^{\frac{q-1}{2}}}\quad\cup\quad\coprod\limits_{j=1}^{\frac{q-3}{2}}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{j}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{-j}}).

Fix
j=1,\cdots,\frac{q-3}{2}
. The morphism
\theta
maps
(\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{j}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{-j}})
onto
\Xi_{j,-j}
; let
\theta_{j}
denote the restriction of
\theta
to
(\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{j}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{-j}})
. Then we have the commutative diagram of affine schemes

(15)

\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{j}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{-j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{j}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{j,-j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{j}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

where
\theta_{0}:\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{X}
is the morphism that was denoted
\theta
in Prop.
4.2.2
,
\beta_{j}:=\Spec(b_{\id^{j}})
is the isomorphism of affine schemes defined by the isomorphism
b_{\id^{j}}
from Prop.
4.1.5
.b, and
\alpha_{j}
is the isomorphism of affine schemes defined by the
k
-algebra isomorphism

\alpha_{j}^{\sharp}:k[x]\times k[y]\quad\longrightarrow k[t_{\id^{j}}]\times k[t_{\id^{-j}}]

given by
\alpha_{j}^{\sharp}(x)=t_{\id^{j}}
and
\alpha_{j}^{\sharp}(y)=t_{\id^{-j}}
. This gives us the commutative diagram of schemes

(16)

\textstyle{\{s_{j}\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{j}}
\scriptstyle{b_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{j}}\cup\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{-j}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{j}}
\textstyle{\Xi_{j,-j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{b_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{0}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}

where
a_{0}
and
b_{0}
are the morphisms that were denoted
a
and
b
in the statement of Prop.
4.2.2
. Now we can apply Prop.
4.2.2
to see that
\theta_{j}
is a coequaliser of
a_{j},b_{j}
.

Note that the morphism
\theta
sends
\Xi^{\prime}_{\mathbb{1}}
(respectively,
\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{\frac{q-1}{2}}}
) isomorphically onto
\Xi_{0}
(respectively,
\Xi^{\prime}_{\frac{q-1}{2}}
). Taking coproducts of the diagrams (
16
) over
j=1,\cdots,\frac{q-3}{2}
and bearing in mind the decompositions
(\ref{eq: XiDecomp})
and
(\ref{eq: XiDashDecomp})
now gives the result in view of Lemma
A.1.3
.
∎

4.2.3.
The non-singular locus of
\Xi

Let
V(t)
denote the set of
q-1
closed points in
\Xi^{\prime}
consisting of the disjoint union of the origins in the
q-1
affine lines
\Xi^{\prime}_{\lambda}=\Spec k[t_{\lambda}]
.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

The morphism
\theta
restricts to an isomorphism

\mathring{\theta}:\Xi^{\prime}-V(t)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\Xi-V(\zeta).

Proof.

Let
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
and write
\lambda=\id^{j}
for some
j=0,\cdots,q-2
. Suppose first that
j\neq 0
and
j\neq\frac{q-1}{2}
. Then the image of
\zeta
in
\mathcal{O}(\Xi_{\lambda,\lambda^{-1}})
under the restriction map
Z=\mathcal{O}(\Xi)\to\mathcal{O}(\Xi_{j,-j})
is
(e_{\lambda}+e_{\lambda^{-1}})\zeta
, which is equal to
X_{\lambda}+X_{\lambda^{-1}}
by Lemma
4.1.4
.b. We see that

\beta_{j}(V(\zeta)\cap\Xi_{j,-j})
is the origin
O
in the pair of crossing lines
\mathcal{X}
. Likewise, if
j=0
or
j=\frac{q-1}{2}
, Lemma
4.1.4
.c implies that
V(\zeta)\cap\Xi_{\frac{q-1}{2}}=V(e_{\lambda}\zeta)
is the origin in the affine line
\Xi_{j}
. So,
V(\zeta)\subset\Xi
consists of precisely
\frac{q-3}{2}+2=\frac{q+1}{2}
closed points and contains the singular locus of
\Xi
, whereas
V(t)
consists of
|\Spec k[\Omega]|=|\widehat{\Omega}|=q-1
closed points in
\Xi^{\prime}
.

The result now follows from the commutative diagram (
15
), together with the observation we made in the proof of Prop.
4.2.2
that
\theta_{0}
restricts to an isomorphism between
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\backslash\{a_{0}(s),b_{0}(s)\}
and
\mathcal{X}\backslash\{O\}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2.7
.

There is a
k
-algebra isomorphism

\mathring{\theta}^{\sharp}:Z_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(\Xi-V(\zeta))\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\quad\mathcal{O}(\Xi-V(t))=k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]

which sends
\zeta
to
t
.

Of course,
\mathring{\theta}^{\sharp}
restricts to the basic map
\varphi:Z\to Z^{\prime}
on
Z
from Def.
4.2.3
.c.

Definition 4.2.8
.

For each
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
we define
\epsilon_{\lambda}:=(\mathring{\theta}^{\sharp})^{-1}(e_{\lambda})
.

The family
\{\epsilon_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}\}
form a complete set of primitive idempotents in
Z_{\zeta}
.

Lemma 4.2.9
.

We have
\epsilon_{\lambda}=\frac{X_{\lambda}}{\zeta}
for all
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

Proof.

Since
\zeta
is a unit in
Z_{\zeta}
, it’s enough to show
\epsilon_{\lambda}\zeta^{2}=X_{\lambda}\zeta
. Since
\mathring{\theta}^{\sharp}
is a ring isomorphism that restricts to
\varphi
on
Z
, it is enough to show that the equation
e_{\lambda}\varphi(\zeta)^{2}=\varphi(X_{\lambda}\zeta)
holds in
k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
. However, using Lemma
4.1.4
.d together with Def.
4.2.3
.c, we have

(17)

\varphi(\zeta)=\varphi\left(\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}X_{\alpha}\right)=\left(\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}e_{\alpha}t\right)=t

and therefore
e_{\lambda}\varphi(\zeta)^{2}=e_{\lambda}t^{2}=(e_{\lambda}t)t=\varphi(X_{\lambda})\varphi(\zeta)=\varphi(X_{\lambda}\zeta)
as required.
∎

Corollary 4.2.10
.

As a
k[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]
-module,
Z_{\zeta}
decomposes as follows:

Z_{\zeta}=\bigoplus\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}\epsilon_{\lambda}k[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}].

Proof.

The isomorphism
\mathring{\theta}^{\sharp}:Z_{\zeta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
given by Lemma
4.2.6
sends
\zeta
to
t
by equation (
17
), and it sends
\epsilon_{\lambda}\in Z_{\zeta}
to
e_{\lambda}\in k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
for each
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
. Since
\{e_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}\}
forms a
k[t,t^{-1}]
-module basis for
k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
, it follows that
\{\epsilon_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}\}
forms a
k[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]
-module basis for
Z_{\zeta}
.∎

4.3.
Bimodule annihilators

4.3.1.
Some generalities about annihilators and bimodules

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra, and let
\phi:A\to A
be a
k
-algebra automorphism. Let
M
be an
A
-bimodule. We write
M_{\phi}
to denote the
(A,A)
-bimodule
M
, with action given by

a\cdot m\cdot b=a\hskip 2.84544ptm\hskip 2.84544pt\phi(b)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a,b\in A,m\in M

and we call
M_{\phi}
the
right
\phi
-twist of
M
.

Thus, the left
A
-action on
M_{\phi}
is the usual one, whereas the right
A
-action is twisted by
\phi
.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra, let
M
be an
(A,A)
-bimodule and let
\phi:A\to A
be a
k
-algebra automorphism. Then

\Ann_{A\otimes_{k}A}(M_{\phi})=(1\otimes\phi^{-1})(\Ann_{A\otimes_{k}A}(M)).

Proof.

Let
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in A\otimes_{k}A
. Then
x\cdot M_{\phi}=0
if and only if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}m\phi(b_{i})=0
for all
m\in M
. This is equivalent to
(1\otimes\phi)(x)\cdot M=0
and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Let
A
be a
k
-algebra with centre
Z
and let
\mult_{Z}:Z\otimes_{k}Z\to Z
be the multiplication map. Then

\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(A)=\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(Z)=\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(1)=\ker\mult_{Z}.

Proof.

For an element
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in Z\otimes_{k}Z
, we have
x\cdot 1=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}=\mult_{Z}(x)
. This shows the last equality. Since
A\supseteq Z\ni 1
, we have
\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(A)\subseteq\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(Z)\subseteq\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(1)=\ker\mult_{Z}
by the above. Let
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in\ker\mult_{Z}
and let
c\in A
. Then because
a_{i},b_{i}
are central in
A
, we have
x\cdot c=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}cb_{i}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}\right)c=\mult_{Z}(x)c=0
. Hence
\ker\mult_{Z}\subseteq\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(A)
.∎

Lemma 4.3.4
.

Let
A
be a commutative
k
-algebra and let
\mult_{A}:A\otimes_{k}A\to A
be the multiplication map.

a)

\ker\mult_{A}
is generated as an ideal in
A\otimes_{k}A
by
\{a\otimes 1-1\otimes a:a\in A\}
.

b)

Suppose that
a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}
generate
A
as a
k
-algebra. Then
\{a_{i}\otimes 1-1\otimes a_{i}:i=1,\cdots,m\}
generates
\ker\mult_{A}
as an ideal in
A\otimes_{k}A
.

Proof.

a) Certainly
\mult_{A}
kills
a\otimes 1-1\otimes a
for all
a\in A
. Conversely, if
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\otimes b_{i}\in A\otimes A
is such that
\mult_{A}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}b_{i}=0
, then

x=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}\otimes b_{i}-1\otimes a_{i}b_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}\otimes 1-1\otimes a_{i})(1\otimes b_{i})

lies in the ideal in
A\otimes A
generated by
\{a\otimes 1-1\otimes a:a\in A\}
.

b) Write
\partial(a)=a\otimes 1-1\otimes a
for
a\in A
. Then for all
a,b\in A
we have

\partial(ab)=ab\otimes 1-1\otimes ab=(a\otimes 1-1\otimes a)(b\otimes 1)+(1\otimes a)(b\otimes 1-1\otimes b)=\partial(a)(b\otimes 1)+(1\otimes a)\partial(b).

Hence the ideal generated by
\{\partial(a_{i}):i=1,\cdots,m\}
contains
\partial(a_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots a_{m}^{k_{m}})
for all non-negative integers
k_{1},\cdots,k_{m}
. Since
a\mapsto\partial(a)
is
k
-linear, and since
A=k[a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}]
by assumption, this ideal contains
\partial(a)
for all
a\in A
. Hence it contains
\ker\mult_{A}
by part a). The reverse inclusion is clear because
\mult_{A}(\partial(a_{i}))=0
for all
i
.
∎

4.3.2.
The bimodule
\left(\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}}\oplus\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}}\right)[\kappa,z,\mu]

Recall the canonical involution
\iota:H\to H
from
[
OS22
]
, §2.4.3: it is a self-inverse
k
-algebra automorphism of
H
that fixes the subalgebra
k[\Omega]
pointwise, and satisfies

\iota(\tau_{0})=-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\iota(\tau_{1})=-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{1}.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

\iota
fixes the centre
Z
of
H
pointwise.

Proof.

The decomposition (
11
) together with Prop.
4.1.5
implies that the central elements
\{X_{\lambda}:\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}\}
generate
Z
as a
k
-algebra. Since
\iota
is a
k[\Omega]
-linear involution, it therefore suffices to check that
\iota
fixes each
X_{\lambda}
. Now if
\lambda\neq\mathbb{1}
, then since
e_{\mathbb{1}}e_{\lambda}=e_{\mathbb{1}}e_{\lambda^{-1}}=0
by (
10
), we compute

\iota(X_{\lambda})=\iota(e_{\lambda}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})=e_{\lambda}(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{0})(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{1})+e_{\lambda^{-1}}(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{1})(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{0})=X_{\lambda}.

On the other hand, since
\zeta=(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}
and since
\iota
fixes
e_{\mathbb{1}}\in k[\Omega]
, we have

\iota(X_{\mathbb{1}})=e_{\mathbb{1}}\iota(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})\iota(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})+\iota(\tau_{1})\iota(\tau_{0})=e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{1})(-e_{\mathbb{1}}-\tau_{0})=e_{\mathbb{1}}\zeta=X_{\mathbb{1}},

where we used the second formula for
\zeta
from Lemma
4.1.2
.
∎

Let
\kappa:H\to R
be a homomorphism of
k
-algebras, let
z\in Z(R)
and let
\mu\in\widehat{\Omega}
. Then in
[
OS22
]
, §2.4.7, Ollivier and Schneider explain how to use these parameters to endow the left
R
-module
R\oplus R
with the structure of a right
H
-module, in fact forming an
(R,H)
-bimodule, that they denote
(R\oplus R)[\kappa,z,\mu]
.
The left
R
-action on
(R\oplus R)[\kappa,z,\mu]
is the obvious one, but
H
acts from the right as follows:

(r_{1},r_{2})h:=(r_{1},r_{2})\kappa_{2}(h)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad r_{1},r_{2}\in R,h\in H.

Here
\kappa_{2}:H\to M_{2}(H_{\zeta})
is the explicit
k
-algebra homomorphism from
[
OS22
]
§2.4.7. In this generality, we will explicitly compute the right action of
Z
on this bimodule.

Lemma 4.3.6
.

We have
\kappa_{2}(X_{\alpha})=z^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}
for all
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
OS22
]
§2.4.7 that
\kappa_{2}(\tau_{\omega})=M_{\omega}=\begin{pmatrix}\mu^{-1}(\omega)\kappa(\tau_{\omega})&0\\
0&\mu(\omega)\kappa(\tau_{\omega})\end{pmatrix}
. Then

(18)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\kappa_{2}(e_{\alpha})&=&-\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\alpha^{-1}(\omega)\kappa_{2}(\omega)=-\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\alpha^{-1}(\omega)M_{\omega}\\
&=&\begin{pmatrix}-\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\alpha^{-1}(\omega)\mu^{-1}(\omega)\kappa(\tau_{\omega})&0\\
0&-\sum\limits_{\omega\in\Omega}\alpha^{-1}(\omega)\mu(\omega)\kappa(\tau_{\omega})\end{pmatrix}\\
&=&\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}\end{array}

using (
9
). On the other hand, we have

\kappa_{2}(\tau_{0})=M_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}-\kappa(e_{\mu})&0\\
z\kappa(\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\kappa_{2}(\tau_{1})=M_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&z\kappa(\tau_{0})\\
0&-\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}.

Then using (
18
) together with (
10
), we compute

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(e_{\alpha}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\kappa(e_{\mu})&0\\
z\kappa(\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&z\kappa(\tau_{0})\\
0&-\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&-z\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu}e_{\mu}\tau_{0})\\
0&z^{2}\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\delta_{\alpha\mathbb{1}}z\kappa(e_{\mu}\tau_{0})\\
0&z^{2}\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}(e_{\alpha^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&z\kappa(\tau_{0})\\
0&-\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\kappa(e_{\mu})&0\\
z\kappa(\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}z^{2}\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
-z\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu^{-1}}e_{\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}z^{2}\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
-z\delta_{\alpha\mathbb{1}}\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}.

Suppose first that
\alpha\neq\mathbb{1}
so that
X_{\alpha}=e_{\alpha}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\alpha^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}
. Then
\delta_{\alpha\mathbb{1}}=0
, and adding the last two displayed equations together gives the result in this case.

Suppose now that
\alpha=\mathbb{1}
so that
X_{\mathbb{1}}=e_{\mathbb{1}}\zeta=e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1)
. Adding the above two equations in this case gives

(19)

\kappa_{2}(e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})=z^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&-z\kappa(e_{\mu}\tau_{0})\\
-z\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}.

Since
\kappa_{2}(e_{\mathbb{1}})=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\mu})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}
and
\kappa_{2}(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1)=\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(1-e_{\mu})&z\kappa(\tau_{0})\\
z\kappa(\tau_{1})&\kappa(1-e_{\mu^{-1}})\end{pmatrix}
, we have

(20)

\kappa_{2}(e_{\mathbb{1}}(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1))=\begin{pmatrix}0&z\kappa(e_{\mu}\tau_{0})\\
z\kappa(e_{\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}.

Adding together
(\ref{eq: exyeyx})
and
(\ref{eq: e1x+y+1})
finishes the calculation.
∎

Corollary 4.3.7
.

\kappa_{2}(\zeta)=z^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
0&\kappa(\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}
.

Proof.

Note that
\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}e_{\lambda}=1
. Now apply Lemma
4.3.6
and Lemma
4.1.4
.d.
∎

Now we specialise slightly, and take
R
to be the central localization
H_{\zeta}
of
H
at
\zeta
. Let
\kappa
be the composition of
\iota:H\to H
with the inclusion
H\hookrightarrow H_{\zeta}
and set

z:=-\tau_{\omega_{-1}}\zeta^{-1}\in H_{\zeta}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mu:=\id^{2}.

Definition 4.3.8
.

Let
B:=\left(\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}}\oplus\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}}\right)[\kappa,z,\mu]
, an
(H_{\zeta},H)
-bimodule.

We will now calculate the right action of
Z
on this bimodule.

Proposition 4.3.9
.

a)

We have
v\zeta=\zeta^{-1}v
for all
v\in B
.

b)

Let
\mathbf{1}:=(\overline{1},\overline{1})\in B
. Then
\mathbf{1}\epsilon_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\mathbf{1}
for all
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
.

Proof.

a) We have
z=-\tau_{\omega_{-1}}\zeta^{-1}
so
z^{2}=\zeta^{-2}
because
\zeta
is central and
(\omega_{-1})^{2}=\omega_{1}=1
. Note that
\kappa(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})=\iota(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})=(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})=\zeta-\tau_{1}\tau_{0}\equiv\zeta\mod H\tau_{0}
, and similarly
\kappa(\tau_{1}\tau_{0})=\zeta-\tau_{0}\tau_{1}\equiv\zeta\mod H\tau_{1}
in view of Lemma
4.1.2
. Writing
v=(\overline{v_{1}},\overline{v_{2}})
, we apply Cor.
4.3.7
:

(\overline{v_{1}},\overline{v_{2}})\zeta=(\overline{v_{1}},\overline{v_{2}})\kappa_{2}(\zeta)=(\overline{v_{1}},\overline{v_{2}})\zeta^{-2}\begin{pmatrix}\zeta-\tau_{1}\tau_{0}&0\\
0&\zeta-\tau_{0}\tau_{1}\end{pmatrix}=(\overline{v_{1}\zeta^{-1}},\overline{v_{2}\zeta^{-1}})=\zeta^{-1}(\overline{v_{1}},\overline{v_{2}}).

b) Recall that
\epsilon_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha}/\zeta
by Lemma
4.2.9
. We will show that

(21)

\zeta^{2}\mathbf{1}X_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\mathbf{1}.

Applying Lemma
4.3.6
, we calculate

(22)

(\overline{1},\overline{1})\kappa_{2}(X_{\alpha})=(\overline{1},\overline{1})z^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\kappa(e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&0\\
0&\kappa(e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})\end{pmatrix}=\zeta^{-2}\left(\overline{e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\iota(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})},\overline{e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\iota(\tau_{1}\tau_{0})}\right).

Next,

(23)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\iota(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})&=&(-\tau_{0}-e_{\mathbb{1}})(-\tau_{1}-e_{\mathbb{1}})=\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1),\quad\mbox{and}\\
\iota(\tau_{1}\tau_{0})&=&(-\tau_{1}-e_{\mathbb{1}})(-\tau_{0}-e_{\mathbb{1}})=\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}(\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1).\end{array}

Suppose first that
\alpha\neq\mu
. Then since
e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}e_{\mathbb{1}}=e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}e_{\mathbb{1}}=0
by (
10
),

\mathbf{1}X_{\alpha}=(\overline{1},\overline{1})\kappa_{2}(X_{\alpha})=\zeta^{-2}\left(\overline{e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}},\overline{e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}}\right).

On the other hand, using Def.
4.1.3
, we have the congruences

X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}=e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}\equiv\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}&\mod H\tau_{0}\\
e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}&\mod H\tau_{1}.\end{array}\right.

Putting these together shows that

X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\mathbf{1}=\left(\overline{X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}},\overline{X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}}\right)=\left(\overline{e_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}},\overline{e_{\alpha\mu^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}}\right)=\zeta^{2}\mathbf{1}X_{\alpha}

as required. Suppose now that
\alpha=\mu
. Then applying (
22
) and (
23
) again, we have

\zeta^{2}\mathbf{1}X_{\mu}=(\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\iota(\tau_{0}\tau_{1})},\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\iota(\tau_{1}\tau_{0})})=(\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}},\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}}),

whereas since
X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}=X_{\mathbb{1}}=e_{\mathbb{1}}\zeta=e_{\mathbb{1}}(\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+\tau_{0}+\tau_{1}+1)
in this case, we see that

X_{\mathbb{1}}\mathbf{1}=(\overline{X_{\mathbb{1}}},\overline{X_{\mathbb{1}}})=(\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}},\overline{e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}}).

Having shown that (
21
) holds, we use part a) to obtain

\mathbf{1}\epsilon_{\alpha}=\left(\mathbf{1}\zeta^{-1}\right)X_{\alpha}=\zeta\mathbf{1}X_{\alpha}=\zeta^{-1}\left(\zeta^{2}\mathbf{1}X_{\alpha}\right)=\zeta^{-1}X_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\mathbf{1}=\epsilon_{\alpha^{-1}\mu}\mathbf{1}.\qed

4.3.3.
The annihilator of
B
in
Z_{\zeta}\otimes Z_{\zeta}

Recall from Cor.
4.2.7
that
Z_{\zeta}
is isomorphic to
k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
. Noting that
k[\Omega]
is isomorphic to the direct product of
|\widehat{\Omega}|=q-1
copies of the ground field
k
, the following statement is clear:

Lemma 4.3.10
.

There is a unique
k
-algebra automorphism
\phi:Z_{\zeta}\to Z_{\zeta}
such that

\phi(\zeta)=\zeta^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\phi(e_{\alpha})=e_{\mu/\alpha}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}.

Note that this
\phi
is in fact an involution.

Proposition 4.3.11
.

The map
\psi:(H_{\zeta})_{\phi}\to B
given by
\psi(h)=h\mathbf{1}
for all
h\in H_{\zeta}
is an injective homomorphism of
(H_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodules.

Proof.

The map
\psi
is clearly left
H_{\zeta}
-linear. Using Prop.
4.3.9
, we have

\psi(h)\zeta=h\mathbf{1}\zeta=h\zeta^{-1}\mathbf{1}=\psi(h\phi(\zeta))\quad\mbox{and}\quad\psi(h)\epsilon_{\alpha}=h\mathbf{1}\epsilon_{\alpha}=h\epsilon_{\mu/\alpha}\mathbf{1}=\psi(h\phi(\epsilon_{\alpha}))

for any
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
. After Cor.
4.2.7
, we know that
Z_{\zeta}
is generated as a
k
-algebra by the
\epsilon_{\alpha}
’s,
\zeta
and
\zeta^{-1}
. It follows that
\psi
is right
Z_{\zeta}
-linear.

We have
\ker\psi=H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}\cap H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}
by definition of
B
. This is equal to
(H\tau_{0}\cap H\tau_{1})_{\zeta}
. But
H\tau_{0}\cap H\tau_{1}=0
by Lemma
4.3.12
below.
∎

Lemma 4.3.12
.

The sum of left ideals
H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
is direct.

Proof.

Recall that
\{\tau_{w}:w\in\widetilde{W}\}
forms a basis for
H
as a
k
-vector space. Now,
\widetilde{W}
contains
\Omega
as a normal subgroup and the images of
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
in
\widetilde{W}/\Omega
generate a copy of the infinite dihedral group. It follows that every element of
\widetilde{W}
has a unique representation in the form
\omega w
where
\omega\in\Omega
and
w
is a word in
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
. Therefore the words in
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
form a basis for
H
as a
k[\Omega]
-module. Every non-empty such word ends in exactly one of either
\tau_{0}
or
\tau_{1}
, which gives the result.∎

Now we study
\coker\psi
.

Lemma 4.3.13
.

The left
H
-module
H/(H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1})
is killed by
\zeta(\zeta-1)
.

Proof.

The fact that
\{\tau_{w}:w\in\widetilde{W}\}
spans
H
implies that
H=k[\Omega]\oplus(H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1})
as a
k
-vector space. Therefore it is enough to show that
\zeta(\zeta-1)e_{\lambda}\in H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
for all
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
. However
\zeta=\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\equiv e_{\mathbb{1}}\mod H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
, so already
\zeta e_{\lambda}\equiv 0\mod H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
if
\lambda\neq\mathbb{1}
, whereas if
\lambda=\mathbb{1}
then
\zeta(\zeta-1)e_{\mathbb{1}}=\zeta(\zeta e_{\mathbb{1}}-e_{\mathbb{1}})\equiv\zeta(e_{\mathbb{1}}^{2}-e_{\mathbb{1}})=0\mod H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.14
.

There exist elements
u,v\in H
such that
\zeta(\zeta-1)=u\tau_{0}+v\tau_{1}
.

Proposition 4.3.15
.

We have
(\zeta-1)\cdot\coker\psi=0
.

Proof.

Let
(\overline{a},\overline{b})\in B
for some
a,b\in H_{\zeta}
. Define
c:=av\tau_{1}+bu\tau_{0}
, where
u,v\in H
given by Cor.
4.3.14
. Then

c=a\left(\zeta(\zeta-1)-u\tau_{0}\right)+bu\tau_{0}\equiv a\zeta(\zeta-1)\mod H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}

and similarly
c\equiv b\zeta(\zeta-1)\mod H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}
. Hence
\zeta(\zeta-1)(\overline{a},\overline{b})=(\overline{c},\overline{c})=c\mathbf{1}=\psi(c)
, so
\coker\psi
is killed by
\zeta(\zeta-1)
. Since
\zeta
is invertible in
H_{\zeta}
,
\coker\psi
is already killed by
\zeta-1
. ∎

Lemma 4.3.16
.

H/H\tau_{0}
and
H/H\tau_{1}
are
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree.

Proof.

Let
H_{0}
be the
k
-subalgebra of
H
generated by
k[\Omega]
and
\tau_{0}
. We have

H_{0}=k[\Omega]\oplus k[\Omega]\tau_{0}=k[\Omega]\oplus\tau_{0}k[\Omega].

Next, by
[
OS18
]
Cor. 3.4,
H
is free of rank
2
as a
H_{0}\otimes_{k}k[\zeta]
-module, with basis
\{1,\tau_{1}\}
. If
H_{0}[\zeta]
denotes the subalgebra of
H
generated by
H_{0}
and
\zeta
, then this means that the multiplication map
H_{0}\otimes_{k}k[\zeta]\to H_{0}[\zeta]
is a
k
-algebra isomorphism, and also that
H
is free of rank
2
as a left
H_{0}[\zeta]
-module, with basis
\{1,\tau_{1}\}
:

H=H_{0}[\zeta]\oplus H_{0}[\zeta]\tau_{1}.

Let
A:=k[\Omega][\zeta]\subset H_{0}[\zeta]
; clearly
A
is isomorphic as a
k
-algebra to the polynomial ring in one variable over
k[\Omega]
. Combining the two displayed equations above, we obtain the decomposition

H=A\oplus A\tau_{0}\oplus A\tau_{1}\oplus A\tau_{0}\tau_{1}

of
H
as a left
A
-module. Now
\tau_{1}^{2}=-e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{1}
by Lemma
4.1.1
, and
e_{\mathbb{1}}
is central in
H
. This implies that right-multiplication by
\tau_{1}
sends
H
into
A\tau_{1}\oplus A\tau_{0}\tau_{1}
. Since this
A
-module is clearly contained in
H\tau_{1}
, we conclude that
H\tau_{1}=A\tau_{1}\oplus A\tau_{0}\tau_{1}
, and therefore

H/H\tau_{1}\cong A\oplus A\tau_{0}

as a left
A
-module. Since
A
is itself free as a
k[\zeta]
-module, we see that
H/H\tau_{1}
is free of rank
2(q-1)
as a
k[\zeta]
-module. It is in particular
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree.

The argument for
H/H\tau_{0}
is the same, after switching
\tau_{0}
with
\tau_{1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3.17
.

The left
H
-module
B
is
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree.

Proof.

As a left
k[\zeta]
-module,
B
is isomorphic to
k[\zeta,\zeta^{-1}]\otimes_{k[\zeta]}\left(\frac{H}{H\tau_{0}}\oplus\frac{H}{H\tau_{1}}\right)
. The expression in the brackets is
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree by Lemma
4.3.16
, and the result follows easily.
∎

Theorem 4.3.18
.

Let
R=Z_{\zeta}\otimes Z_{\zeta}
. Then we have
\Ann_{R}(B)=(1\otimes\phi)(\ker\mult_{Z_{\zeta}})
.

Proof.

Using Prop.
4.3.11
, we see that

\Ann_{R}(B)\subseteq\Ann_{R}((H_{\zeta})_{\phi}).

Suppose that
r\in R
kills
(H_{\zeta})_{\phi}
; then
r
kills
\operatorname{im}(\psi)
. By Prop.
4.3.15
,
(\zeta-1)B\subseteq\operatorname{im}(\psi)
. Hence
r\cdot\left((\zeta-1)\otimes 1\right)
kills
B
, so
\zeta-1
kills
r\cdot B
. But
B
is
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree by Cor.
4.3.17
, so
r\cdot B=0
. This shows that

\Ann_{R}(B)=\Ann_{R}((H_{\zeta})_{\phi}).

Now we apply Lemma
4.3.2
to the
(Z_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodule
H_{\zeta}
to see that

\Ann_{R}((H_{\zeta})_{\phi})=(1\otimes\phi^{-1})(\Ann_{R}(H_{\zeta})).

Now
Z_{\zeta}
is the centre of
H_{\zeta}
, so
\Ann_{R}(H_{\zeta})=\Ann_{R}(Z_{\zeta})
by Lemma
4.3.3
. Since
\phi=\phi^{-1}
,

\Ann_{R}(B)=\Ann_{R}((H_{\zeta})_{\phi})=(1\otimes\phi)(\Ann_{R}(Z_{\zeta})).

The result now follows from Lemma
4.3.3
applied with
A=Z_{\zeta}
.
∎

Write
\delta(a):=a\otimes 1-1\otimes\phi(a)
for all
a\in Z_{\zeta}
. The following
finite
set of ideal generators for
\Ann_{R}(B)
will be useful to us later.

Corollary 4.3.19
.

We have
\Ann_{R}(B)=R\delta(\zeta)+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}R\delta(\epsilon_{\alpha})
.

Proof.

By Cor.
4.2.7
, there is a
k
-algebra isomorphism
Z_{\zeta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}k[\Omega][t,t^{-1}]
that sends
\epsilon_{\alpha}
to
e_{\alpha}
and
\zeta
to
t
. Therefore
\{\epsilon_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}\}\cup\{\zeta,\zeta^{-1}\}
forms a set of
k
-algebra generators for
Z_{\zeta}
. Writing
\partial(a)=a\otimes 1-1\otimes a
for all
a\in Z_{\zeta}
, it follows from Lemma
4.3.4
.b that

(24)

\ker\mult_{Z_{\zeta}}=R\partial(\zeta)+R\partial(\zeta^{-1})+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}R\partial(\epsilon_{\alpha}).

Note that
\partial(\zeta^{-1})=\zeta^{-1}\otimes 1+1\otimes\zeta^{-1}=(\zeta^{-1}\otimes\zeta^{-1})\partial(\zeta)
, so
R\partial(\zeta^{-1})=R\partial(\zeta)
. Also, note that
(1\otimes\phi)\partial=\delta
. Therefore, applying
1\otimes\phi
to (
24
) gives

(1\otimes\phi)\left(\ker\mult_{Z_{\zeta}}\right)=R\delta(\zeta)+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}R\delta(\epsilon_{\alpha}).

The result now follows from Thm.
4.3.18
.
∎

4.4.
The bimodule annihilator of
\Ext^{\ast}_{G}(X,X)

Our goal will be to compute the ideal

\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}E^{\ast},\quad\mbox{where}\quad E^{\ast}=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}E^{n},\quad\mbox{and}\quad E^{n}=\Ext^{n}_{G}(X,X).

For brevity, we will write for each
n\geq 0

J_{n}:=\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}\left(E^{n}\right).

First, we will use the Poincaré duality isomorphisms from
[
OS19
]
to relate
J_{n}
with
J_{d-n}
, where
d
is the dimension of
G
as a
p
-adic Lie group. For this, we need to look at the anti-involution
\mathcal{J}:E^{\ast}\to E^{\ast}
that was introduced in
[
OS19
]
,
\lx@sectionsign 6
: it is an anti-automorphism of the graded algebra
E^{\ast}
by
[
OS19
]
, Prop. 6.1, and therefore in particular restricts to a
k
-algebra anti-automorphism of
H=E^{0}
.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

\mathcal{J}
fixes
Z
pointwise.

Proof.

By
[
OS19
]
Prop. 6.1, we know that
\mathcal{J}(\tau_{g})=\tau_{g^{-1}}
for all
g\in G
, where
\tau_{g}
denotes the characteristic function of the double coset
IgI
— see
[
OS19
]
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2. It follows therefore that
\mathcal{J}
restricts to the standard anti-involution on the group algebra
k[\Omega]\subset H
that sends the group elements
\tau_{\omega}
to
\tau_{\omega^{-1}}
, for each
\omega\in\Omega
. Equation (
9
) now implies that

\mathcal{J}(e_{\lambda})=e_{\lambda^{-1}}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}.

Next, we have
s_{i}^{-1}=-s_{i}
for
i=0,1
inside
G
. Writing
\epsilon=\tau_{\omega_{-1}}
, it follows that

\mathcal{J}(\tau_{i})=\tau_{i}\epsilon,\quad i=0,1.

With these two pieces of information in hand, we can now compute the effect of
\mathcal{J}
on the algebra generators
X_{\lambda}
of
Z
from Def.
4.1.3
, for each
\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}
. When
\lambda\neq\mathbb{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(X_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(e_{\lambda}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\tau_{1})\mathcal{J}(\tau_{0})\mathcal{J}(e_{\lambda})+\mathcal{J}(\tau_{0})\mathcal{J}(\tau_{1})\mathcal{J}(e_{\lambda^{-1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt\tau_{0}\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pte_{\lambda^{-1}}+\tau_{0}\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pt\tau_{1}\epsilon\hskip 2.84544pte_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tau_{1}\tau_{0}e_{\lambda^{-1}}+\tau_{0}\tau_{1}e_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\lambda}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{\lambda}.

We have used here the fact that
\epsilon
is a central involution in
H
, as well as the relations

\tau_{i}e_{\lambda}=e_{\lambda^{-1}}\tau_{i},\quad i=0,1

from Lemma
4.1.1
. Next, recall from Lemma
4.1.2
that
\zeta=(\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{1}+e_{\mathbb{1}})+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}
. When
\lambda=\mathbb{1}
, the calculation goes as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(X_{\mathbb{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(e_{\mathbb{1}}\zeta)=\mathcal{J}(\zeta)e_{\mathbb{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle((\tau_{0}\epsilon)(\tau_{1}\epsilon)+(\tau_{1}\epsilon+e_{\mathbb{1}})(\tau_{0}\epsilon+e_{\mathbb{1}}))e_{\mathbb{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+\tau_{1}\tau_{0}+e_{\mathbb{1}}\tau_{0}\epsilon+\tau_{1}e_{\mathbb{1}}\epsilon+e_{\mathbb{1}})e_{\mathbb{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\zeta e_{\mathbb{1}}=X_{\mathbb{1}}.

We have used here the fact that
e_{\mathbb{1}}\epsilon=\mathbb{1}(\epsilon)e_{\mathbb{1}}=e_{\mathbb{1}}
. We have shown that
\mathcal{J}
fixes each
X_{\lambda}
. Since these generate
Z
as a
k
-algebra in view of Prop.
4.1.5
,
\mathcal{J}|_{Z}
is the identity map.∎

For our next result, we need to introduce the
k
-algebra involution
\sigma:Z\otimes_{k}Z\to Z\otimes_{k}Z
, given by
\sigma(z_{1}\otimes z_{2})=z_{2}\otimes z_{1}
for
z_{1},z_{2}\in Z
.

Lemma 4.4.2
.

For each
n=0,\cdots,d
, we have
J_{d-n}=\sigma(J_{n})
.

Proof.

For a
k
-vector space
V
, let
V^{\vee}
denote the full
k
-linear dual
V^{\vee}=\Hom_{k}(V,k)
. When
V
happens to be an
(H,H)
-bimodule, then
V^{\vee}
is also an
(H,H)
-bimodule. Following
[
OS19
]
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.1, this bimodule structure is given by the following rule:

(a\cdot f\cdot b)(v)=f(b\hskip 2.84544ptv\hskip 2.84544pta)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad v\in V,a,b\in H,f\in V^{\vee}.

We are only interested in
(Z,Z)
-bimodules here. Regarding every
(Z,Z)
-bimodule
V
as a left
A:=Z\otimes_{k}Z
-module in the standard way, we can write this action of
A
on
V^{\vee}
as follows:

(x\cdot f)(v)=f(\sigma(x)v)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad v\in V,x\in A,f\in V^{\vee}.

It follows immediately that for every
(Z,Z)
-bimodule
V
we have

(25)

\sigma^{-1}(\Ann_{A}V)\subseteq\Ann_{A}(V^{\vee}).

Now,
[
OS19
]
Prop. 7.18 gives us an injective homomorphism of
(H,H)
-bimodules

\Delta^{n}:E^{n}\hookrightarrow({}^{\mathcal{J}}(E^{d-n})^{\mathcal{J}})^{\vee}.

Since
\mathcal{J}
fixes
Z
pointwise by Lemma
4.4.1
, we may write this as

\Delta^{n}:E^{n}\hookrightarrow(E^{d-n})^{\vee},

an injective homomorphism of
(Z,Z)
-bimodules. Using (
25
), we obtain

J_{d-n}=\Ann_{A}(E^{d-n})\subseteq\sigma(\Ann_{A}(E^{d-n})^{\vee})\subseteq\sigma(\Ann_{A}(E^{n}))=\sigma(J_{n}).

Replacing
n
with
d-n
gives
J_{n}\subseteq\sigma(J_{d-n})
, so
J_{d-n}\subseteq\sigma(J_{n})\subseteq\sigma^{2}(J_{d-n})=J_{d-n}
. ∎

Recall the multiplication map
\mult_{Z}:Z\otimes_{k}Z\to Z
from
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: GenBimod}
.

Lemma 4.4.3
.

a)

We have
J_{0}=\ker\mult_{Z}
.

b)

J_{0}=\sigma(J_{0})
.

Proof.

Since
\Ext^{0}_{G}(X,X)=\Hom_{G}(X,X)=H
and since
Z=Z(H)
, Lemma
4.3.3
implies that
J_{0}=\ker\mult_{Z}
. The second statement follows from Lemma
4.3.4
.a.
∎

Until the end of
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: BiModAnnExt}
, we assume that
\mathfrak{F}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
p\neq 2,3
and
\pi=p
.
This assumption allows us to apply the results of
[
OS22
]
to study
J_{1}
. Recall first that by
[
OS22
]
Prop. 6.10(1), there is a certain short exact sequence of
(H,H)
-bimodules

(26)

0\to\ker(f_{1})\oplus\ker(g_{1})\to E^{1}\to C\to 0.

Here
C
is a certain
(H,H)
-bimodule with
\dim_{k}C=4
.

Lemma 4.4.4
.

We have
\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(\ker g_{1}))=J_{0}
.

Proof.

By
[
OS22
]
Prop. 6.3,
\ker(g_{1})\cong F^{1}H
as an
(H,H)
-bimodule, where
(F^{n}H)_{n\geq 0}
is the descending filtration on
H
defined at
[
OS22
]
, §2.2.4. Writing
A=Z\otimes_{k}Z
, we then have

\Ann_{A}(\ker(g_{1}))=\Ann_{A}(F^{1}H)\supseteq\Ann_{A}(H).

For the reverse inclusion, note that
F^{1}H=H\tau_{0}+H\tau_{1}
, so
\zeta(\zeta-1)
kills
H/F^{1}H
from the left by Lemma
4.3.13
. Since
H
is
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree and since
\zeta
is central in
H
, left-multiplication by
\zeta(\zeta-1)
gives an injective homomorphism of
(H,H)
-bimodules
H\hookrightarrow F^{1}H
. Hence

\Ann_{A}(\ker(g_{1}))=\Ann_{A}(F^{1}H)=\Ann_{A}(H)=\Ann_{A}(E^{0})=J_{0}.\qed

On p. 38 of
[
OS22
]
, Ollivier and Schneider consider a certain twist of
(H_{\zeta}\oplus H_{\zeta})[\kappa,z,\mu]
that they denote
(H_{\zeta}\oplus H_{\zeta})^{\pm}
. The left action of
H
on
(H_{\zeta}\oplus H_{\zeta})
is unchanged, but the right
H
-action is twisted by pre-composing the previous right
H
-action by
\iota
. They show that the left
H_{\zeta}
-submodule
H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}\oplus H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}
of this direct sum is stable under this new right
H
-action, which allows them to pass to the quotient to form the
(H_{\zeta},H)
-bimodule

\left(\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}}\oplus\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}}\right)^{\pm}=\frac{(H_{\zeta}\oplus H_{\zeta})^{\pm}}{(H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}\oplus H_{\zeta}\tau_{1})^{\pm}}.

Then they prove the following Theorem — see
[
OS22
]
Prop. 3.28 and Thm. 6.8:

Theorem 4.4.5
.

There is an
(H_{\zeta},H)
-bimodule isomorphism

\left(\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{0}}\oplus\frac{H_{\zeta}}{H_{\zeta}\tau_{1}}\right)^{\pm}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ker(f_{1})

We are only interested in the
(Z_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodule structure on
\ker(f_{1})
. Recall the
(H_{\zeta},H)
-bimodule
B
from Def.
4.3.8
.

Corollary 4.4.6
.

There is an
(H_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodule isomorphism
B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ker(f_{1})
.

Proof.

Note that
\zeta
acts invertibly on
\ker(f_{1})
from both sides by its definition: indeed the left
\zeta
-actions and the right
\zeta
-actions are mutually inverse. Therefore
\ker(f_{1})
is in fact a
(H_{\zeta},H_{\zeta})
-bimodule. Since
\iota
fixes
Z
by Lemma
4.3.5
, precomposing with
\iota
makes no difference to the right action of
Z
. Now we can apply Thm.
4.4.5
.
∎

Theorem 4.4.7
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{F}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
p\neq 2,3
and
\pi=p
.

a)

We have
J_{1}=J_{0}\cap\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(B)
.

b)

J_{1}=\sigma(J_{1})
.

Proof.

a) Again, write
A=Z\otimes_{k}Z
. Using (
26
), we see that

\Ann_{A}(E^{1})\subseteq\Ann_{A}(\ker(f_{1})\oplus\ker(g_{1})).

For the reverse inclusion, suppose that
x\in\Ann_{A}(\ker(f_{1})\oplus\ker(g_{1}))
. Since the
(H,H)
-bimodule
C
appearing in (
26
) satisfies
\dim_{k}C<\infty
, there is a non-zero
g(\zeta)\in k[\zeta]
such that
g(\zeta)E^{1}\subseteq\ker(f_{1})\oplus\ker(g_{1})
. Hence
x\cdot(g(\zeta)E^{1})=0
, so
g(\zeta)(x\cdot E^{1})=0
. Since
E^{1}
is
k[\zeta]
-torsionfree by
[
OS22
]
Lemma 5.1, this forces
x\cdot E^{1}=0
. Hence
x\in\Ann_{A}(E^{1})
and

\Ann_{A}(E^{1})=\Ann_{A}(\ker(f_{1})\oplus\ker(g_{1}))=\Ann_{A}(\ker(f_{1}))\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{A}(\ker(g_{1})).

Using Cor.
4.4.6
and Lemma
4.4.4
, we see that

(27)

J_{1}=\Ann_{A}(E^{1})=\Ann_{A}(B)\cap J_{0}.

b) We know that
B
is in fact a
(Z_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodule, so that

\Ann_{A}(B)=A\cap\Ann_{Z_{\zeta}\otimes_{k}Z_{\zeta}}(B).

In view of formula (
27
) and Lemma
4.4.3
.b, it is enough to show that the obvious extension of
\sigma
to
Z_{\zeta}\otimes Z_{\zeta}
preserves
\Ann_{Z_{\zeta}\otimes_{k}Z_{\zeta}}(B)
.

Recall from Thm.
4.3.18
that this ideal is generated by
\{\delta(a):a\in Z_{\zeta}\}
, where we define
\delta(a):=a\otimes 1-1\otimes\phi(a)
for each
a\in A
. Since
\phi^{2}(a)=a
for all
a\in Z_{\zeta}
, we calculate

\sigma(\delta(a))=\sigma\left(a\otimes 1-1\otimes\phi(a)\right)=-(\phi(a)\otimes 1-1\otimes a)=-\delta(\phi(a)).

Therefore
\sigma
preserves
\Ann_{Z_{\zeta}\otimes_{k}Z_{\zeta}}(B)
as required.
∎

Putting everything together, we obtain

Corollary 4.4.8
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{F}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
p\neq 2,3
and
\pi=p
. Then

\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(E^{\ast})\hskip 2.84544pt=\hskip 2.84544pt\ker\mult_{Z}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(B).

Proof.

Here
d=3
, so
\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(E^{\ast})=J_{0}\cap J_{1}\cap J_{2}\cap J_{3}
. However
J_{2}=\sigma(J_{1})
and
J_{3}=\sigma(J_{0})
by Lemma
4.4.2
. Using Lemma
4.4.3
.b and Thm.
4.4.7
.b, we obtain

\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(E^{\ast})=J_{0}\cap J_{1}.

Now use Thm.
4.4.7
.a and Lemma
4.4.3
.a to conclude. ∎

4.5.
The computation of the quotient space

From now on, we assume that
\mathfrak{F}=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
,
p\neq 2,3
and
\pi=p
. Our goal is to compute the quotient locally ringed space
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
, where

\mathcal{R}:=V(\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(E^{\ast}))\subset\Xi\times\Xi.

4.5.1.
Passing to the normalisation

Cor.
4.4.8
tells us that

\mathcal{R}=V\left(\ker\mult_{Z}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(B)\right).

Recall from Def.
4.2.3
that
\theta:\Xi^{\prime}\to\Xi
is the normalisation of
\Xi
, where
\theta=\Spec(\varphi)
and
\varphi:Z\to Z^{\prime}
is the map sending
X_{\lambda}\in Z
to
e_{\lambda}t\in Z^{\prime}=k[\Omega][t]
. Because the localisation map
Z\to Z_{\zeta}
factors through
Z^{\prime}
, we see that the
(Z_{\zeta},Z_{\zeta})
-bimodule
B
from Def.
4.3.8
can be viewed as a
(Z^{\prime},Z^{\prime})
-bimodule via restriction along
Z^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Z_{\zeta}
.

Definition 4.5.1
.

We define
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}:=V\left(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{Z^{\prime}\otimes_{k}Z^{\prime}}(B)\right)\subset\Xi^{\prime}\times\Xi^{\prime}.

Lemma 4.5.2
.

Write
A^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\otimes_{k}Z^{\prime}
. Then we have

\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}}=A^{\prime}(t\otimes 1-1\otimes t)+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}A^{\prime}(e_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes e_{\alpha}).

Proof.

The
k
-algebra
Z^{\prime}=k[\Omega][t]
is generated by
\{t\}\cup\{e_{\alpha}:\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}\}
. Use Lemma
4.3.4
.b.
∎

It turns out that
\Ann_{Z^{\prime}\otimes_{k}Z^{\prime}}(B)
is slightly easier to compute than
\Ann_{Z\otimes_{k}Z}(B)
.

Proposition 4.5.3
.

Write
A^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\otimes_{k}Z^{\prime}
. Then we have

\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B)=I:=A^{\prime}(t\otimes t-1\otimes 1)+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}A^{\prime}(e_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes e_{\mu/\alpha}).

Proof.

By Cor.
4.2.7
, there is a
k
-algebra isomorphism
Z^{\prime}_{t}\cong Z_{\zeta}
. Under this isomorphism,
t\in Z^{\prime}
maps to
\zeta\in Z_{\zeta}
and
e_{\alpha}\in Z^{\prime}
maps to
\epsilon_{\alpha}=X_{\alpha}/\zeta\in Z_{\zeta}
, for each
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
. This isomorphism also induces
k
-algebra isomorphisms
A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}\cong(Z^{\prime}_{t})\otimes_{k}(Z^{\prime}_{t})\cong(Z_{\zeta})\otimes_{k}(Z_{\zeta})\cong R
. Regarding
B
as an
A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}
-module via this isomorphism, we can apply Cor.
4.3.19
to obtain

\Ann_{A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}}(B)=A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}(t\otimes 1-1\otimes t^{-1})+\sum\limits_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}(e_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes e_{\mu/\alpha})=A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}\cdot I.

Using the definition of
I
, we see that
A^{\prime}/I
is isomorphic to
S[t,t^{-1}]
as a
k
-algebra, where
S:=k[\Omega]^{\otimes 2}/\left\langle e_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes e_{\mu/\alpha}:\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}\right\rangle
. This ring is
t
-torsionfree. Therefore
A^{\prime}/I
is
t\otimes t
-torsionfree, so
A^{\prime}\cap(I\cdot A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t})=I
. Hence
\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B)=A^{\prime}\cap\Ann_{A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t}}(B)=A^{\prime}\cap(I\cdot A^{\prime}_{t\otimes t})=I.

∎

Recall the coequaliser diagram
\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\Xi}
from Prop.
4.2.5
.

Proposition 4.5.4
.

Let
q^{\prime}:\Xi^{\prime}\to\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
be a coequaliser of in
\LRS
of
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}}
, and let
s:\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
be a coequaliser in
\LRS
of
\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}a}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}b}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
. Then

\Xi/\mathcal{R}\quad\cong\quad(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}.

Proof.

The situation is summarised in the following diagram:

(28)

\begin{split}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
