The result now follows from Lemma
A.1.2
, provided we can find an epimorphism
\theta^{\prime}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{R}
in
\LRS
making the diagram commute, in the sense that
\theta^{\prime}\pr_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}=\pr_{i}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\theta
holds for
i=1,2
.

Write
A=Z\otimes_{k}Z
and
A^{\prime}=Z^{\prime}\otimes_{k}Z^{\prime}
; then by Cor.
4.4.8
we have

\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R})=\frac{A}{\ker\mult_{Z}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{A}(B)}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})=\frac{A^{\prime}}{\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B)}.

The map
\varphi\otimes\varphi:A\to A^{\prime}
is an injective
k
-algebra homomorphism; furthermore we have

\ker\mult_{Z}=(\varphi\otimes\varphi)^{-1}(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Ann_{A}(B)=(\varphi\otimes\varphi)^{-1}(\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B)).

Therefore
\varphi\otimes\varphi
descends to a natural
injective

k
-algebra homomorphism

(29)

\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}:\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}).

We define
\theta^{\prime}:=\Spec(\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}):\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{R}
to be the corresponding morphism of affine schemes. The following diagram of commutative rings

\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}

is readily checked to be commutative: for example, we have

\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(\varphi(a))=\overline{\varphi(a)\otimes 1}=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(\overline{a\otimes 1})=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}(\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(a))\quad\mbox{for any}\quad a\in Z.

Because the schemes
\Xi,\Xi^{\prime},\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are all affine, it follows that
\theta^{\prime}\pr_{i}^{\mathcal{R}}=\pr_{i}^{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}\theta
holds for
i=1,2
. It remains to show that
\theta^{\prime}
is an epimorphism in
\LRS
, and this follows from Lemma
4.5.5
below, once we check its two conditions.

a) We note that
Z^{\prime}=k[\Omega][t]
is a finitely generated
Z
-module via
\varphi:Z\to Z^{\prime}
: indeed,
\Omega\subset Z^{\prime}
is a finite generating set as a
k[t]
-module, and
k[t]=k[\varphi(\zeta)]\subseteq\varphi(Z)
. Hence
A^{\prime}
is a finitely generated
A
-module, so
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime})
is a finitely generated
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R})
-module. Hence
|\theta^{\prime}|
is surjective by
[
AM
]
Prop. 5.1 and Thm. 5.10.

b) The map
(\theta^{\prime})^{\sharp}(\mathcal{R})=\overline{\varphi\otimes\varphi}
is injective, as we saw above in equation (
29
).
∎

Lemma 4.5.5
.
4
4

4

Because
|\cdot|:\LRS\to\Top
is a left adjoint, it must preserve epimorphisms, so condition a) is necessary for Lemma
4.5.5
to hold. The discussion
[
KStm
]
gives an explicit example where Lemma
4.5.5
fails if only condition b) is assumed to hold.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a morphism of affine schemes. Suppose that

a)

|f|:|Y|\to|X|
is surjective,

b)

f^{\sharp}(X):\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
is injective.

Then
f:Y\to X
is an epimorphism in
\LRS
.

Proof.

Suppose that
uf=vf
for some morphisms
u,v:X\to Z
in
\LRS
. Then
|u||f|=|v||f|
implies that
|u|=|v|
because
|f|
is surjective. Hence it remains to show that
u^{\sharp}=v^{\sharp}
. Now,
|u|_{\ast}(f^{\sharp})\circ u^{\sharp}=(uf)^{\sharp}=(vf)^{\sharp}=|v|_{\ast}(f^{\sharp})\circ v^{\sharp}
, so it is enough to show that
f^{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}_{X}\to f_{\ast}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is injective. This can be checked on basic open subsets
D(g)
of
X
,
g\in\mathcal{O}(X)
. But since
X,Y
are affine, the map
f^{\sharp}(D(g)):\mathcal{O}(D(g))\to\mathcal{O}(f^{-1}D(g))
is the localisation of the injective map
f^{\sharp}(X):\mathcal{O}(X)\to\mathcal{O}(Y)
at
g
and is therefore also injective.
∎

4.5.2.
Calculating
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

For each
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
, we let
\Xi_{\alpha}:=V(1-e_{\alpha})\subset\Xi^{\prime}
be the closed subscheme cut out by the idempotent
1-e_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime})=k[\Omega][t]
. Let
t_{\alpha}
be the image of
t\in\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha})
; then
\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha})=k[t_{\alpha}]
and
\Xi^{\prime}=\coprod_{\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}}\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}
. It follows that

\Xi^{\prime}\times\Xi^{\prime}=\coprod_{\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}}\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta}.

We write
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}:=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cap(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta})
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}
, so that

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\coprod_{\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}.

For each
\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}
, will identify
\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta})=k[t_{\alpha}]\otimes_{k}k[t_{\beta}]
with the polynomial algebra
k[x,y]
, via
x\mapsto t_{\alpha}\otimes 1
and
y\mapsto 1\otimes t_{\beta}
. With this notation in hand, we can now calculate the defining equations for each
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Proposition 4.5.6
.

For every
\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}
we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}=V((x-y)^{\delta_{\alpha,\beta}}(xy-1)^{\delta_{\alpha,\mu/\beta}}).

Proof.

Consider the canonical projection
\pi_{\alpha,\beta}:A^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}\times\Xi^{\prime})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta})=k[x,y]
with kernel
\ker\pi_{\alpha,\beta}=\langle(1-e_{\alpha})\otimes 1,1\otimes(1-e_{\beta})\rangle
. Then we for all
\gamma,\delta\in\widehat{\Omega}
we have

(30)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\gamma}\otimes e_{\delta})&=&\delta_{\alpha,\gamma}\delta_{\beta,\delta},\\
\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\gamma}\otimes 1)&=&\delta_{\alpha,\gamma},\\
\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(1\otimes e_{\delta})&=&\delta_{\beta,\delta}.\end{array}

Using Lemma
4.5.2
together with equations
(\ref{eq: piabcd})
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x-y\rangle+\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}\langle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\lambda}\otimes 1)-\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(1\otimes e_{\lambda})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle x-y\rangle+\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}\langle\delta_{\alpha,\lambda}-\delta_{\beta,\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle(x-y)^{\delta_{\alpha,\beta}}\rangle.

Similarly, using Prop.
4.5.3
together with equations
(\ref{eq: piabcd})
, we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle xy-1\rangle+\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}\langle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(e_{\lambda}\otimes 1)-\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(1\otimes e_{\mu/\lambda})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle xy-1\rangle+\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\widehat{\Omega}}\langle\delta_{\alpha,\lambda}-\delta_{\beta,\mu/\lambda}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle(xy-1)^{\delta_{\alpha,\mu/\beta}}\rangle.

By the elementary Lemma
4.5.7
below, we then have

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}})\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle(x-y)^{\delta_{\alpha,\beta}}\rangle\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\langle(xy-1)^{\delta_{\alpha,\mu/\beta}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle(x-y)^{\delta_{\alpha,\beta}}(xy-1)^{\delta_{\alpha,\mu/\beta}}\rangle.

Recalling that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cap(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta})
, and using Def.
4.5.1
, we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}=V(\pi_{\alpha,\beta}(\ker\mult_{Z^{\prime}}\hskip 2.84544pt\cap\hskip 2.84544pt\Ann_{A^{\prime}}(B)))\subset\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta}.

The result follows. ∎

Lemma 4.5.7
.

Let
A
be a ring, let
e,f\in A
be two central idempotents and consider the canonical projection
\pi:A\twoheadrightarrow A/\langle 1-e,1-f\rangle
. Then for every pair of ideals
I,J
of
A
we have
\pi(I\cap J)=\pi(I)\cap\pi(J)
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\pi(I)\cap\pi(J)
. Then we can find
x\in I
and
y\in J
such that
a=\pi(x)=\pi(y)
. Now,
x=(1-e+e)(1-f+f)x
implies that
\pi(x)=\pi(efx)
and similarly
\pi(y)=\pi(efy)
. Hence
\pi(efx)=\pi(x)=\pi(y)=\pi(efy)
, so
efx-efy\in\ker\pi
. But
ef(1-e)=ef(1-f)=0
, so
ef\ker\pi=0
. Hence
ef(efx-efy)=0
, so
efx=e^{2}f^{2}x=e^{2}f^{2}y=efy
. Since
efx\in I
and
efy\in J
, we see that
efx=efy\in I\cap J
. Hence
a=\pi(x)=\pi(efx)\in\pi(I\cap J)
, so
\pi(I)\cap\pi(J)\subseteq\pi(I\cap J)
. The reverse inclusion is clear. ∎

We introduce an equivalence relation
\sim
on
\widehat{\Omega}
by setting
\alpha\sim\beta
if and only if
\beta\in\{\alpha,\mu/\alpha\}
. For an equivalence class
\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim
, we write

(31)

\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}:=\coprod_{\alpha\in\gamma}\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}:=\coprod_{\alpha,\beta\in\gamma}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}.

We use the projection maps
\pr_{1}^{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}},\pr_{2}^{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}}:\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\to\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}
to define the map

f_{i}^{\gamma}:=\pr_{i}^{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\to\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\quad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,2.

Then for each
\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim
, we may form the coequaliser diagram in
\LRS

(32)

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}^{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}^{\gamma}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}.}

Lemma 4.5.8
.

We have
\Xi^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
.

Proof.

Only the second statement needs proof. For this, we have

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\alpha,\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\coprod\limits_{\alpha\in\gamma}\coprod\limits_{\beta\in\widehat{\Omega}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}.

But by Prop.
4.5.6
, we have
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}=\emptyset
if
\beta\notin\{\alpha,\mu/\alpha\}
. Hence
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
.
∎

Corollary 4.5.9
.

We have
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cong\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
in
\LRS
.

Proof.

We have
\Xi^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
by Lemma
4.5.8
. Note also that
\pr_{i}^{\Xi^{\prime}}=\coprod\limits_{\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\sim}f_{i}^{\gamma}
for
i=1,2
. Now the result follows from Lemma
A.1.3
.
∎

Recall
[
Sta
]

Section 01JA
that a
gluing datum in
\LRS
consists of the following data:

•

an index set
I
,

•

a locally ringed space
X_{i}
for each
i\in I
,

•

an open subspace
\iota_{i,j}:U_{i,j}\hookrightarrow X_{i}
for each
i,j\in I
,

•

an isomorphism
\varphi_{i,j}:U_{i,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{j,i}
in
\LRS
for all
i,j\in I
,

such that

(1)

U_{i,i}=X_{i}
for all
i\in I
, and

(2)

\varphi_{k,j}\circ\varphi_{j,i}|_{U_{i,j}\cap U_{i,k}}=\varphi_{k,i}|_{U_{i,j}\cap U_{i,k}}
holds for all
i,j,k\in I
.

Recall from
[
DG
]
Prop. I.1.1.6 that
\LRS
admits all colimits.

Definition 4.5.10
.

Let
\left(I,\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{\iota_{i,j}\}_{i,j\in I},\{\varphi_{i,j}\}_{i,j\in I}\right)
be a gluing datum in
\LRS
. Form the coproduct
U:=\coprod\limits_{i\in I}U_{i}
and let
\ell_{i}:U_{i}\hookrightarrow U
be the canonical inclusions for each
i\in I
. Define

(33)

\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{i,j}U_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{i\in I}U_{i}}

by setting
u=(u_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
and
v=(v_{i,j})_{i,j\in I}
, where

(34)

u_{i,j}=\ell_{i}\circ\iota_{i,j}\quad\mbox{and}\quad v_{i,j}=\ell_{j}\circ\iota_{j,i}\circ\varphi_{i,j}\quad\mbox{for all}\quad i,j\in I.

If
q:U\to X
is a coequaliser of this diagram in
\LRS
, then we call
X
the
gluing of the
X_{i}
’s with respect to the gluing datum
.

Remark 4.5.11
.

It is shown in
[
Sta
]
,
Lemma 01JA
that the glued locally ringed space
X
admits an open covering
\{U_{i}:i\in I\}
such that
U_{i}\cong X_{i}
for all
i\in I
. It follows immediately that
X
is a scheme whenever each
U_{i}
is a scheme.

Theorem 4.5.12
.

Suppose that
\gamma=\{\alpha,\beta\}
, where
\beta=\mu/\alpha
and
\alpha\neq\beta
. Then
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is isomorphic to the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

We will first recall the well-known gluing datum that is used in the construction of the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
: see, for example
[
Sta
]
,
Example 01JE
. The indexing set is
I:=\{\alpha,\beta\}
. The spaces to be glued are
X_{\alpha}:=\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\Spec k[t_{\alpha}]
and
X_{\beta}:=\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta}=\Spec k[t_{\beta}]
. The
U_{i,j}
’s are as follows:

U_{\alpha,\alpha}:=\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha},\quad U_{\beta,\beta}=\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta},\quad U_{\alpha,\beta}=\Spec k[t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{-1}]\quad\mbox{and}\quad U_{\beta,\alpha}=\Spec k[t_{\beta},t_{\beta}^{-1}]

The inclusions
\iota_{\alpha,\beta}
are made clear by the notation, and the gluing isomorphisms
\varphi_{i,j}
are as follows:
\varphi_{\alpha,\alpha}=\id_{U_{\alpha}}
,
\varphi_{\beta,\beta}=\id_{U_{\beta}}
,
\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}=\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}^{-1}
, and
\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}:U_{\alpha,\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\beta,\alpha}
is determined by the corresponding map
\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}
on coordinate rings, which is given by
\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}(t_{\beta})=t_{\alpha}^{-1}
. Hence we have the coequaliser diagram

U_{\gamma}:=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.70876pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.70876pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{i,j\in\{\alpha,\beta\}}U_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.29865pt\raise 7.09029pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{u}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.70876pt\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.53708pt\raise-7.09029pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{v}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.70876pt\raise-2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.70876pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 83.70876pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\coprod\Xi^{\prime}_{\beta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 139.4984pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 176.94194pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 146.94194pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 176.94194pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Recall that
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is defined by the coequaliser diagram (
32
). We see that to show that
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
as locally ringed spaces, it will be enough find an isomorphism of schemes

\tau:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{\gamma}

such that the following two diagrams of affine schemes are commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\textstyle{U_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}}

and

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}}
\textstyle{U_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}.}

Here, both of the top horizontal arrows are the closed embedding of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
into
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}
. Now, the relation
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
also decomposes as a disjoint union

\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\coprod\limits_{i,j\in\{\alpha,\beta\}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}\quad\subset\quad\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\coprod\limits_{i,j\in\{\alpha,\beta\}}\Xi^{\prime}_{i}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{j}

so it will be enough to work componentwise, and for each
i,j\in\gamma
to find an isomorphism

\tau_{ij}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}U_{i,j}

such that the following two diagrams of affine schemes are commutative:

(35)

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{i,j}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{i}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\textstyle{U_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u_{i,j}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{i}}

and

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{i,j}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{i}\times\Xi^{\prime}_{j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}}
\textstyle{U_{i,j}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{v_{i,j}}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{j}.}

Because
\alpha\neq\beta
, the connected components
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}
of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
are given by Prop.
4.5.6
as follows:

(36)

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha}&=&V(\langle t_{\alpha}\otimes 1-1\otimes t_{\alpha}\rangle),\\
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\beta,\beta}&=&V(\langle t_{\beta}\otimes 1-1\otimes t_{\beta}\rangle),\\
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\beta}&=&V(\langle t_{\alpha}\otimes t_{\beta}-1\otimes 1\rangle),\quad\mbox{and}\\
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\beta,\alpha}&=&V(\langle t_{\beta}\otimes t_{\alpha}-1\otimes 1\rangle).\end{array}

Suppose first that
j=i
. In this case
u_{i,i}=\id_{\Xi^{\prime}_{i}}
, so we define
\tau_{i,i}:=\pr_{1}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}}
; then the first diagram in (
35
) commutes by definition. For the second diagram, note that
v_{i,i}=u_{i,i}\circ\varphi_{i,i}
is also equal to
\id_{\Xi^{\prime}_{i}}
, so
v_{i,i}\circ\tau_{i,i}=\pr_{1}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,i}}=\pr_{2}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,i}}
in view of the first two equations in
(\ref{eq: Rij})
. Hence the second diagram in (
35
) commutes as well.

Suppose now that
j\neq i
. Looking at the last two equations in
(\ref{eq: Rij})
, we see that the element
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(t_{i})|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}}=\overline{t_{i}\otimes 1}
is a unit in
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j})
. Therefore the
k
-algebra homomorphism
(\pr_{1}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}})^{\sharp}:\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{i})\to\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j})
extends to the localisation
\mathcal{O}(U_{i,j})
of
\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{i})
, which means that
\pr_{1}|_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}}
factors through the Zariski open subset
U_{i,j}
of
\Xi^{\prime}_{i}
. In other words, there exists a morphism
\tau_{i,j}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{i,j}\to U_{i,j}
, making the first diagram in
(\ref{eq: tauij})
commutative. Looking at the last two equations in
(\ref{eq: Rij})
again, we see that the corresponding map on coordinate rings

\tau_{i,j}^{\sharp}:k[t_{i},t_{i}^{-1}]\to\frac{k[t_{i}]\otimes k[t_{j}]}{\langle t_{i}\otimes t_{j}-1\otimes 1\rangle}

sends
t_{i}
to
\overline{t_{i}\otimes 1}
and
t_{i}^{-1}
to
\overline{t_{i}\otimes 1}^{-1}=\overline{1\otimes t_{j}}
, and that it is an isomorphism. Therefore
\tau_{i,j}
is also an isomorphism. It remains to check that the second diagram in
(\ref{eq: tauij})
is commutative. Since all schemes involved are affine, it will be enough to check on coordinate rings.

Suppose that
(i,j)=(\alpha,\beta)
. Since
v_{\alpha,\beta}=u_{\beta,\alpha}\circ\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}
by (
34
), this diagram is

\textstyle{\frac{k[t_{\alpha}]\otimes k[t_{\beta}]}{\langle t_{\alpha}\otimes t_{\beta}-1\otimes 1\rangle}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\alpha}]\otimes k[t_{\beta}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\beta},t_{\beta}^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\beta}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}

and it is commutative because
\tau_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}(\varphi_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}(t_{\beta}))=\tau_{\alpha,\beta}^{\sharp}(t_{\alpha}^{-1})=\overline{1\otimes t_{\beta}}=\overline{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}(t_{\beta})}
.

Similarly, when
(i,j)=(\beta,\alpha)
, because
v_{\beta,\alpha}=u_{\alpha,\beta}\circ\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}
by (
34
), this diagram is

\textstyle{\frac{k[t_{\beta}]\otimes k[t_{\alpha}]}{\langle t_{\beta}\otimes t_{\alpha}-1\otimes 1\rangle}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\beta}]\otimes k[t_{\alpha}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[t_{\beta},t_{\beta}^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{\beta,\alpha}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\alpha},t_{\alpha}^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[t_{\alpha}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}

and it is commutative because
\tau_{\beta,\alpha}^{\sharp}(\varphi_{\beta,\alpha}^{\sharp}(t_{\alpha}))=\tau_{\beta,\alpha}^{\sharp}(t_{\beta}^{-1})=\overline{1\otimes t_{\alpha}}=\overline{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}(t_{\alpha})}
. ∎

Next, we will study the following diagram of
k
-schemes:

(37)

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}

where
\mathbb{A}^{1}=\Spec k[x]
is an affine line,
\mathbb{R}=\Delta\cup\mathbb{H}=V((x-y)(xy-1))\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2}=\Spec k[x,y]
is the union of the hyperbola
\mathbb{H}=V(xy-1)
and the diagonal
\Delta=V(x-y)
,
\pr_{1},\pr_{2}
are the projection morphisms whose respective comorphisms are determined by
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(x)=x
and
\pr_{2}^{\sharp}(x)=y
, and the morphism
\psi:\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, viewed as a natural transformation between the corresponding functors of points, is given by the rule

\psi(a)=(a^{2}+1:a)\quad\mbox{for all}\quad a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}.

Lemma 4.5.13
.

We have
\psi\circ\pr_{1}=\psi\circ\pr_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\psi(\pr_{1}(a,b))=\psi(a)=(a^{2}+1:a)
and
\psi(\pr_{2}(a,b))=\psi(b)=(b^{2}+1:b)
. However
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}
implies that
a=b
or
ab=1
, and in either case we have
(a^{2}+1)b=a(b^{2}+1)
. Hence
\psi(\pr_{1}(a,b))=\psi(\pr_{2}(a,b))
. ∎

We let
y
be a local coordinate on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, determined by
y((a:1))=a
, so that
Y_{0}:=\Spec k[y]
and
Y_{\infty}:=\Spec k[1/y]
form a standard open covering of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
by two affine lines.

Lemma 4.5.14
.

Let
X_{i}=\psi^{-1}(Y_{i})
and
\mathbb{R}_{i}=\pr_{1}^{-1}(X_{i})
for
i=0,\infty
. Then
X_{i}
is a basic affine open in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and
\mathbb{R}_{i}
is a basic affine open in
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We have
X_{0}=\{a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}:(a^{2}+1:a)\in Y_{0}\}=\{a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}:a\neq 0\}
, which is the basic affine open
D(x)\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Similarly,
X_{\infty}=\{a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}:(a^{2}+1:a)\in Y_{\infty}\}=\{a\in\mathbb{A}^{1}:a^{2}+1\neq 0\}
, which is the basic affine open
D(x^{2}+1)\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}
. The preimage of any basic affine open
D(f)\subset\Spec k[x]
under the morphism of affine schemes
\pr_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is
D(\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(f))
, which is a basic affine open in
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore
\mathbb{R}_{0}
and
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
are basic affine opens in
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Proposition 4.5.15
.

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(Y_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(X_{i})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{R}_{i})}
is an equaliser diagram of commutative rings if
i=0
or
i=\infty
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{H}_{i}=\mathbb{R}_{i}\cap\mathbb{H}
for
i=0,\infty
. By postcomposing
\pr_{1}^{\sharp},\pr_{2}^{\sharp}
with the restriction maps
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{R}_{i})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}_{i})
, we can replace
\mathbb{R}_{i}
by
\mathbb{H}_{i}
in this proof.

First we consider the case
i=0
, where the diagram becomes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[y]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[x,x^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[x,x^{-1}]}

and the maps are given by
\psi^{\sharp}(y)=x+x^{-1}
,
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(x)=x
,
\pr_{2}^{\sharp}(x)=x^{-1}
. Clearly
\psi^{\sharp}
is injective, and
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}\psi^{\sharp}=\pr_{2}^{\sharp}\psi^{\sharp}
by Lemma
4.5.13
.

Suppose that
a=\sum\limits_{i=-n}^{n}a_{i}x^{i}\in\ker(\pr_{1}^{\sharp}-\pr_{2}^{\sharp})
for some
a_{i}\in k
. Then
a_{i}=a_{-i}
for all
i
, so
a
lies in the
k
-linear span of
\{x^{n}+x^{-n}:n\geq 0\}
. Hence
a\in F_{m}:=\sum\limits_{n=0}^{m}k(x^{n}+x^{-n})
for some
m\geq 0
. We will show by induction that
F_{r}\subseteq k[x+x^{-1}]
for all
r\geq 0
. The base case
r=0
is clear. Assuming inductively that
F_{r-1}\subseteq k[x+x^{-1}]
for some
r\geq 1
, we see that
x^{r}+x^{-r}\equiv(x+x^{-1})^{r}\mod F_{r-1}
, so
x^{r}+x^{-r}\in k[x+x^{-1}]
as well. Hence

(38)

\ker(\pr_{1}^{\sharp}-\pr_{2}^{\sharp})=k[x+x^{-1}],

and the sequence is exact in the middle as required.

Next, we consider the case where
i=\infty
. The diagram becomes

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{k[y^{-1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k[x,\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}^{\sharp}}
\textstyle{k\left[x,x^{-1},\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}\right]}

where the maps are now given by
\psi^{\sharp}(y^{-1})=\frac{1}{x+x^{-1}}=\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}
,
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}(x)=x
, and
\pr_{2}^{\sharp}(x)=x^{-1}
. It is again clear that
\psi^{\sharp}
is injective, and that
\pr_{1}^{\sharp}\psi^{\sharp}=\pr_{2}^{\sharp}\psi^{\sharp}
by Lemma
4.5.13
.

Suppose that
\frac{a(x)}{(x^{2}+1)^{n}}\in\ker(\pr_{1}^{\sharp}-\pr_{2}^{\sharp})
for some
a(x)\in k[x]
and some
n\geq 0
. Then

\frac{a(x)}{(x^{2}+1)^{n}}=\frac{a(x^{-1})}{(x^{-2}+1)^{n}}.

Multiplying through by
(x^{2}+1)^{n}
shows that
a(x)=a(x^{-1})x^{2n}
. Hence
\deg(a)\leq 2n
, and dividing through by
x^{n}
shows that
a(x)x^{-n}\in k[x,x^{-1}]
is invariant under the substitution
x\mapsto x^{-1}
. Using
(\ref{eq: kerpr1pr2})
, we see that
a(x)x^{-n}=b(x+x^{-1})
for some
b(x)\in k[x]
with
\deg b\leq n
. Write
b(x)=b_{0}+b_{1}x+\cdots+b_{n}x^{n}
for some
b_{0},\cdots,b_{n}\in k
; then

\frac{a(x)}{(x^{2}+1)^{n}}=\frac{x^{n}}{(x^{2}+1)^{n}}\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}\left(\frac{x^{2}+1}{x}\right)^{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{n}b_{i}\left(\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}\right)^{n-i}\in k\left[\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}\right].

Hence
\frac{a(x)}{(x^{2}+1)^{n}}\in k\left[\frac{x}{x^{2}+1}\right]
and the sequence is exact in the middle as required.
∎

Proposition 4.5.16
.

\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
is a coequaliser diagram in
\LRS
.

Proof.

Noting that
\psi\circ\pr_{1}=\psi\circ\pr_{2}
by Lemma
4.5.13
, this follows from Lemma
4.2.1
, applied with the standard covering
\{Y_{0}=\Spec k[y],Y_{\infty}=\Spec k[y^{-1}]\}
of
Y=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, once we have checked its three conditions. Condition b) follows from Lemma
4.5.14
and condition c) follows from Prop.
4.5.15
, so it remains to check condition a). This amounts to checking the following:

(i)

|\psi|:|\mathbb{A}^{1}|\to|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
is surjective,

(ii)

the equivalence classes of the equivalence relation
\sim
on
|\mathbb{A}^{1}|
defined by
\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\pr_{1}|}
\scriptstyle{|\pr_{2}|}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{A}^{1}|}
are equal to the fibres of
|\psi|
,

(iii)

the induced map
|\mathbb{A}^{1}|/\sim\quad\longrightarrow\quad|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
is a homeomorphism.

For (i) and (ii), for any
k
-variety of finite type
X
, we use
[
MO
]
, Chapter IV, Thm. 2.3 to identify
|X|
with the set of
\mathcal{G}
-orbits in
|X_{\overline{k}}|
, where
\mathcal{G}=\Aut(\overline{k}/k)
and
X_{\overline{k}}=X\times_{k}\overline{k}
is the base-change of
X
to
\overline{k}
. Then we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}_{\overline{k}}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\overline{k}}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\psi_{\overline{k}}|}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\overline{k}}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{R}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{A}^{1}|\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{|\psi|}
\textstyle{|\mathbb{P}^{1}|}

where the vertical arrows are surjective. Chasing this diagram reduces us to the case
k=\overline{k}
.

(i) The map
|\psi|
sends the generic point in
|\mathbb{A}^{1}|
to the generic point in
|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
, and
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
to the point at infinity
(1:0)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Any other point in
|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
is of the form
(c:1)
for some
c\in k
; since
k
is algebraically closed, the equation
a+a^{-1}=c
has a solution, so
(c:1)\in\operatorname{im}(|\psi|)
.

(ii) Let
a,b\in|\mathbb{A}^{1}|
be such that
\psi(a)=\psi(b)
. If
\psi(a)
is the generic point of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, then
a
and
b
must both be equal to the generic point of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, so assume otherwise. Then necessarily
a,b
are closed points in
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Since
k=\overline{k}
we may assume that
a,b\in\mathbb{A}^{1}(k)=k
. Now
\psi(a)=\psi(b)
implies that
(a^{2}+1:a)=(b^{2}+1:b)
, hence
(a^{2}+1)b=a(b^{2}+1)
, so
(a-b)(ab-1)=0
and
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}(k)
. Setting
u=(a,b)\in|\mathbb{R}|
, we see that
a=\pr_{1}(u)
and
b=\pr_{2}(u)
as required.

(iii) The map
|\psi|:|\mathbb{A}^{1}|\to|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
is surjective and has finite fibres. Hence the quotient topology on
|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
induced by this map from the Zariski topology on
|\mathbb{A}^{1}|
is the cofinite topology, and therefore coincides with the Zariski topology on
|\mathbb{P}^{1}|
.
∎

Theorem 4.5.17
.

Suppose that
\gamma=\{\alpha\}
, where
\alpha=\mu/\alpha
. Then
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is also isomorphic to the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Since
\gamma=\{\alpha\}
, we have
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\Spec k[t_{\alpha}]
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha}
in view of equation (
31
). Since
\alpha=\mu/\alpha
, Prop.
4.5.6
tells us that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha}=V((x-y)(xy-1))
, where
x=t_{\alpha}\otimes 1
and
y=1\otimes t_{\alpha}
. Hence we have a commutative diagram of schemes

(39)

\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}^{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{f_{2}^{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pr_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}}

where the top line is the diagram (
32
), and the vertical arrows are the isomorphisms of schemes whose corresponding comorphisms are

\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{R})=\frac{k[x,y]}{\langle(x-y)(xy-1)\rangle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\alpha,\alpha})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{A}^{1})=k[x]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}k[t_{\alpha}]=\mathcal{O}(\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}),

given by
x\mapsto t_{\alpha}\otimes 1,y\mapsto 1\otimes t_{\beta}
and
x\mapsto t_{\alpha}
, respectively. Since
\psi
is a coequaliser of
\pr_{1},\pr_{2}
in
\LRS
by Prop.
4.5.16
, this gives the required isomorphism of schemes
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
∎

Corollary 4.5.18
.

\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the disjoint union of
\frac{p+1}{2}
projective lines.

Proof.

After Cor.
4.5.9
, Thm.
4.5.12
and Thm.
4.5.17
,
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to the disjoint union of
|\widehat{\Omega}/\!\!\!\sim\!\!\!|
projective lines. There are exactly
2
elements
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
that satisfy
\alpha=\mu/\alpha
: if
\alpha=\id^{j}
for some
j=0,\cdots,p-2
, then
\alpha^{2}=\id^{2j}=\mu=\id^{2}
if and only if
2j\equiv 2\mod p-1
which is equivalent to
j=1
or
j=\frac{p+1}{2}
. These correspond to the singleton equivalence classes, and all other classes have size two. Therefore
|\widehat{\Omega}/\!\!\sim\!\!|=2+\frac{p-3}{2}=\frac{p+1}{2}
.
∎

4.5.3.
Re-gluing the projective lines to form
\Xi/\mathcal{R}

At this point we have to introduce more notation.

Definition 4.5.19
.

For each
1\leq r\leq\frac{p+1}{2}
, let
\gamma_{r}:=\{\id^{r},\id^{2-r}\}
and define

Z_{r}:=\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma_{r}}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma_{r}}

Note that
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cong Z_{1}\coprod Z_{2}\coprod\cdots\coprod Z_{\frac{p+1}{2}}
by Cor.
4.5.9
and Cor.
4.5.18
. After Thm.
4.5.12
and Thm.
4.5.17
, we know that each
Z_{r}
is isomorphic to the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Note also that if
\alpha=\id^{r}
, then
\alpha=\mu/\alpha
if and only if
r=1
or
r=\frac{p+1}{2}
.

Definition 4.5.20
.

Let
1\leq r\leq\frac{p+1}{2}
and write
\alpha=\id^{r}
.

•

If
r=1
, let
z_{r}
be the local coordinate on
Z_{r}
that pulls back to
\frac{t_{\alpha}}{t_{\alpha}^{2}+1}
under the morphism
\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
from the proof of Thm.
4.5.17
.

•

If
r\neq 1
and
r\neq\frac{p+1}{2}
, let
z_{r}
be the local coordinate on
Z_{r}
that pulls back to
t_{\alpha}
under the morphism
\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\coprod\Xi^{\prime}_{\id^{2}/\alpha}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
from the proof of Thm.
4.5.12
.

•

If
r=\frac{p+1}{2}
, let
z_{r}
be the local coordinate on
Z_{r}
that pulls back to
t_{\alpha}+t_{\alpha}^{-1}
under the morphism
\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
from the proof of Thm.
4.5.17
.

•

Let
O_{r},\infty_{r}\in Z_{r}
be the closed points defined by
O_{r}:=\{z_{r}=0\}
and
\infty_{r}:=\{z_{r}=\infty\}
.

Recall that for any
\alpha\in\widehat{\Omega}
, the origin on the affine line
\Xi^{\prime}_{\alpha}
is denoted by
O_{\alpha}
, and that
q^{\prime}:\Xi^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
denotes the quotient map. After revisiting the proofs of Thm.
4.5.12
and Thm.
4.5.17
, we have the following

Lemma 4.5.21
.

Let
1\leq r\leq\frac{p+1}{2}
.

•

If
r=1
, then
q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{1}})=O_{1}
.

•

If
r\neq 1
and
r\neq\frac{p+1}{2}
, then
q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{r}})=O_{r}
and
q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{2-r}})=\infty_{r}
.

•

If
r=\frac{p+1}{2}
, then
q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{\frac{p+1}{2}}})=\infty_{\frac{p+1}{2}}
.

Recall the coequaliser diagram
\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\Xi^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{\Xi}
from Prop.
4.2.5
. After Prop.
4.5.4
, we wish to better understand the coequaliser diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.21506pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.21506pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.28783pt\raise 9.1617pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}a}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.21506pt\raise 2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.69832pt\raise-9.16168pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.21725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}b}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.21506pt\raise-2.58334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.21506pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 81.23291pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{s}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.85397pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 94.85397pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Lemma 4.5.22
.

The pairs
\{\left(q^{\prime}a(s_{r}),q^{\prime}b(s_{r})\right):r=1,\cdots,\frac{p-3}{2}\}
are explicitly given as follows:

(q^{\prime}a(s_{r}),q^{\prime}b(s_{r}))=(O_{r},\infty_{r+2})\quad\mbox{for all}\quad r=1,\cdots,\frac{p-3}{2}.

Proof.

Suppose first that
r\neq\frac{p-3}{2}
. Then using Def.
4.2.4
.b and Lemma
4.5.21
, we have

q^{\prime}(a(s_{r}))=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{r}})=O_{r}\quad\mbox{and}\quad q^{\prime}(b(s_{r}))=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{-r}})=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{2-(r+2)}})=\infty_{r+2},

where the last equality holds because
1\leq r<\frac{p-3}{2}
implies that
1<r+2<\frac{p+1}{2}
.

Suppose now that
r=\frac{p-3}{2}
. Then we still have
q^{\prime}(a(s_{r}))=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{r}})=O_{r}
, but now the last case in Lemma
4.5.21
gives

q^{\prime}(b(s_{\frac{p-3}{2}}))=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{-\left(\frac{p-3}{2}\right)}})=q^{\prime}(O_{\id^{\frac{p+1}{2}}})=\infty_{\frac{p+1}{2}}.\qed

Theorem 4.5.23
.

The locally ringed space
\Xi/\mathcal{R}
is a scheme.

Proof.

We know that
\Xi^{\prime}_{\gamma}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{\gamma}
is a scheme for each
\gamma\in\widehat{\Omega}/\!\!\sim\!
by Thm.
4.5.12
and Thm.
4.5.17
. Hence
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a scheme by Cor.
4.5.9
. By Prop.
4.5.4
, it remains to show that the locally ringed space
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is a scheme. Now,
\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cong\coprod\limits_{r=1}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}Z_{r}
and we define

X_{1}:=\coprod\limits_{r\hskip 2.84544pt\equiv\hskip 2.84544pt1\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{\tiny or}\hskip 2.84544pt2\!\!\!\mod 4}Z_{r}\quad\mbox{and}\quad X_{2}:=\coprod\limits_{r\hskip 2.84544pt\equiv\hskip 2.84544pt3\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{\tiny or}\hskip 2.84544pt0\!\!\!\mod 4}Z_{r}

so that
X_{1}\coprod X_{2}=\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. We also define maps
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}:\Xi_{\sing}\to\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
as follows:

\theta_{1}(s_{r})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}O_{r}&\mbox{ if }&r\equiv 1\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{or}\hskip 2.84544pt2\mod 4\\
\infty_{r+2}&\mbox{ if }&r\equiv 3\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{or}\hskip 2.84544pt0\mod 4\end{array}\right.\quad\mbox{and}\quad\theta_{2}(s_{r})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\infty_{r+2}&\mbox{ if }&r\equiv 1\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{or}\hskip 2.84544pt2\mod 4\\
O_{r}&\mbox{ if }&r\equiv 3\hskip 2.84544pt\mbox{or}\hskip 2.84544pt0\mod 4\end{array}\right.

so that
\theta_{1}(\Xi_{\sing})\subseteq X_{1}
and
\theta_{2}(\Xi_{\sing})\subseteq X_{2}
. Using Lemma
4.5.22
, for all
s_{r}\in\Xi_{\sing}
we have

\left(\theta_{1}(s_{r}),\theta_{2}(s_{r})\right)=\left(q^{\prime}a(s_{r}),q^{\prime}b(s_{r})\right)\quad\mbox{or}\quad\left(q^{\prime}b(s_{r}),q^{\prime}a(s_{r})\right).

Let
s:\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
be a coequaliser of
\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}a}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}b}
\textstyle{X_{1}\coprod X_{2}}
; it follows that

\textstyle{\Xi_{\sing}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{2}}
\textstyle{X_{1}\coprod X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}}

is a coequaliser diagram in
\LRS
. Hence
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is isomorphic to the gluing of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
along
\theta_{1}:\Xi_{\sing}\to X_{1}
and
\theta_{2}:\Xi_{\sing}\to X_{2}
. Since
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
are both closed embeddings, this gluing is a scheme by Prop. 1.1.1
[
Ana
]
.∎

Using Lemma
4.5.22
, we can also deduce the following

Corollary 4.5.24
.

Write
P_{r}:=s(Z_{r})\subset(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
for
r=1,\cdots,\frac{p+1}{2}
.

a)

Suppose that
p\equiv 1\mod 4
, so that
\frac{p+1}{2}
is odd and
\frac{p-1}{2}
is even. Then the connected component of
P_{1}
in
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is given by

P_{1}\cup P_{3}\cup P_{5}\cup\cdots\cup P_{\frac{p+1}{2}}

and the connected component of
P_{2}
in
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is given by

P_{2}\cup P_{4}\cup P_{6}\cup\cdots\cup P_{\frac{p-1}{2}}.

b)

Suppose that
p\equiv 3\mod 4
, so that
\frac{p-1}{2}
is odd and
\frac{p+1}{2}
is even. Then the connected component of
P_{1}
in
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is given by

P_{1}\cup P_{3}\cup P_{5}\cup\cdots\cup P_{\frac{p-1}{2}}

and the connected component of
P_{2}
in
(\Xi^{\prime}/\mathcal{R}^{\prime})/\Xi_{\sing}
is given by

P_{2}\cup P_{4}\cup P_{6}\cup\cdots\cup P_{\frac{p+1}{2}}.

Appendix

A.1.
Some categorical results about colimits and coequalisers

We omit the proof of the following standard result.

Lemma A.1.1
.

Suppose that
q:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
is a coequaliser of the arrows
f,g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
in a category
\mathcal{C}
. Then
q
is an epimorphism.

Lemma A.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category, containing the following diagram:

100.43074pt\raise-94.14977pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 172.69447pt\raise-88.96228pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

Suppose that in this diagram, we have

a)

q
is a coequaliser of
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}
,

b)

q^{\prime}
is a coequaliser of
\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
,

c)

\theta
is a coequaliser of
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}
, and

d)

s
is a coequaliser of
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}a}
\scriptstyle{q^{\prime}b}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}
.

Suppose further that there exists a morphism
\theta^{\prime}:\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{R}
such that:

e)

\theta^{\prime}
is an epimorphism, and

f)

\theta f^{\prime}=f\theta^{\prime}
and
\theta g^{\prime}=g\theta^{\prime}
.

Then there exists an isomorphism
\varphi:\mathcal{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{Z}
such that
sq^{\prime}=\varphi q\theta
.

Proof.

By d), we have
sq^{\prime}a=sq^{\prime}b
. Hence by c), there is a unique morphism
\alpha:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z}
such that
\boxed{sq^{\prime}=\alpha\theta}
. Using this, we have
\alpha f\theta^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f)}}{{=}}\alpha\theta f^{\prime}=sq^{\prime}f^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b)}}{{=}}sq^{\prime}g^{\prime}=\alpha\theta g^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f)}}{{=}}\alpha g\theta^{\prime}.
Then
\alpha f=\alpha g
by e), so by a), there is a unique morphism
\varphi:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}
such that
\boxed{\alpha=\varphi q}
.

Next, we have
q\theta f^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f)}}{{=}}qf\theta^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a)}}{{=}}qg\theta^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f)}}{{=}}q\theta g^{\prime}
, so by b), there is a unique morphism
\tau:\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
\boxed{q\theta=\tau q^{\prime}}
. Then
\tau q^{\prime}a=q\theta a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c)}}{{=}}q\theta b=\tau q^{\prime}b
, so by d), there is a unique morphism
\psi:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that
\boxed{\psi s=\tau}
. We will show that
\varphi
and
\psi
are mutually inverse.

Firstly,
\varphi\psi sq^{\prime}=\varphi\tau q^{\prime}=\varphi q\theta=\alpha\theta=sq^{\prime}
. But
q^{\prime}
and
s
are coequalisers by b) and d), hence they are epimorphisms by Lemma
A.1.1
. Therefore
\varphi\psi=1_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Secondly,
\psi\varphi q\theta=\psi\alpha\theta=\psi sq^{\prime}=\tau q^{\prime}=q\theta
. Since
\theta
and
q
are coequalisers by c) and a), they are epimorphisms by Lemma
A.1.1
. Hence
\psi\varphi=1_{\mathcal{Y}}
. Finally,
\varphi q\theta=\alpha\theta=sq^{\prime}
.∎

We omit the proof of the following standard result.

Lemma A.1.3
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be a set and let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with coproducts. Suppose that

\left\{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 8.3452pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.3452pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 14.01953pt\raise 7.59029pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{a_{i}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.74391pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.74391pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern is a family of coequaliser diagrams in

. Then

\scriptstyle{\coprod\limits_{i\in I}b_{i}}
\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{i\in I}X_{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\coprod\limits_{i\in I}q_{i}}
\textstyle{\coprod\limits_{i\in I}Y_{i}}

is also a coequaliser diagram in
\mathcal{C}
.

A.2.
An alternative stability proof

By Prop.
2.2.3
,
\Mod_{k}(G)
has Krull dimension
1
, so the only non-trivial term in the Krull-dimension filtration of
\Mod_{k}(G)
is
\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
. Its stability then follows from our general result, Prop.
3.7.8
. Here we give an alternative, more direct, argument for the stability of
\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
. Our argument is inspired by Paškūnas’ proof of the corresponding result for the group
GL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and representations with a fixed central character (
[
Pas
]
Prop. 5.16).

An
H
-module is called
locally finite
if each of its elements is contained in a submodule of finite length. By Remark
2.2.2
the locally finite
H
-modules form the localising subcategory
\Mod(\mathcal{H})_{0}
of objects of Krull dimension
0
.

Lemma A.2.1
.

\Mod(H)_{0}
is stable.

Proof.

By
[
MCR
]
Cor. 13.1.13(iii) and Thm. 13.10.3(i), any simple module over the affine PI
k
-algebra
H
is finite dimensional over
k
. Hence a locally finite module is the same as a module in which every element is contained in a finite dimensional submodule. It easily follows that
\Mod(H)_{0}
satisfies the criterion
[
Gab
]
Prop. V.6.12.
∎

Correspondingly, a
G
-representation in
\Mod_{k}(G)
is locally finite if each of its elements is contained in a subrepresentation of finite length.

Lemma A.2.2
.

If the representation
V
in
\Mod_{k}(SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))
is admissible then also its injective hull in
\Mod_{k}(SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))
is admissible.

Proof.

Put
K:=SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
. It is well know that a representation
V
in
\Mod_{k}(K)
is admissible if and only if its Pontrjagin dual
V^{\vee}
is finitely generated as a module over the completed group ring
k[[K]]
. The inclusion
V\hookrightarrow E(V)
into an injective hull dualizes to a projective cover
E(V)^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow V^{\vee}
. Since the ring
k[[H]]
is noetherian (cf. the explanations in the proof of
[
Sch
]
Prop. 5) this cover
E(V)^{\vee}
must be finitely generated as a
k[[H]]
-module. It follows that
E(V)
is admissible.
∎

Lemma A.2.3
.

For a representation
V
in
\Mod_{k}(G)
we have:

a)

V
is of finite length if and only if
V
is finitely generated and admissible.

b)

V
is locally finite if and only if it is locally admissible (in the sense of
[
EP
]
).

Proof.

a) Suppose that
V
is of finite length. Then it obviously is finitely generated. In order to see that
V
is admissible we may assume that it is irreducible. But then, by Thm.
2.1.1
the
H
-module
V^{I}
is finite dimensional, which means that
V
is admissible. Now assume, vice versa, that
V
is finitely generated and admissible. Using a filtration of
V
as in Lemma
2.1.3
and the fact that admissibility is preserved by passing to subquotients (
[
Eme
]
Prop. 2.2.13) we may assume that
V
lies in
\Mod^{I}(G)
. Since
V^{I}
is finite dimensional the equivalence in Thm.
2.1.1
tells us that
V
is of finite length.

b) If
V
is locally finite then, by a), it is locally admissible. Suppose therefore that
V
is locally admissible. But then it is the union of admissible and finitely generated subrepresentations. Again by a) it follows that
V
is the union of subrepresentations of finite length.
∎

Lemma A.2.4
.

If
V
is an injective object in
\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
then
V
is also an injective object in
\Mod_{k}(SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))
.

Proof.

The proof is almost literally the same as for
[
EP
]
Cor. 3.8. For the convenience of the reader we note:

–

Use Lemma
A.2.3
.b to replace the assumption that
V
is locally finite by the assumption that
V
is locally admissible, which loc. cit. uses.

–

The group
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is the amalgam of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
and
(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
p&0\end{smallmatrix})SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})(\begin{smallmatrix}0&p^{-1}\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix})
along the Iwahori subgroup (cf.
[
Ser
]
II.4.1 Cor. 1).

We also point out that in loc. cit. a version of Lemma
A.2.2
is used without mentioning it.
∎

Proposition A.2.5
.

\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
is stable.

Proof.

By Gabriel’s criterion (cf.
[
Ste
]
Prop. VI.7.1) we have to check the following: Let
V_{0}\subseteq V
be any essential extension in
\Mod_{k}(G)
(in particular
V_{0}\neq\{0\}
) such that
V_{0}
is locally finite. We then have to show that
V
is locally finite as well. First of all, by replacing
V
by an injective hull, we may assume that
V
is an injective object. Secondly since
\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
is localising, by possibly enlarging
V_{0}
, we may assume that
V_{0}
is the maximal locally finite subrepresentation of
V
. It is then straightforward to see that
V_{0}
is an injective object in
\Mod_{k}(G)_{0}
. Hence using Frobenius reciprocity we deduce from Lemma
A.2.4
that

(41)

\Ext_{\Mod_{k}(G)}^{1}(\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma),V_{0})=\Ext_{\Mod_{k}(K)}^{1}(\sigma,V_{0})=0

for any smooth representation
\sigma
of the subgroup
K:=SL_{2}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Reasoning by contradiction we assume that
V_{0}\neq V
. It suffices to construct a nonzero locally finite subrepresentation of
V/V_{0}
. For this we pick an irreducible
K
-subrepresentation
\sigma
in the
K
-socle of
V/V_{0}
. We also pick a vector
v\in(V/V_{0})^{I}
which generates
\sigma
as a
K
-representation. We let
\bar{X}\subseteq V/V_{0}
denote the
G
-subrepresentation generated by
v
and by
X
its preimage in
V
. We obtain:

a)

The short exact sequence
0\rightarrow V_{0}\rightarrow X\rightarrow\bar{X}\rightarrow 0
in
\Mod_{k}(G)
does not split since
V_{0}\hookrightarrow X
is an essential extension.

b)

By the Frobenius reciprocity equality
\Hom_{k[K]}(\sigma,\bar{X})=\Hom_{k[G]}(\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma),\bar{X})
the inclusion
\sigma\subseteq\bar{X}
corresponds to a
G
-homomorphism
\bar{\psi}:\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)\twoheadrightarrow\bar{X}
, which is surjective since
v
lies in its image.

Applying (
41
) to a) shows that the map
\Hom_{k[G]}(\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma),X)\twoheadrightarrow\Hom_{k[G]}(\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma),\bar{X})
is surjective, so that
\bar{\psi}
has a preimage
\psi:\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)\rightarrow X
. If
\bar{\psi}
would also be injective then
\psi\circ\bar{\psi}^{-1}:\bar{X}\rightarrow X
would split the sequence in a). It follows that
\bar{\psi}
is a proper quotient map. Since
\bar{\psi}
is a map in
\Mod_{k}^{I}(G)
the equivalence of categories in Thm.
2.1.1
implies that the sequence

\bar{\psi}^{I}:\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)^{I}\twoheadrightarrow\bar{X}^{I}
is a proper quotient map of
H
-modules. We claim that any proper
H
-quotient of
\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)^{I}
has finite
k
-dimension. Again Thm.
2.1.1
then implies that
\bar{X}
has finite length, which would contradict the maximality of
V_{0}
.

In order to determine the
H
-module structure of
\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)^{I}
we introduce the finite dimensional subalgebra
H_{0}:=\End_{k[K]}(k[K/I])^{\op}
of
H
. It is well known that the
H_{0}
-module of invariants
\sigma^{I}
is one dimensional and therefore provides a character
\chi:H_{0}\rightarrow k
. By
[
Oll
]
Lemma 3.6 there is an isomorphism of
H
-modules
\ind_{K}^{G}(\sigma)^{I}\cong H\otimes_{H_{0}}\chi
.
5
5

5

In
[
Oll
]
the field
k
is assumed to be an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. But in our situation all irreducible mod
p
representations of
SL_{2}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
are defined over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, which makes this assumption unnecessary.

On the other hand recall the notations introduced in
\lx@sectionsign\ref{sec: propIHA}
. Note
\tau_{0}
and
e_{1}
lie in
H_{0}
and that
Z(H)
contains the polynomial ring
k[\zeta]
by Lemma
4.1.2
. As a consequence of
[
OS18
]
Cor. 3.4 we have the isomorphism of
k[\zeta]
-modules

k[\zeta]\oplus k[\zeta]\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}H\otimes_{H_{0}}\chi

which sends
(1,0)
to
1\otimes 1
and
(0,1)
to
\tau_{1}\otimes 1
.

Let
Q:=k(\zeta)
be the field of fractions of
k[\zeta]
and let
V:=Q\otimes_{k[\zeta]}(H\otimes_{H_{0}}\chi)
.
Then
V
is a 2-dimensional vector space over
Q
with basis
{v,w}
where
v:=1\otimes 1\otimes 1
and
w:=1\otimes\tau_{1}\otimes 1
. Suppose now that we have a nonzero
H
-submodule of infinite codimension in
H\otimes_{H_{0}}\chi
. It gives rise to a line in the vector space
V
which is respected by the
Q
-linear action of
\tau_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
. Let
a:=\chi(e_{1})
and
b:=\chi(\tau_{0})
. So,
a
is
0
or
1
and
b
is some element of
k
. We calculate that the matrix
A
of the action of
\tau_{0}
with respect to the basis
{v,w}
is

(\begin{smallmatrix}b&\zeta-a(b+1)\\
0&-(a+b)\end{smallmatrix})

and the matrix
B
of the action of
\tau_{1}
with respect to the same basis is

(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
1&-a\end{smallmatrix})
.
The minimum polynomial of
B
is visibly
X^{2}+aX
. If
a=0
then
B
is nilpotent and the only
\tau_{1}
-stable line in
V
is spanned by
w
. If
a=1
then
B
is diagonalizable, and there is an additional
\tau_{1}
-stable line spanned by
v+w
. So we just need to check that these two lines are not
\tau_{0}
-stable. Passing to the matrices we need to check that the column vectors
(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix})
and
(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
1\end{smallmatrix})
are not
A
-eigenvectors:

–

A(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix})=(\begin{smallmatrix}\zeta-a(b+1)\\
-(a+b)\end{smallmatrix})
is not in
Q(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix})
since
\zeta-a(b+1)
is not zero in
Q
;

–

A(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
1\end{smallmatrix})=(\begin{smallmatrix}\zeta-a\\
-(a+b)\end{smallmatrix})=c(\begin{smallmatrix}a\\
1\end{smallmatrix})
for some
c\in Q
implies
c=-(a+b)\in k
, but also
\zeta-a=ca\in k
, which is impossible. ∎
